# DEDICATION.

V Otive to that Right Honourable Pair, The Noble Lord and Lady Delaware; Whose Acts of Charity were often shown, To poor Corinna under Fortune's Frown; And to Posterity shall here be spread; Their last, kind, Christian-Office to the Dead.

## vi The DEDICATION.

Instead of Church-Yard Notice be it known, Tho' our Two Lovers have no Marble Stone, These Leaves their mould'ring Ashes shall out-live, And Fame Immortal to Each Other give.

THE

# PREFACE.

THE Public having been pleased to give a very Candid Reception to PYLADES and CORINNA; I here present them with the Remainder of their Correspondence, and have according to my Promise subjoined true Copies of their Last Wills and Testaments.

This Volume, as well as the former, proves Both Parties to have been HONOURABLE LOVERS, and all the Efforts that were ever infinuated or attempted by the Relations of A 4 PYLA-

## viii The PREFACE.

PYLADES, after his Decease, to prove them guilty of a Criminal Correspondence, have received a full Defeat.

I shall not urge any thing farther upon this Head, because I find the Opponents of CORINNA are no more; and I always defend that truly, decent, and just Maxim—De Mortuis nil, nisi Bonum.

In Three Letters I received from a Clergyman at Gloucester $^{*}$ , he writes thus, viz.

## SIR,

" YOU are pleased to enquire what Relations of Pylades are living, his

" Brother (who is dead) has left one Son

" (a Child) and next to him his nearest

" Relation and Heir is, Sir, your humble

" Servant, SAMUEL GWINNETT.

"My Coufin Richard Gwinnett (i.e. "PYLADES) was justly allowed by all, who had the Happiness of knowing him, to

*Bearing Date July 28th, August 20th, and September 4th, 1731.

The PREFACE.

ix

"to be a Person of an uncommon Genius, and possessed of every Quality which can render one Man agreeable to another; and his Reputation as a fine Gentleman, and an accomplished Scholar, I shall always think myself obliged to vindicate.

This Gentleman likewise expresses his Fears, " Lest any Proceedings should lessen that high Esteem which the Writings and Conversation of PYLADES have so justly procured Him from all who have any Taste of Politeness.

I have as strict a Regard for the Memory of PYLADES, as this Gentleman can have; and hereby assure Him that the following Papers, will equally Delight and Instruct every Reader, who can distinguish Politeness from Pedantry, and a Sincere Affection from an Artificial Gallantry.

The other Parts of CORINNA's Correspondence will I doubt not prove very acceptable to the Public; the Names of the Persons being a sufficient Recommendation of their Writings.

## The PREFACE.

I have still in my Custody many curious Miscellanies written by PYLADES, both in Prose and Verse, which are intended to be published with a New Edition of CORINNA's Poems, now preparing for the Press.

New-Year's Day, 1732.

VALE.

# LETTERS

BETWEEN

PYLADES and CORINNA.

V O L U M E II.

LETTER I.

REMARKS on several Parts of England.

S'te Furton, 11 June 1709.

NOW I am safely arrived at Home, perhaps you may expect, my dear CORINNA, that I should mount Pegafus, and give you an Account of my JOURNEY in Heroicks, especially since I was advanced to ride in a fort of Triumphal-Car with a Baronet and Senator* but really the Tuneful-Tribe have quite forsaken me, and therefore I hope you will be contented to know in plain Profe, that we lay the first Night at Henley,† where we met with nothing remarkable.

The next Day we dined at Newington, $\parallel$ from whence I fend you a Poetical Present, which,

* Sir JOHN GUISE. † Upon Thomas. || In Oxfordshire.

VOL. II.

B

L

## 2. LETTERS between PYLADES

I doubt not, will prove acceptable. And, as you know, I am always fond of reading the Memoirs of the Dead upon their Monuments; in the Church here, on a Black-Marble Gravestone, I met with the under-written Epitaph, composed by Mr. WALLER, 1686, which I am certain is not inserted in any Edition of his Works. And with this Mite, till a better Offering presents worthy of being devoted to your Shrine, I remain in haste, tho' with the greatest Since- rity, (I wish I could say solely) your

PYLADES.

## HENRY DUNCH, Esq;

HERE lies the Prop and Glory of his Race; Who that no Time his Mem'ry may deface,

His grateful Wife, under this speaking Stone,

His Ashes hid to make his Merit known.

Sprung from an opulent and worthy Line,

Whose well-us'd Fortune made their Virtues shine;

A rich Example his fair Life did give,

How others should with their Relations live,

A pious Son, a Husband, and a Friend,

To Neighbours too, his Bounty did extend,

So

and CORINNA.

3

So far, that they lamented when he dy'd, As if all to him had been near ally'd.

His curious Youth would Men and Manners know,

Which made him to the Southern Nations go.

Nearer the Sun, tho' they more civil seem,

Revenge and Luxury have their Efsteem ;

Which well observing, he return'd with more

Value for England, than he had before.

Her true Religion, and her Statutes too,

He practised not less, than seek'd to know;

And the whole Country griev'd for their ill Fate,

To lose so good, so just a Magistrate.

To fhed a Tear may Readers be inclin'd,

And pray for ONE, he only left behind,

Till SHE, who does inherit His Estate,

May Virtue love like HIM, and Vices hate.

## EDMOND WALLER. *

* This Epitaph escaped Mr. Fenton's Notice, and is not in the Edition he published of Mr. Waller's Works, 1730.

B 2

LET

LETTERS between PYLADES

## LETTER II.

THE Third Day, we reached to Dinner at Oxan, where we had the Conversation of our young Poet,* who wrote the Poem to the Duke of Marlborough; but I think the Muse has forsaken him too: However he was so complaisant as to accompany us to Woodstock, and tarried there with us all Night.

In the Morning we viewed Blenheim Rock, for so it may more properly be called than CASTLE, it is such an heavy Gothic Pile. The Gardens are very delightful, and gave us great Satisfaction, but the House put Sir John perfectly into the Spleen. The Dutchess was there, but we did not see Her. In our Way from Woodstock, we called to see Cornbury House (now Lord Rochester's) where we saw the old Lord Clarcendon's fine Collection of printed Books, and such variety of Pictures, in three-quarter Draughts, half, and whole Lengths, as I never saw in one House before, but not one History-Piece, nor Landskip. There are a great many Originals of Vandyke of the ROYAL FAMILY, Bishops, Judges, and learned Men in abundance, by eminent Hands; among the rest, there is Cowley and Waller. That of Cowley is very unlike the Cut in the Front of his Book; and Waller's is such a grave, dull, demure Face, (so much like that of your humble Servant) that I should ne-

* Mr. William Harrison of New Coll.Ovon, Author of a fine Poem intitled Woodlock-Park.

ver

and CORINNA.

5

ver have imagined he could have been the Author of such polite, such courtly Thoughts.

I returned to Oxford, where I spent three most agreeable Days at Christ-Church, and then set out for Wales. At the good Town of Monmouth I found all in good Health.* This Journey afforded nothing remarkable, save an old Man whose Name is Bright, he lives at Long-hope, and is above 120 Years old. I made him a Visit, as most Gentlemen who travel that way do. He is plump and ruddy, feeds heartily, fleeps well, and has all his Senses in tolerable Perfection, except his Hearing. He was never let Blood, took Physic, nor had Fever, Ague, Small-Pox, or any Distemper to lie by. His long Life has been spent in hard Labour, and a rustic Way of living: He had eight Children by his first Wife, and three by the second, who is now his Nurse, and has been married to him forty three Years. His great Age makes him much talked of, and the Judges in their Circuit made him a Visit of an Hour; he says he can well remember Queen Elizabeth, and was a sturdy Lad able to go two Miles to School in her Time, but the Spanish Invasion is beyond his Memory; I could not but look on this Man as a venerable Monument of Antiquity, rather to be admired than envied.

Yours, &c.

PYLADES.

- Here Pylades had a Sister married. B 3

P. S.

## 6 LETTERS between PYLADES

P. S. I had almost forgot to acquaint you, the Oxford News is, that they have had Two Days this Week Horse-Racing there, in Contempt of Woodstock-Park which was usually the Place for that Diversion. They had likewise Yesterday such a Prize, I will not say fought, but earnestly contended for with vast Attendance both of Auditors and Spectators, as neither Greece nor Rome, among all the Games of the Amphitheatre and Circus; neither Bartholomew nor May-Fair among all their Raree-shews could ever boast of the like; and that was, WHISTLING for a Cart-Whip. There were several Competitors, who came off with great Applause, and the Judge of the Performance was JOHN à STUNSFIELD, who is the most accurate and famous Man in England at the Tabor and Pipe. He was originally a Shepherd's Boy, and at this Day can neither write nor read, and yet will play a Sonato at twice hearing; and, by the Strength of Memory alone, can play almost all the Tunes in Use. He has picked up near 60 l. per Ann. by Piping. The History of his Life is very remarkable but too long to insert. He lives at Stunsfield* near Woodstock.

* This is the Place where the famous Roman-Pavement was discovered, 1712.

OI:

and CORINNA.

7

On Bleinheim-CASTLE.

An Imitation of MARTIAL.

Atria longe patent ; sed nec cænantibus usquam,

Nec somno locus est ; quàm bene non habites?

MARTIAL.

There lies the Bridge, and here's the Clock,

Observe the Lyon and the Cock,

The fpacious Court, the Colonade,

And mark how wide the Hall is made?

The Chimnies are so well design'd,

They never smoke in any Wind.

This Gallery's contriv'd for Walking,

The Windows to Retire and Talk in;

The Council-Chamber for Debate,

And, All the Reft are Rooms of State.

Thanks,

LETTERS between PYLADES

Thanks, SIR, cry'd I,'tis very fine,

But where d'ye Sleep, or where d'ye Dine?

I find by all you have been telling,

That 'tis a House, but not a DWELLING.

## LETTER III.

June 14 1709.

I was constrained to break off very abruptly in my last, among the Pictures at Cornbury-House. To refume therefore the Relation which I left unfinished you must know, my Dear Corinna, that among a great many others of the Royal Family, there was one Picture of the QUEEN* drawn by LILLY in Beaufort-Buildings. (where I went to see that fine Piece of the Virgin Mary) It much exceeds CLOSTERMAN's at Guild-Hall, and is, I think, the best Likeness that ever I saw of her Majesty.

Sir John was so pleased with the Figure, and the Manner of Painting, that he refolves to make LILLY a Visit when he comes next to Town.

From hence we passed over a very pleasant Country, of easy Hills, and spacious Downs, till we came to Rendcombe, where I tarried with Sir John till the Monday following; and have since been very dull at home, unless it were one Day when I went to Glofter with my Brother CATCHMAID, who called at our House in his return from Badminton. The old Dutchefs + it seems is going

* ANNE. † Of Bcufrt.

from

and CORINNA.

9

from thence to live at Chelsea, there having been some Misunderstanding between her and her Grandson.

This is written on Whit-Tuesday, but when it will come to your Hands I cannot fay; tho' I ear-efeltly wish it may meet with a speedy Arrival, together with the Heart of

Your PYLADES.

# LETTER IV.

## MADAM,

Am again got to Oxford, the most pleasant and desirable Place, that in all my little Travels I was ever acquainted with; methinks, I want nothing to compleat my Happiness, but your more delightful, more desirable, Company. How could I then, with inexpressible Satisfaction, recollect those many entertaining Hours, which I have formerly passed within this happy Seat of the Muses! how could I contract the Pleasures of many Years into one transporting Day, and not only renew, but redouble them all! But alas! when I consider the Occasion that brought me hither, and perceive it also to be the same that withholds me from seeing you, this damps all my Joy, and gives me a lively Instance, that if the Gods could not mix Pleasure and Pain in one Cup, yet they have so closely connected them, that they often spring from the same Source, and grow like bitter and sweet Fruit on the same Tree. O fortu-

10

LETTERS between PYLADES

fortunate Husbandmen! (said Virgil, with a great Emphaiss) did they but know their own Felicity. Much more properly, I think, might the Exclamation be applied to Academians, were they but sensible of their Happiness, and knew how rightly to improve it. For being quietly secluded from the Noise and Interruption of Business, and advanced above the poor groveling Concerns of this Life, they are surrounded with glorious Helps and Opportunities of launching out into the boundless Ocean of Arts and Sciences, and exploring the unfathomable Depths of human and divine Knowledge, which is not only the greatest of temporal Delights, but will perhaps be the everlasting Entertainment of immortal and illuminated Spirits. Here opens a large Scene before me, and upon this engaging Subject, methinks, I could never be weary of writing, as I never could of talking to you.

I thank you for that Sight of Part of Mr. Pope's Poem (To a Lady Singing to her Lute) and promise my self Satisfaction from the whole. You know Mr. Waller has a celebrated Copy on the same Subject; which of the Two is best, you will easily judge on comparing both together. But in my Opinion, one would not hastily chuse a Theme which another had written on before with admired Success, unless one were very well assured of it. There seems to be a kind of implicit Competition in such Attempts, which fall short of the acquiring Praise, when they do no more than equal the Pattern proposed.

As I told you above, that I have been a Rambler

---

and CORINNA.

ler all this Week, so you are not to take this for a Letter, but a Medley, as I met with Occurrences on my Road; among which give me Leave to relate a merry Passage which happened lately. A party of Lord Oxford's Troop being quartered here, and a certain Serjeant at Law of our Acquaintance,* lodging in this City on his Way to London, the Drummers made him the Compliment of beating the Revel under his Window, early in the Morning; which ended, the senior Drum made his elegant Speech: We Her Majesty's Drummers in the Right Honourable, &c. humbly crave Leave to entertain Her Majesty's Serjeant, learned in the Law, with a Point of War; and so fell a drumming most furiously. When, the grave Serjeant, stepping out into the Balcony, beckoned for Silence, which having obtained, made this Reply, I Her Majesty's Serjeant at Law, do humbly crave Leave to entertain Her Majesty's Drummers with a Point of Law; and so pulling out a Breviate, went to opening a Law Cafe. Upon which they all sneaked off without any Rejoinder, as must till the next Opportunity.

## LETTER V.

BY Reason of the wet Weather I could not get to this Place till Yesterday, and then I had as pleasant a Passage as could be desired. The Enter-

- Tkemas Strode, Efq;

II

Your unalterable

PYLADES.

## 12 LETTERS between PYLADES

Entertainment I find here, is good Air, good brown Bread, good small Beer, good Beef and Mutton, and what's best of all, a good Stomach. If among all these Enjoyments you should think I want good Company to compleat them, I will tell you how I am supplied, as to that respect, not by saying, as my Lady Elizabeth does, *Never left alone, than when alone:* But I met with an old Acquaintance of mine, who came up in the Boat with me, and whose Conversation is extreamly delightful. He is a Man of great Learning, Civility, good Humour, Sobriety, and all genteel Accomplishments. He has been a great Traveller, and entertains one very agreeably, as well as instructively, with an Account of the foreign Countries he has seen; after another kind of Rate, than the Generality of Ramblers commonly do. He is a great Master of Language, and speaks with such Ease and such Eloquence, that if one did not hear his Words flow from him like Honey and Oil, one should be apt to think all he said was studied and elaborate. He is a very understanding, and what is more excellent, an honest Statesman too: Has been employed in Affairs of the greatest Moment, conversed with Princes of the first Rank, and was some Years ago an intimate Acquaintance of our present King, whom with the highest Justice he extols as one of the greatest and best of Men. In short, I think him ignorant of nothing that is suitable to

* Lady Elizabeth Howard, whom Mr. Dryden married.

§ K. WILLIAM III.

13

his Character, and fit for him to know; and he has the trueft and most rational Notions of Happiness and Milery, as far as this Life is concerned, of any Man I have had the Honour to convert with, at least such as are most adapted to my Inclination and Understanding. This ingenious beloved Companion of mine, has been so kind as to give me his Company all the while I stay in the Country, and is contented to fare as I do with the utmost Civility and Condescension. Whether you remember him or not I cannot tell, but he has been at your House, and you were so pleased with what he said, that you desired a Repetition of it, and intimated your Inclination to hear his harmonious Voice again in some short Time. This Associate of mine, when he was alive, (for you must know he has been dead for some Years) dwelt within Sight of Troynovant, the ancient Name of London, in a very opulent Fortune, and yet (to his eternal Commendation be it spoken) in seven Years Time never once went to it. Since his Death he has been constrained to appear there, but was glad of an Opportunity to get out of it, especially since it was to go within half a Mile of his ancient Habitation in the Country.* There is one Thing seems a little odd in him, since his Appearance in the World again, to disguise himself that he may not be stared at, he goes all

* Moor-Park, a pleasant Retirement, near Earnham in Surrey, where Sir Waltham Lond. Died Anne 1700.

Purfuant to the Direction in his Will, his Heart was put into a Silver Box and buried under the Sun-Dial in his Garden, opposite to the Window from whence he used to contemplate and admire the Works of Nature. A learned Divine, upon this Act of Sir William's, in a Letter to a Friend thus expostulates. " As to that particular of laying his Heart in his Gar- den,

## 14 LETTERS between PYLADES

in Leather, but of such a decent Colour, and so exactly fitted to his Body, that not one in a hundred takes Notice of it. But it would be endlessly to tell you half what he said or did; one Thing I particularly remember, as we were failing in the Boat together, he told me, that the Three Things he would above all wish for, were Health, Peace and fair Weather, and that a clear Day was a son of sensual Pleasure, which I took the more Notice of, because I found it experimentally true: For my Heart was sensibly cherished with an unaccountable kind of Joy, while the Sunshone with such resplendent Brightness through a serene Air, that if he had put on the same sweet Aspect in his Amorous Purfuit, not coy Daphne herself would have fled from him. But then he was eager, violent, and scorching; and I fancy the delicate Nymph ran away for fear of being tanned, and losing her fine Complexion, and I think I am running after. But I must desist and return to my Companion, whom I shall treat like a Gentleman while I am here, and bring him back with me again; for I am sure he is so civil, that he will not leave me against my Will, and at my Return, to do him the greatest Honour I can, I will lodge him in, his Name'-sake Bosom, the TEMPLE. But to leave Allegory and

Fiction, there is nothing in Reality invites me to London again, except yourself: And therefore in hopes of finding you enjoy that Health there, which I am forced to fetch out of the Country, shall next Week with the greater Satisfaction return

My Dear CORINNA,

HERE is a great deal of Company. Being but a fresh Comer I can yet send you no News from this Place; except it be that Yesterday Mr. Wycherley dined with Sir John, whose conversation, I find like his Poetry, is very much recayed; unless plain, fulsom Obscenity (not to be borne with in a Young Man, but unpardonable in an Old one) may pass for Wit and good Breeding. I find the Common Diversions of this Place, which are Gaming and Drinking, will give me no Entertainment; and truly I am so little delighted with what I have seen already, that I wish Sir John may keep to his Intention of going away next Week.

16 LETTERS between PYLADES

## BATH Lampoon. 1709.

IN vain! who seeks to mend a vicious Age, Indulges Spleen, and lets the Satire rage. What Knave or Hypocrite e'er left his Trade, Or Whore grew chaste, Or Fool was wiser made By Satire? Yet the sweet Catullus drew Mamurra to the Life, and all his shameless Crew. Then since the Poet's kind Intentions fail, At least we'll lafh the senseless Croud, and rail.

Fair Countefs, my first Verse is giv'n to You For Chastity and Wit alike's your Due, Who, after many various Scenes of Love, Would on these Sulph'rous Banks a New or prove, And in the well-tun'd Eunuch once unite Sweet Mufic, and your other dear Delight. Curse Eunuch, curse, thy unpropitious Skies, That what thy Song has gain'd, thy Voice denies.

And is there then, O Sex, no little Space

Of Earth, where Modesty has yet a Place?

Stung with Mad-Love, restlefs Xantippe raves,

Defpifes Fame, and flighted Cenfure braves.

Aloud hoarse Codrus' Wit, and Shape commends, For him hates Husband, Children, House, and Friends,

Whilst he not made soft Passion to return,

Swears when she Sighs, and laughs to hear her Mourn:

Net proffers Luft the Fair One durft not use,

Religion and the Pox those Joys refuse :

But Cornifk, who has oft such Hazards run,

Accepts the Present, fearless what's to come.

Hot Wishes blossom, Spight of faded Charms,

And draw the lufty Stallion to her Arms.

Surely these Springs, for Barrennefs so good New warm the Veins, and falt the Itching Blood. VOL. II. C From

From out each steaming Bath the Fair arise, Luft glowing in their Cheeks, and sparkling in their Eyes.

Therefore let ev'ry He, who wants an Heir,

Send his chaste Spouse to try her Fortune there:

Nor think the pow'rful Remedy can fail,

For She, so heated, ne'er will want the Male:

And tho' they pregnant grow with foreign Seed,

'Tis more than Odds that it may mend the Breed.

Beaus, Soldiers, Gamefsters, a promiscuous Crew,

With diff'rent Arts their various Ends purfue:

Physicians, Lawyers, and the well-fed Priest,

All Sorts of Cheats that do the World infest,

And hope for Share of Cully, or of Whore.

From this Circean-Herd we'll try to chuse A few distinguish'd Fools to glad the Muse.

24 LETTERS between PYLADES

# A SECOND JOURNEY with Sir John and Lady GUISE.

LETTER VII.

Newport-Pagel, Tuesday, June 19. 17.1.

WE set out on Sunday Afternoon, according to Appointment, and reached St. Alban's that Night, where there happened nothing remarkable, but that the next Morning we made a Visit to Duke Humphrey. Sir John had seen his Grace before, as well as my self, and my Lady is not much diverted with such antique Rarities; so we left the good Duke, the Proto-Martyr, the fat Abbots, and dead Monks (even whilst alive) to their beloved Rest, and travelled very safely as far as Dunfrable, when unluckily, within a Furlong of that Town, an Axle-tree of the Coach broke short off, so that we were forced to stay there five Hours to have a new one made, and were so long hindered, that we got no farther than Newport-Pagnel that Night. I rose a little before Sir John this Morning, in order to have one Minute's Conversation with my dear Corinna, this being a Post-Town, from whence I could comply with your Desires and my own Inclinations of sending you this Account of our Progress. Our next Stage is Leicester, and then to Burton, where we hope to arrive to Morrow-Night. There have been great

and CORINNA.

25

great Thunder-showers in these Parts, and the Roads are very dirty, instead of dusty. How sudden are the Changes of Weather and Ways in this Climate? This Morning promises very fairly, and I hope we shall finish the rest of our Journey with entire Satisfaction. But the Horses are put to, and I shall lose my Breakfast if I write any more. Adieu.

# LETTER VIII.

## Burton upon Trent, June 23. 1711.

WE came to this Place on Wednesday Night, pretty much fatigued with bad Ways, and a dirty Journey, which you will say is something unusual at this Time of the Year. I have seen little yet, besides my Lady's House and Gardens, which indeed are very fine, and most exquisitely fitted up. There is one Apartment below Stairs, might very well become a Dutchess. The Hangings of one Room cost 300l. I think they are the finest Tapefry I ever saw. The Designs are the Four ELEMENTS: Represented by Ceres and Cybele. Neptune and Thetis. Juno and Iris. Vulcan and Venus; with their proper Attendants, and suitable Embellishments. The Figures were copied from Originals belonging to the King of France; and I fancy you may have seen the Prints (as well as I) from a Copper-plate. But the Excellency of the Work, and the Liveliness of the Colours

26 LETTERS between PYLADES

Colours in Tapeftry, give such a Beauty to the Whole as is not to be conceiv'd from Black and White alone. The rest of the Apartments, Withdrawing-Room, Bed-Chamber, Dressing-Room, and Closets, are all of a Piece, both for Architecture and Furniture. In our Passage from Leicester, where we dined the last Day, we went through my Lord Ferrers's Park and Court, and my Lord Chesterfield's. The latter had such a Romantic Appearance, and so pleasant a Situation, that Sir John (it being but two Miles off) intends to go to view the Inside of the House and Gardens, which they say are extreamly fine. But amidst all these Objects of Delight, I want my chief Satisfaction and think the rude Kitchen-Garden at Fulham, with my dear Corinna in it, more agreeable than all the Curiosities of Art or Nature, in any Place without her.

## LETTER IX.

## Burton upon Trent, June 30, 1711.

THIS Week, my dear CORINNA, has been spent in viewing my Lord Chesterfield's and my Lord Stanhope's Seats in Derbyshire; the first of which is a very pleasant Place, situate in the midst of a Park; and is chiefly remarkable for the fine Gardens and Water-works, which are very curious and furprizing. In one Part you walk between two Rows of Orange-Trees, with little Fountains playing betwixt every Tree. The

and CORINNA.

27

nole is very cool and shady, and would be more freeable in France or Italy, than in this Country, where the Weather has been very cold since we time, and last Night Sir John ordered a Fire to be made in the Parlour. I fancy you would be of its Mind, and take more Delight in a Fire than a Mountain. Lord Stanhope's is a little Box not worth timing. We shall tarry here about a Week anger, and in my next, I hope, I may, be able to point a certain Day for your writing to Ciren-fter, that I may receive the good News of your Health, as my most desirable Welcome into Glou-stershire, soon after my arrival there. I am in great Fears on that Account; God grant they may be vain. I am impatient till I hear from you, and am very glad our Stay here, will be so short, that I may not be long deprived of that Satisfaction.

Yours, &c.

# LETTER X.

Sburdington, July 14, 1711.

I AM at length arrived here, my dear CORINNA. We set out from Burton, last Wednesday, and say that Night at Warwick, which is a very pretty Town, and abounds with Rarities. In the Church, which is new and well-built, we saw the Monument of Guy Earl of Warwick, of Brafs gilt, with a long Inscription on a Plate of Brafs round the Edges,

28

LETTERS between PYLADES

Edges, giving an Account of his great Exploits, Family, Pedigree, &c. In the same Place lies Robert Earl of Leicester, the Favourite of Queen Elizabeth, with his Wife, whom they say he poisoned, to make Way for his Amour with the Queen. There is also a very handsome Monument of Marble, belonging to the Lord Brook's Family, to whom the Castle of Warwick belongs; where we saw Guy of Warwick's Porridge-Pot, which holds eighty Gallons. As also, his Great Sword and Armour, and his Walking-Staff about three Yards long, and the Rib of the Dun-Cow, which he flew: But what pleased me best was Phillis's Iron-Slippers. For you know all Knights-Errant have a Mistress, and it seems Guy's was a huge two-banded Damfel, cycleped Phyllis, who I suppose used to ride abroad with him in quest of Adventures, cashed up in Iron like her doughty Knight, even from Head to Toe.

The next Day we came to Rendcombe, without any remarkable Adventure on the Road; and Yesterday I reached Home, where I hope to rest a little, for truly I am pretty much fatigued with Three Days Travelling.

## LETTER XI

MADAM,

I Was much concerned for the Lois of your Letter, which never came to my Hands: 'Tho' my Excess of Friendship would not suffer me to complain, left you should think me guilty

0!

and CORINNA.

of a mercenary Selfishness, and that I did not write out of a disinterested Friendship, but only when bribed by your prevailing Epistles. And having heard you say, that true Friendship no more required Letter for Letter, than Visit for Visit, I was willing to convince you I thought so too, by giving you Two for One. But now to my Promise, and the Particulars of your former Letter: I thank you for the History of the Peripatetic * which was very diverting: But the many tragical Occurrences of the Town you mention, enhance my Affection to the Country: Every Thing is welcome to me which indulges that Passion, not that I take Delight in the Calamities of another, (there being nothing I more abhor) I pity what I cannot help, and am glad to find my self at a Distance from such Scenes of Misery. And tho' the Country is by the delicate Cockneighs called savage and rustic, yet we are not so barbarous as to destroy one another, much less ourselves. Our Cruelty goes no farther than an innocent Hare, and if a Fox pays his Life for his Offences, though the Game be Carrion, we have the Farmer's good Wishes; and a generous Hunter is sufficiently rewarded, in having done the Public some Service. Perhaps my Love for the Country may make me partial to its Pleasures, and blind to its Inconveniences: However, I must own my Fondness so far, as to acquaint you, that I write this in Paradise, in a Summer-House, which stands

* Captain Hemingway's Walk to Wimbledon.

29

in

30

LETTERS between PYLADES

in a well-stored Orchard, distant from the Dwell-ing-House: Hither I retire sometimes to enjoy my Thoughts, and want only a Partner to render it a perfect Eden. Here the Works of Nature lie open to my View, (which in my Esteem are far more delightful than the finest Product in Art) my Eyes are feasted with the various Prospect of Fields and Woods, Mountains and Precipices at a Distance, and Trees close by, adorned with inviting Fruit. Here my Ean are charmed with the Harmony of the winged Choir. My Smell is delighted with the wholsome Perfumes, which the Earth plentifully exhales from her fragrant Bosom. And the gentle Sheep graze under my Feet, no longer afraid of their quiet Companion. This is a plain Description, CORINNA, as free from Artifice, at the Place where I am; for I shall tell you no Stories of Pan, Sylvanus, Pomona, and the rest of those idle Whimsies of the Poets. Nor shall I undertake to inform you, what Tune the Bird's fing, or to interpret their Language, and tell you what they say; nor do I concern my self whether they think the same of me for coming amongst them, that Montaigne supposed his Cat thought of him for playing with ber:* But if you ask me what I think of my self, among these solitary Delights, Heaven forgive me and you too.

- Montaigne playing with his Cat, Complains, She thought Him but an Aft.

H V D.

1

and CORINNA.

31

I thank you for the Catalogue of new Books, which feldom or never come to us; among which there is one* which, if I were magotty enough, I would animadvert upon, with all the Seriousness of a Virtuoso, who had catched a new sort of Butterflie, or found a pretty Cockle-Shell, and it could not chuse but contribute much to your Diversion, for one Gimcrac to Comment on another: But it unluckily falls out I am no Greshamite, and for Want of Spectacles, &c. am forced to make use of those dull Eyes Nature has given me, and whether there be Eels or Whetstones in Vinegar, I cannot discover. But I know it has one extraordinary Quality, which Hannibal long ago found out, when he cut his Way thro' the Alps with it, and for this it is still successfully used by our Country Beef-Eaters; who, when the stubborn Rump withstand their flashing Whittles, take but a little Vinegar to sharpen their Fortitude, and then never fail of hewing their Way victoriously thro' the very Bones of it.

If you will allow me to touch upon a tender Subject, and after the ingenious Mr. Norris, to give my Thoughts without Disguise of a Matter which has sometimes employed them, I think more Objects than one deserve our Love: For if (in Mr. Norris's own Way of arguing) Things are more or less valuable, as they have more or fewer Degrees of Excellence, and are more or less exhibitative of the divine Perfection: If the Creatures are in their several Kinds good, and so pronounced by their Creator, tho' not

* Lifter, De Cocblis.

32

LETTERS between PYLADES

absolutely and inderivatively, yet relatively, and by appointment, why not to be loved also with an Affection proportionable to this Merit? That the summum bonum claims our highest and most intense Degree of Love, and that nothing ought upon any Account ever to stand in Competition with Him, is what I suppose all own and understand, who know any Thing of the Matter: But that the Love of God is exclusive of all other Love, is what I think our Duty neither enjoyns, nor our Practice comes up to. Mr. Norris's fine-spun metaphysical Theory of Love * is all unravelled, with a fine Distinction of his own, which is, that we ought to love God only as Good, but other Things for their Good; viz. as far as they are useful and beneficial to us, which is what our Catechism has long ago taught; where the next Command to loving God with all our Heart, is to love our Neighbour as our selves. And in my Opinion, a Friend has Right to a much greater Degree of Love than a Neighbour. I would not be thought hereby to suggest, that I in the least deserve your Affection; and can therefore offer but one Motive, which is, that you would generously shut your Eyes, and blindly resolve to love, only because you are sincerely beloved by

Your most beloved

## PYLADES

- See Mr. Norris's Book, intitled, The Theory and Regulat on Love.

LET.

and CORINNA.

33

LETTER XII.

CORINNA's Answer.

SIR,

TO write on a Subject which one knows only as a nominal Idea, is in my Opinion a very ridiculous Attempt : However, since you have so frankly communicated your Thoughts by writing, and may interpret my Silence for Affent, I chuse rather to expose my Ignorance, than remain liable to a Misconstruction.

How sorry am I to tell you, that I find we have such very opposite Schemes of Thinking, as render it impossible for You and I ever to discourse with Pleasure on the two most noble Subjects, Love and Religion. What Pity it is, the brightest Parts should not pursue the most glorious Ends, and that you, who are so well qualified to maintain a good Cause, should ever take upon you to defend an ill one? Ah, Sir! Mistake no more, the divinest Modification of the Soul, for a meer Appetite common to Brutes, with Human Nature; but far, far beneath the Dignity of a rational and immortal Being!

You bid me consider the physical End of our Creation; I do so, and adore the DIVINE WISDOM! But can we with any Colour of (that modern Idol) Reason, form a more perfect Design than the first Institution? Can finite excel infinite? Or shall the Creature pretend to be wiser than the CREATOR?

## D

五

## 34 LETTERS between PYLADES

I take it in my Turn now, to request, nay conjure you, to consider seriously and impartially, the Confusion that must reign in the World, should your Notions take Place. What an Hobbean State of Nature, or rather what a wretched Scene of brutal Passions and universal Misery must ensue? Are not the Consequences sufficient to evince the adorable Wisdom of the Divine Oeconomist? And will you yet suffer those very excellent Parts, GOD and NATURE have bleft you with, to be misguided by the Free Thinkers of an unhappy Age?

I hope better Things of you, and am for my own Sake willing to suppose you have only rallied me to shew your Wit : Let it pass so, and you will oblige me ; for I shall write no more on a Subject so abhorrent to,

## SIR,

Your Humble Servant,

CORINNA.

## LETTER XIII.

## A Definition of LOVE.

MADAM,

THO' the Original Cause of Love may justly be reckoned among the Arcana Nature, and as DRYDEN well observes, can never

6s

and CORINNA.

35

be assigned,* or clearly described, since Proteus like, it assumes all manner of Forms, and often seemingly contradictory Appearances; since Deformity it self has had its Votaries, as well as the admired Goddess of Beauty. And Wit, which leads some captive, has met with open Defiance and Rebellion from others. But I have Reasons more than sufficient to justify my Passion; Reasons which proceed from Judgment as well as Inclination, and which I should not be afraid to enumerate, were it not for offending your Modeesty, and putting my self under the last Confusion, when I consider that you have not the like Motives to induce you to a Return of Kindness which I had to begin my Addresses. If I were asked the Foundation and Reason of Love in general? my Reply would be, that it was some Excellence or Goodness in the Object relating to the Lover, or some Power or Capacity in the Beloved, of promoting or continuing the Lover's Happiness, and that the Degree of Love is proportionable to the Opinion we have of this Goodness, Power or Capacity. This Definition, if rightly understood, may serve to explain all the general Phenomena of Divine or Human Love, and may also prescribe a Measure for the regulating that most noble Passion of the Soul. This only in general: But to unfold the mysterious Cause of Love between particular Persons, to describe the secret Springs that

D 2 mutu-

* The Cause of Love can never be assign'd, 'Tis in no Face, but in the Lover's Mind.

DRYDEN.

36

LETTERS between PYLADES

mutually touch their Souls, to tell why some love and others hate at first Sight, I may perhaps be able to do, when Philosophy shall intelligibly explain the Reason why the LOADSTONE attracts Iron, but leaves Silver and Gold, more noble Metals, or give a tolerable Account of Sympathy and Antipathy.

I am, &c.

PYLADES.

# LETTER XIV.

A Parallel of ORINDA and CORINNA.

Long Vacation.

MADAM,

IF I did not believe your good Humour to be equal to your Wit, I should think you bantered your Friend most unmercifully. I am sure you would not charge me with a Fault I am uncapable of committing, nor accuse me of running away with the Wit of the Town, if you had the same Opinion of me that I have of myself; for I also have hitherto been very much deceived in my own Character, if there is any Body takes-me for a Wit. That is a Title as far above my Ambition, as out of my Reach; and I have just the cunning of the Fox to cry four Grapes, when I

can

§ Sce Vol. I. Pag. 178.

and CORINNA.

37

cannot come at them. I must confess I never so sensibly perceived the Want of Wit as I do now, for it requires such a vast Fund to pay you in your own Coin, and to ballance the Accompts in Epistolary Commerce, that I shall certainly turn Bankrupt unless you will give large Credit, or, if need be, charitably forgive great Sums. The Satire against Wit, * I remember, was for erecting a Wit-Bank in behalf of the poor Traders to the barren Coast. What Advantage it would be to me, who have no Cash to put in, I know not, perhaps it might have furnished the Town with a Storehouse, that might have supplied its Wants all this Vacation. But it luckily falls out that r. describing the dearth of Wit so ingeniously, you consute yourself, as oftentimes the richest Men complain most of Poverty. Who can think it is scarce, when he sees it overflow so abundantly from your Pen? I easily believe it runs low enough with the mercenary Scriblers and softy Garretteers this Cucumer Season, but I am convinced by other undeniable Arguments, besides your last, that your Treasury is full and inexhaustible.

CORINNA's Wit is like her Eyes ;

Whoever reads, or looks, he dies;

And at a double Avenue,

She does each captive Heart subdue.

## D 3

For

* A Poem under that Title, written by Sir Richard Blackmore.

38

LETTERS between PYLADES

For who can stand against such Charms,

Where Love exerts his strongest Arms ;

Where Wit and Beauty both confpire

To make the ravifh'd World admire.

This is the only Day I have had to my self ever since I wrote to you last, and this I design to spend happily in conversing with you; and in the best Company I shall have while I am in the Country, for which I am obliged to you. I have looked a little into Mrs. Philips, and it is not the first Time I have been wonderfully pleased with her solid Masculine Thoughts, in no Feminine Expression. And I like her the better, because, in my Opinion, her Genius and Way of Writing, comes the nearest (of any Female Author I have met with) to yours, which I think the best that ever fell within my Knowledge. Her refined and rational Thoughts of Friendship (not unlike Yours) which is a Subject she very much delights in, show a Soul above the common Level of Mankind, and mightily raise my Desire of practising what she so nobly describes. I am of Opinion that any Person's Humour and Disposition appears as much, or more, in their Writings, than in their Conversation; and I would never desire a better Character of any one, than what I could learn from their own Pen, where it ran without Restraint, or any particular Byafs. And though I know nothing of Mrs. Philips, but what I have learned from her own Poems, I am confident she

and CORINNA.

was discreet, good-humoured, modest, constant, and virtuous, as well as ingenious. Her Country Life is so sweet a Poem, and sprinkled with such profound Philosophical Thoughts, expressed in easy Poetical Language; or else by a kind of Sympathy it suits so well with my Sentiments and Inclination, that though I have read several Poems, in more Tongues than One, upon the same Subject, yet I do not know where to find a better. But whither am I running? Beyond my Lafte, I am sure; for I am no more a Critic than a Wit or a Poet, leaving those Studies to such as practise them with more Success. And to a Friend I will own, that Philosophy would be my Darling, if I had either Time, or Strength, or Opportunity to pursue it with the Application that it merits and requires: But I have neither, and therefore among the various noble Subjects, with which Philosophy entertains and improves the most exalted Minds, I shall chuse out One, which came from an Oracle, and, in two Words, contains enough to employ all my idle Hours, which can be spared from the necessary Concerns of Life. It is Nosce Teipsum,* (you understand it without an Interpreter). If the Subject be worthless and barren, that is my Fault; yet such as it is, I know none so little studied, and none so necessary and useful among all that employ the busy Minds of Men. My Humour and my Style exactly agree, and I write this with the most prevailing Passion of my Mind, next to my Esteem for Corinna. Pray continue your perverse Humour,

* Know tly Self.

39

mour,

LETTER XV.

His Thoughts of Philosophy.

MADAM,

SINCE you desire my Thoughts at large, concerning Philosophy, you shall have such as not first, but last offer themselves; and if I had more Time, perhaps they might be fewer. For the longer I think, the less I know; and when I cast up what I have gained by my Philosophical Enquiries, and see what the Sum total amounts to, I find my self reduced almost to that Philosopher's Poverty, who after all his Study and Pains came to this Conclusion, Hoc tantum Scio, quid nihil Scio.* I am so very sensible of the Weakness of Human Understanding, both from my own Thoughts, and the irreconcilable Opinions and endless Controversies of others, that I have but one sure Refuge to fly to, but one Support to

! * Thus much I know ; that I know nothing.

rely

and CORINNA.

41

ely upon with any Confidence, and that is Revelation. Were it not for This, I believe universal ignorance would have perpetually overspread the face of the Earth. For whatever the Men of Reason of our Days may boast, I know no better way to learn what Natural Reason can do, than from what it does, and to inform ourselves of that, we must travel to those unhappy Countries, which never heard of any such thing as Revelation; and here, if the Testimony of all Travellers may be credited, we shall soon find enough to humble our Thoughts of Natural Reason, so much indeed, that some of those poor Wretches, who are funk lowest, seem hardly distinguishable from Brutes by any thing but Shape. They have the same Faculties with the rest of their Species, and might make the same use of them as far as Nature will go. But how imperfect is That, even in those Places where Learning has been at the greatest height it could attain, without the Improvement of express Revelation. I say express, because I am of their Opinion, who think all the Learning of the World was originally owing to some obscure Acquaintance with, or dark Hints from the Writings of Mofes, and the divine Dispensations to the Israelites. (But be that Matter as it will.) How gross and crude were the most knowing Heathen's Notions of the Deity? How fickle their Belief of the Soul's Immortality, and a future State? And how different their Sentiments about, what one would think should be easiest known, Man's chieftest Good and supreme Happiness? But not to mention Theories, which some Men

## 42 LETTERS between PYLADES

Men are audacious enough even now to contend. How imperfect was their most refined Morality in comparison with what Revelation teaches! So far from the noble Generosity of forgiving Injuries, and doing Good to Enemies, that it allowed Revenge. So far from Meekness and Humility, that it represented Vain-glory and Ambition as the most honourable Qualities. If this be the Condition of Natural Reason, what then is Philosophy good for? Why truly in my Esteem nothing more, than as it is subservient to the better Understanding of Revealed Truths. This is the only good Use I can make of what little I have, or ever shall have, and I have so mean an Opinion of Human Learning abstractedly considered, that I reckon it only as you do, an innocent Amusement which may serve to pass away some idle Hours now and then more agreeable than impertinent Conversation. Influenced by such Considerations as these, I chose my Nosce Teipsum, not as a Subject meerly Philosophical, but in its largest extent, comprehending all the Duties of the Christian Religion. For he that rightly knows himself, will never be ignorant of his Duty to God and his Neighbour. And I pitched upon it the rather because I think our most certain Knowledge proceeds from Reflection: And it gives me Occasion with great Satisfaction to observe, how exactly Reason and Religion agree in every thing conducive to Human Perfection and Happiness. Thus, CORINNA, at your Request, for I can deny you nothing; I have given you the Result of my Philosophy. If you desire a farther Account of

my

and CORINNA.

by Religion, you may find it in Dr. Scor's Christian Life; whom I am more indebted to, and have greater Esteem for, than any uninfpired Writer whatsoever. The Philosophy of the Christian Region, and the Reason of all its Precepts is, in my apprehension, so clearly taught, and so plainly proved in that divine Book, that he must want both Head and Heart, who can read it without being not almost, but altogether a Christian. As to that Particular in your former Letter; Whether Angels have the same Methods of Reasoning that We have, and Their Science and Ours be the same; it is what I think no Man living knows, nor ever will know, till he gets to Heaven, or an Angel comes thence to inform him. Human Science, such as it is, improved by divine Revelation, must be our Guide thro' this World, and what we shall do in the next it will be time enough to know, when we come there.

P. S. In Answer to your Friend's Quere— Deus de Deo & Lumen de Lumine. Which in plain English is no more than God of God, and Light of Light, as it is translated in the Nicene-Creed; and is there generally understood, by modern Interpreters, to signify the Eternal Generation of the Second Person in the Trinity; by Way of Emanation, as Light from the Sun or Heat from Fire. But whether the Fathers always used it in that Senfe requires

requires more Reading than I am Master of to decide. All that I can say, is, that it is an easy matter to make the Fathers, who are very voluminous, speak quite contradictory to themfelces and to one another. But whoever allows the Divinity of our blessed Saviour cannot make any Doubt of his Eternal Generation; since the latter, is necessarily included in the former.

## To PYLADES, on his foregoing CONFESSION of PHILOSOPHY.

## I.

AH, Sir! with you, I now Confess,

Most vain are all our Sciences:

Life is short, and Art so long, †

Nature weak, and Knowledge strong;

And e're Industry wins the Day,

Grim Death arrests us on the Way,

But tho' we could the Goal attain,

And Masters of all Science reign;

Yet still we must to Death's dread Summons bow,

And Go we know not where; and Die we know not how.

Vita Brevis eff, Ars Longa. Hippocrat.

in vain, he did on Nature's Strength rely,

He, who has Arts great Circle run,

## IV.

'Tis there, we learn our Maker's Will;

There, we praise his mighty Skill;

There, we shun a dreadful Doom;

There, we find a Life to come.

To those sacred Books we owe

All the solid Good we know:

In each charming Page do shine

Laws both Human and Divine:

Wond'rous Judgments! Mercies too!

Yet not a Word, but what is True:

and CORINNA.

The Vaftnefs of Almighty Love:
