# LETTER XVI.

Expressing his Fears of a Rival.

Did not receive yours, my dear CORINNA; till this Evening, and am glad to hear your Health continues in a tolerable State, tho' at the same Time I am an equal Sharer with you in all your Afflictions, and hope I shall, e're long, be able to give more effectual Proofs of my Friendship than an unaactive Sympathy, or a few good Wishes. I thank you for my Entertainment at CATHERINA's, and may you always find equal Welcome, and better Weather to visit your Friends. It grieves me to the Heart to hear that your Mother's Unkindness increases to such a high Degree, especially when I consider, that I am in a great Measure the unfortunate Occasion of it; nor am I unconcerned at the repeated Addresses of the Stoic, || in such a resolute and important Manner; because I am apprehensive of the Trouble it may give you by your Mother's Chagrin, and consequently me. I began to flatter my self that you had put a Stop to any farther Sollicitations of that Kind, and that I should have had no more Disquiet, from that other conceited Rival *; but I perceive some People are not so soon silenced. However, since you are

|| Capt. Hemington.

* The Postafter. See Vol. I. Pag. 53.

E 4

not

56

LETTERS between PYLADES

not shocked (at least I hope you are not) by all the Arguments he or his Mother can propose, I shall without any Uneasiness hear all they can urge; and if I can help it, Another shall never have your Person while I possess your Mind. I could much sooner part with my Life, than resign you to another; and if your Resolution be so firmly settled as to withstand the Attacks of such powerful Vexation at Home, and Importunity from Abroad, I shall have the greater Reason to admire and esteem an affectionate Friendship, so sincere and inviolable. Long ago would I have completed our Union by a Conjugal Tie, not to be divided by any Human Power; if Heaven had been propitious to my Endeavours and Desires. But Providence has thought fit, by many Misfortunes and Crosses hitherto, to obstruct my Designs; which yet if I can at last accomplish, I shall be as happy as this World can make me. But in the mean Time, Is not this to be injurious to you? Is not this to withhold you from better Fortune, and do you a real Diskindness by too resolute a Love, and inflexible Friendship? If you apprehend it to be so, I cannot gainlay a Matter so plainly apparent; nor ought I to be more selfish and ungenerous, than you have all along professed yourself towards me; and whatever might be the Consequence of a Separation, I had much rather my Heart should Break, than give yours the least Moment of Uneasiness. Of all my Rivals (which have been no small Number) I have always fancied that you had the best Opinion of the

and CORINNA.

57

Stoic, and forgive my Surmises, (for you know we are too apt to believe what we fear) I am almost ready to think your Manner of expressing, that if I will give you away, You cannot help it, does intimate some faint latent Wish, or at least Indifference in the Matter, that I should quit my Claim, and resign you to the better Fortune which is now so apparently proposed to you. Pray be free, and let me know if I am not mistaken in both these Particulars. Not long ago Mr. Gwillim was effectually rejected, after a Visit or two; though according to the Character you gave of his Qualifications, I should think him a much more agreeable Person than the Stoic, who yet has been favoured with Letters, with Conversation, and his Mother with frequent Visits. It is this Female Sollicitrix that I am most afraid of; and therefore impute it to the Excess of my Affection, if my Jealousy rises to a higher Degree of Uneasiness on this Occasion, than it has done on any other.

But to finish this disagreeable Topic, the Sum of the whole Matter is this: It is impossible for me to be Happy without you, nor is it in my Power to love another as I do, and must love you. But my Substistence is you know, at present precarious, and how long it may continue so, God only can determine. I have some Hopes from Sir Charles Duncomb, and some from Sir John Guife, but what they may come to cannot be foretold. It is true, I can make no Brags of Expectations from Great Men at Court, nor of Riches already acquired; and therefore, to

58

LETTERS between PYLADES

proceed to a nearer Union, in the present unlucky Posture of Affairs, would be to make both of us exceedingly Unhappy without Hopes of Redress. In the mean time, you shall never want the utmost Assistance I am able to give you, and I hope Fortune will not always frown. But since you have been so generously kind to give the Disposal of your Self to me; I should, and do in Point of Friendship, leave you free to the Choice of what you think most conducive to your true and solid Happiness. Pursue that with the most Rational Views you can, and God direct you for the best. My Misfortunes have been already very great, and I know nothing could aggravate my Afflictions more, than the Loss of you; but may you be always happy and easy, whatever Fate attends

Your very unfortunate,

but most Faithful and Affectionate

PYLADES

P. S. To let you know how much the Accidents of our Lives, as well as Humours and Inclinations do agree: You must know, that I have also been persecuted this Week with the Proposals of a Wife, from Mr. Lawrence, the Chaplain of Highman, being a Niece of his, worth, as it is laid, 2000 l. He wrote to me about it, and would have mentioned it to my Father, but I rode over

to

and CORINNA.

9 5

to Highman, and put a Stop to it; so that it will not come to my Governor's Knowledge, which if it had done, I should have met with some Disquiet. I do not tell you this out of any Vanity, but neatly as a Case something parallel with Yours. I hope I shall not suffer in your Esteem by my Absence, which is like to be more tedious than I can well bear; for if I should get Leave to come up to Town this Term, I should be disabled from coming the next; and I judge it much better for my Health to come when the Spring is more advanced.

Pray conceal nothing from me, but let me hear from Time to Time of all Occurences. I shall long to hear of your Health: Adieu! my dearest only Love. Pray be careful of me: And God Almighty perpetually bless, guide, and preserve You.

## LETTER XVII.*

## Thanks Her for Removing his Fears.

## NOTHING but your dear self (my most beloved CORINNA) could be more welcome

- This Letter was occasioned by my refusing a Gentleman in a very good Post, who engaged his Mother and Sister, my intimate Friends, to second his Proposal of Marriage with me, and they were importunate for the same; which gave Mr. GWINNETT great Uneafiness, and upon my rejecting so advantageous a Match, and dropping the Acquaintance of that Family, he promised me a Support till he should be in Circumstances to marry. E. T.

60

LETTERS between PYLADES

come than your last kind Letter, which, in such an affectionate Manner, removed all my groundless Doubts and Fears, and brought me such desireable such repeated Assurances of your inviolable Fidelity, and undiminished Love.

Tho' I never remember myself to have been guilty of any Error, for which I had so much Cause to rejoice as I have for this; yet I heartily ask your Pardon, for entertaining the least Glimpse of Distrust, or drawing any inconsequent Inferences from your Words; which, I can assure you, was not occasioned by any Diffidence of your Sincerity, but from the Consideration of my own Unworthiness; and was chiefly intended to procure a farther Explication of what I was too much concerned for, to endure the least Suspicion about. And, to my great Satisfaction, the Success has answered my Hopes, and my CORINNA is no more to be moved by Female Application, than by the insignificant Offers of Wealth, and worldly Grandeur. How shall I ever be able to requite such generous Love! such sincere, disinterested Friendship! all I can do will be too little, which yet shall not discourage me from doing the most I am able.

As for your Carriage towards Mrs. Hemington, since it rifes no higher than the Bounds of Civility. I can see nothing in it that is blameable, nothing that is not discreet and commendable: But since my Jealousy must bear some Proportion to my Love, I hope you will not think me guilty of any great Fault, if I was ready to take the alarm at the least Appearance of Danger; and

gave

and CORINNA.

61

gave too much Attention to those Scruples, which my Fears had represented, and magnified to my Imagination. For, to tell you the Truth, I am a very partial Lover; and so desirous of ingrossing you wholly to myself, that I can scarce allow you to be civil to another, less the Civility should by Degrees improve into Friendship; wherein I can admit no third Person; nor do I think it capable of consisting in Perfection betwixt more than Two. But since you have assured me that you can love no other but your unworthy PYLADES, I shall rest entirely satisfied, and require no other Test than your Veracity, on which I would, at any Time, most willingly stake my Life, my Fortune, and my Happiness. I can easily apprehend the Joys and Transports of your Breast, by what I feel in my own; and as the first Confession of your Love, gave me greater Pleasure than Words can express, so every new Confirmation, and Repetition of it, adds fresh Raptures to my delighted Soul, and crowns the Satisfaction which arises from unchangeable Love, and faithful Friendship. After such Assurances of Fidelity and Affection, as you have given me, I shall not harbour any Doubts, or giddy Surmises, whatever my Jealousy may suggest: Spare not, therefore, to write whatever your Friendship shall dictate; and you may be certain, I shall never take any Offence at what you say, because I am, before-hand, satisfied, that you did not intend to give any.

I return you my Thanks for your generous Advice, in reference to Mrs. Segar, for that is the

62

LETTERS between PYLADES

the Name of Mr. Lawrence's Niece: But I chuse rather to follow your Example, than your Counsel; and prefer my Inclinations to my Interest Money is no Temptation to me, and for personal Merit, Knowledge, Virtue, and all the Endowments of Mind and Body, I am very well assured, I shall never find your Equal; at least in my own Opinion. What care I then? if I am Banished from all the World, if I can but obtain you, whom I value more than all the World beside: And I shall have this Comfort in my Exile that I shall enjoy your Conversation with fewer Avocations, and more uninterrupted Complacency.

But lest you should think I speak altogether without Experience, and that my Esteem for you may depend upon my Ignorance of other People's Deserts; you must know, that several Years ago, I saw, and was in the Company of Mrs. Sgar, more than once, at the Widow Lawrence of Shurdington; and if my Sentiments of her then, had been required, I should not have thought her so agreeable, as to have made any Conquest upon a Heart not pre-ingaged. What Alterations Time may have made, I know not, nor do I care to be informed. I never was one of those passionate Lovers that take Fire at first Sight, and is subdued at once by an outside Appearance: And for Conversation, I cannot find any genuine and lasting Charms in any but Yours; all others grow very insipid, after a very little Acquaintance; and the more I know them, the less I like them: But you, on the contrary, are

and CORINNA.

63

so perfectly agreeable, that my Love increases with my Intimacy; and the longer our Friendship continues, the more resolute and intense will mine become.

As for Concerns of Family and Estate, I am not obliged to make my self Miserable, that others may be Rich, for whom it is not my Duty to provide; and besides, I hope Matters are not so bad, but that they may be retrieved.

I returned from Monmouth on Monday last. Pray excuse all my Faults, and be very careful of

Your most faithfully affectionate

PYLADES.

P. S. Adieu, my dearest only Love! God Almighty perpetually bless, and preserve you.

## LETTER XVIII.

## He complains of the Shortness of her Letters.

THO' I cannot much complain so long as you are in Health, my Dear CORINNA, yet the extraordinary Brevity of your Letters (which indeed I cannot tell how to excuse often) does very much lessen the Satisfaction which I promised my self in those Weekly Remembrances. I know your Time is so ingrossed by Family Concerns, and your Mother's Affairs, that I should

## 64 LETTERS between PYLADES

should be unreasonable in expecting any great share of it : But since these missive Visits are all that this unhappy Distance allows, I hope you will not make them so very short when you can spare Time to comply with my Desires, who the longer they are, think them so much the Better.

Yours, &c.

PYLADES.

# LETTER XIX.

On SELF-MURDER.

His Concern for CORINNA, upon mistaking an Expression in one of her Letters.

WHATEVER wrong Inferences you might draw from the mis-interpreted Conclusion of my Letter, I must own I was perfectly amazed at the Beginning of yours. Never could I have imagined that one so well principled, and of so sound an Understanding, as you are, could upon any Provocation have entertained the dreadful Thoughts of Taking a deadly Potion; especially since upon an accidental Conference about the Heinousness of Self-Murder you offered such good Arguments against it, and seemed to have more Courage, than ever to commit so Pufilanimous, to sinful an Action; which as it admits of no Repentance, so it can afford no Hopes of Pardon. May

and CORINNA.

65

May God of his infinite Grace and Mercy preserve both you and me, and all People, in their right Understanding, and secure us from the Temptation of such horrible Resolutions; for I can never think any One in their right Senses can venture on such a desperate Act as may, in all Probability, prove ten thousand times worse than the Disease. Tho' you cannot want Arguments for your Conviction, yet however, to confirm your Abhorrence of this Crime, I would desire you to read Dr. Adams's Book against Self-Murder, which cannot but give you full Satisfaction as to the Guilt, and will, I hope, utterly deter you from all Purposes of the Perpetration.

As to your Apprehensions of my basely deserting You (for that I suppose your Meaning by the Secret you mention) it is what I never intended, and I hope I never shall be guilty of so foul a Crime. I was willing to keep a Sum of Money in reserve for myself, if any Rupture should have happened between my Father and me (as I foresaw it would) as soon as my Intentions of marrying you had been brought to a Consummation.

Now tho' my Intentions are frustrated, by the Misfortune of losing this Money, it is what Providence has ordained, I ought to submit to it with entire Resignation, and hope you will not aggravate my Misfortune, by upbraiding me with the Neglect of your Advice; since what I did was in order to secure the Money for ourselves. And now that you may not tax me any more with Secrecy (tho' I think it is what you have all along professed, and at the Beginning of our Ac- Vol. II. F quaintance

66

LETTERS between PYLADES

quaintance I declared) I have always judged that our Uniting in Marriage has so far depended on Providence, and future Contingencies, that unless we could obtain some competent Maintenance, it were better to continue as we are than to plunge ourselves into worse and irretrievable Circumstances. That I have earnestly endeavoured, from the Beginning of our Acquaintance, to procure some tolerable Livelihood, is what, I suppose, will not be denied: That I have met with ill Success in my Attempts is too true; and that I am now less likely to succeed, than I formerly imagined my self, is too sad a Truth to be disowned: For within these seven Years, my Father (what with Building, and some other Losses besides this last; what with needless Expences about the House and Backside, together with some Extravagancies in House-keeping) has run out above 1000 l. there are several Children to be placed out in the World, and Debts to be paid, which must all be done out of a small Estate; for there is no Getter, as yet, in our Family.

Judge then what must be my Fate if I should marry without my Father's Consent : to be sure, he would give me nothing that he could keep from me ; and the bare Reversion of my Mother's Jointure, if I could dispose of it to the best Advantage, would not be sufficient to maintain me. Add to this, that I am never like to get any thing in the Business I am bred to, for want of my Health in London ; where I can never long reside : So that I have at present but a melan-

and CORINNA.

67

choly Prospect before me, whether it may hereafter clear up, is more than I can determine. And, tho' I had rather part with Life than with my Dear CORINNA, yet, it must be confessed, it is better to die when God pleases, than to live miserable all one's Days.

It is many Times the Course of Country Gentlemen, to live at such a Rate, that at length they have little more than the Name of an Estate left; and then, if the Heir does not bring a Fortune sufficient to clear the Incumbrances in a little Time, farewel to all. If this should be my unhappy Case, as I hope it never will; yet you may be affured I will never proceed in any Matrimonial Affair without your Knowledge; nor ever conclude any Treaty of that Kind, without making you a Sharer of the Fortune; so that your Condition may be made easy, and not in the least precarious.

For this I should think my self obliged in Conscience to do, in Consideration of those good Offers you have refused on my Account; what I have slighted for your Sake are not worth mentioning. Thus, my Dear CORINNA, you have my Thoughts at large, and without reserve, concerning a tender Point, which would not well allow of so open a Treatment, for fear of Misconstruction and Offence, but in Obedience to your Commands. Tho' what I have written is I think no more than what you have often expressed as your Sentiments, both in Word and Letter; and but three Posts since, you proposed an Expedient of this Kind, for repairing my shattered Fortune,

F2

which

68

LETTERS between PYLADES

which makes me the more surprised at your terrible Denunciation of a Mortal Draught, upon the Discovery of I know not what Secret.

It is certainly very imprudent for any Person to marry, unless they can propose to themselves a comfortable Course of Life in that State, which, in its best Circumstances, is attended but with too many Troubles. What then can be expected in the midst of Want and remediless Penury? Let me intreat you, my dearest only Love, to do me the Justice to believe, that what I have said, is not from any Decay of Affection, or out of any Desire of Separation; far from my Heart are all such Thoughts, whatever Misfortunes I may meet with, yet my Love and Friendship must, and will continue, as long as Life. And whatever Necessity may urge me to hereafter, yet this you may depend on, that you shall be made Judge of the Case; and if you do not allow it to be highly reasonable, and as much for your Advantage as my own, I will submit to your Determination, and shall be happy at least in my Obedience to you, whatever be my Fate in other Respects.

I most heartily join with you in your pious Petition at the End of your Letter, and earnestly beseech you, to lay aside all Thoughts of such Desperate Remedies, whatever Evils you may at any Time lie under. Let us rely on Providence, whose Goodness

and CORINNA.

69

may bestow better Things than we desire; at least, may you be happy, whatever Fate may be allotted for

Your most Faithful,

and Affectionate

PYLADES.

## LETTER XX.

## CORINNA's Answer to the foregoing Letter.

Must own my self so perfectly astonished at the unkind (I will not say wilful) Misconstruction in your last, that I can scarce compose myself to Write. To be mistaken in one or two Articles, is more than I could have expected from a Person whom I thought had known me as well as I do my self; but to be misconstrued in all, is very severe Usage. As to the Money, I had dropped that Argument long since, if you had not, by repeated Desires, obliged me to acquaint you, from Time to Time, with what I heard; and in doing that, I have been particularly careful not to express any Thoughts of mine that might increase your Affliction; yet am I reproached in your last, with upbraiding you for neglecting my Advice.

In

70

## LETTERS between PYLADES

In the next Place, you are pleased to give me an Exhortation against rash Marriage, and plunging ourselves into remediless Want and Penury. I must own your Arguments are very just and reasonable; But why all this to me? Did I ever prompt you to such an Indiscretion? Or did I ever seem to desire you should put me upon it? Or are these Truths you have now uttered any more than what from the very Beginning of our Acquaintance I have often inculcated, both by Word of Mouth and Letter? and confirmed but lately by my last, save three. Certainly you could not think me either so fickle or so stupid, as to resign you with Indifference! And sure I am, I could never have believed you would have refused me the Consolation of uttering a few tender Expressions on so solemn an Occasion: But since that poor Relief has given you such dismal Apprehensions, I shall take care to restrain my Pen for the future. But to come now to that, which tho' it has the last Place in this Paper, has the first in your Letter, and my Astonishment: I must say I am so perfectly amazed, that I can scarce believe it to be your Writing. Good God! how is it possible you could surmise a Crime so remote from my Thoughts? whatever my Misfortunes have been, they have never yet tempted me to such a Wickedness; and as for what may come, I hope the Grace of God will still continue to preserve me. Let me intreat you to read over that Part of my Letter, and upon second Thoughts, judge whether you had any Cause for such a dreadful Censure. What I wrote

was

and CORINNA.

71

was in haste, I make no foul Copies, and I cannot recollect every Word; but my Memory does not charge me with the least dubious Expression, unless you will needs have my using the simple Figure of a Deadly Draught to be more emphatical than your calling my Proposal a desperate Drench. Sure I am, I meant no more by it, than I understood you did by yours; and shall be extreamly concerned if my Pen has let fall any thing liable to such a Construction: However, let this suffice for your Conviction; I solemnly declare, in the Presence of Almighty God, who alone knows the Integrity of this Affeveration, that I neither had any Design upon my own Life, or desired that you should suppose I had; nor did it enter into my Thoughts that you could strain such an Inference from so innocent, so common a Phrase as that was. Your Designs for my Advantage are more generous than I deserve, or can with Honour accept: But I think it extreamly unkind, that you should imagine the Precariousness of my Fortune made a deeper Impression on me, than the Loss of You. No, PYLADES, those who Love like Me, and yet can force themselves to Resign what they love, may with Ease Resign all Things else in this World. Nor can so mean a Thought, as Goods of Fortune, or the most necessitous Prospect, ever dare to interrupt so noble, so glorious a Conquest.

I take the candid View you are pleased to give me of your Father's Circumstances, as the highest Instance of your Friendship : And tho' the Knowledge of these sad Truths have cut off all my Hopes of Happiness in this World, yet F4

## 72 LETTERS between PYLADES

I have gained this Advantage by it, that I can no longer harbour a Wish so detrimental to your self and Family, as Our Union. Adieu, my dearest PYLADES; may you enjoy all the Blessings this Life can afford, and be eternally happy hereafter, is the unfeigned Prayer of

Your most Affectionate,

and Faithful

CORINNA.

P. S. My Mother gives you her Service: She knows nothing of this Change of Affairs. I think I have answered your Letter so fully, that it requires no Rejoinder; and I could wish, for my Quiet, it might have none; but I must intreat to see my last, save One, when you have a convenient Opportunity.

## LETTER XXI.

## PYLADES's Reply.

ON my coming Home this Week, I received your Letter, dated the 26th of July, and tho' you were not, as you say, surprized at Mine, I must own that I am a little at Yours; for I cannot imagine, what Declaration you mean. I am not conscious of any other, than what I have made from the very Beginning of our Acquain-

tance,

and CORINNA.

73

ance, which was, that since I cannot have my Health in London, no Motive should prevail with me to settle there. And as soon as I could obtain Substience in the Country, whereby we might live above down-right Slavery and Want, I should think nothing needful to compleat my Happiness, but the most entire and sacred Union with you.

I have, it is true, been long in vain endeavouring after this Settlement, which yet I do not despair of acquiring, if over-ruling Providence shall think it best for us. As to any Intention of my Friends, as you intimate, I know of no Proposal that has of late been made to them, and if any such Thing should happen, you shall never have Cause to complain for want of timely Notice thereof. What you apprehend from your personal Defects, Advance of Age, and Decay of Beauty, is altogether groundless; for I can assure you my Affection is not founded upon so superstitial a Regard as Face or Form. Besides, in those natural Decays I am equal with you, and therefore the more agreeable: Nor can I ever be so happy in any Other's Arms as Yours.

I hope to be with you in less than two Months, and then I shall more fully answer all your Doubts and Scruples. In the mean Time you may be assured, that it is no easy Matter to transplant an inflection so long eradicated as mine has been. And if your Health be not deficient, I hope nothing else will be wanting to compleat our Happiness in a Conjugal Union. If hard Necessity, and overruling Fate should otherwise ordain, yet it is what lies at present in Darkness, and beyond the

74

LETTERS between PYLADES

the Reach of Human View ; and therefore, with your Leave, I shall still call you (what you first omitted) my dearest only Love, and desire you to be very careful of

Your most faithfully Affectionate

PYLADES.

# LETTER XXII.

## CORINNA's Rejoinder.

Cannot be surprized at the Declaration in your Last, the late Alteration of your Style having given me Leisure to prepare for it; nor can I with Justice, or Gratitude complain. I have always had Friendship enough to partake of whatever Good or Evil has attended you, since our first Acquaintance, and, if I know my self, shall always continue to do so. I am but too sensible of the extream Difficulties with which you must have struggled, to support me; and could never find any real Satisfaction, since my Calamities have made me a Burthen to my Friend; and so truly disinterested are my Sentiments towards you, that I protest, in the Presence of Almighty God, I had much rather see you Happy in the Arms of Another, than Miserable in Mine. Why then should you conceal the Intention of your Friends any longer from Me? Or, safe such formal Inuendoes to acquaint me with them.

and CORINNA.

them. Unworthy should I be of the glorious Name of Friend ! if I could not bear so Rational a Trial ; or should prefer my Private, single Defires, to the Satisfaction of your whole Family, and the solid Establishment of your own Happiness.

# To P Y L A D E S.

## A

CONVERSATION

BETWEEN

MELISSA (Lady Chudleigh) MUSIDORA (Mrs. Bridgman) CORINNA, the STOIC, (Captain Hemington) &c.

LAST Saturday, the charming Musidora, took me again with her to visit Melissa; and having staid some Time, left me there; when the Stoic || coming in, my Lady began to tell him how happy she had been in the most agreeable Conversation of Mrs. Bridgman. Ay, Madam, says he) where the Carcass is, there the Eagles will be gathered together. Her Ladyship smiled upon me at these Words, and I could not forbear tel-

|| Capt. Hemington.

75

I am, &c.

CORINNA.

ling

76

LETTERS between PYLADES

ling him, It was but a course Compliment for a Courtier, to call a fair Lady Carrion, and to her Face too. MELISSA laughed heartily at his Blunder, and the Raillery I made him upon it; neither was I without a sufficient Share in the Diversion, for never was any Man under a more visible Confusion. Never did any Creature look so simple as the poor Philosopher, when he cried, A Fool's Bolt was soon shot; and adding, those Words passed from him without Consideration. Which ingenious Confession ended our Mirth for that Time.

On Monday I went to take my Leave of MELISSA, who enquired for you very kindly, and said, She should have been glad to have seen you oftner, and commanded me to give you her Service. While we were talking, the Stoic peeped in, and withdrawing immediately, I cried, See Madam, the Dove still flies from the Vulture. Have not I as much Cause, returned she, to be offended at the Fest? Yes, certainly, said I, and more too, for your Ladyship had the worst Part of the Allusion. Well then, replied she, let's forgive an Inadvertency which is common to People who have not been long used to a general Conversation. By this time the Philosophical-Warriour had re-assumed his Fortitude, and ventured to make One in our Circle; as soon as the customary Scrapes, Bows, Twinkles, and Compliments were over, her Ladyship told him, I had not forgot his calling me a Bird of Prey, and that I was meditating a Revenge for the Scandal. And I, replied he, am so far from recanting that I still say, by a sad Experience, Mrs. THOMAS is the same I then called her, and one of the most

and CORINNA.

77

dangerous Sort; for she Devours the Hearts of her Admirers, and is herself Invulnerable. This is a fine Character, indeed, said I, but since you have chosen Prometheus for the Simile, I hope you will accompany him to the End, and confess that your Heart Increases, as fast as the Vulture Devours it. That is the Torment, replied he, could your Cruelty Waste it, I should in Time be free from my Pain; and know no more Uneasiness; but, alas! my hard Fate still preserves me to bear the Rigour of your Frowns. I know not, said I, what your Ladyship thinks, but, to me, the Captain seems a stranger Metamorphosis than any in Ovid: Is it possible a Month's-Waiting, in the Royal-Presence, should make such an Alteration! and that He whom I thought scarce Conversable, should utter such a Piece of Gallantry. It is his Way, replied she, to be silent in my Company, but he were much to blame should be continue that Humour in yours. Alas, said I, he was so taken up with the Contemplation of his own Perfections, that I used to number his Words by the Clock. I must confess his Visits did me much Honour, but they always gave me a great deal of Fear. Then I perceive, returned she, your Thoughts are mutual. You say, you Dreaded his Sight, and he has often told me, he Trembled at Yours. Good Madam, said I, am I such a frightful Object? And good Madam, interrupted he (to me) am I such a frightful Companion? Yes, replied I, when People refuse to join in Conversation, I always suppose them to be making Remarks on the Company. And when, continued he, was I grilty of such Rudeness? The 19th of November last, replied I,

you

78

LETTERS between PYLADES

you were Three Hours at our House, and hardly structured us with Three Dogma in all that Time. You are unjust, replied he, to make that Inference, or expect I should listen to any Discourse while I gazing on your Face; and that I constantly did so you yourself acknowledges. Never, said I, unless you can show my Picture on the Cieling, for sure I any your Eyes were so fixed on that Place, that, ever since, when I am minded to assume a Stoical Gravity, I always direct mine thither. Is it impossible, returned he, to view the Sun by Refracted Rays? No certainly, replied I. But Madam (said he to my Lady) I hear the Roads are most grievously inflicted with Robbers, I hope you will have a good Guard (Now you must know by the way, that her Lady's ship has bought a French Chaise, for herself and the Stoic; and He has bought another of the same Sort for Madam his Sister, and my Lady's Woman. These go but with one Horse each, and the Chaise is hung so low, that where the Roads are deep, they must be up to the Mid-leg. Neither Mrs. Hemington, nor Mrs. Abigail know how to manage their Horse, judge then what Comical Adventures they must expect in a long Journey of 160 Miles. My Lady has promised me an Account at large, and if it does not prove Tragical, as I much fear, I believe it will be Diverting enough. But to return) Yes, replied her Lady's hear the Devonshire Coach was robbed last Week and Two Gentlemen killed. Pray Captain my good Fusee. Yes, Madam, said he, cany ... fright Why, returned she, am I to defend You, defend Me? Neither, replied he, can I defend me

and CORINNA.

79

than themselves; it is not Strength of Arms, but the Presence of Mind that arises from a good Conscience which strikes a Terror on those guilty Wretches. But what do we talk of Fusees? Could I defend my Heart from those Eyes, as well as my Purse from Padders, I would fear no Dangers that could be sal me. (Here my Lady perceiving I played my Fan about my Face, maliciously pretended to want it, and kept it during my Stay.) Well, Captain, said she, I find you are desperately in Love. What Demonstration will you give Mrs. Thomas of your Sincerity? Could you vault over the Heads of an Hundred Men that obstructed your Passage, and Dance through a Cheese-cake-Basket, to force your Way to her? Through Fire and Bullets, cried he, if my Mistress were in view, and would but inourage my Addresses. Alack, replied I, how a Body may be mistaken; I thought this Gentleman had gained a perfect Apathy, and I now find he is as great a Slave to his Passions as the gentlest Wight Drive. Mr. Hemington, said my Lady, has deired me to chuse him a Wife, and I would have your Advice. What think you of CORINNA? I think, said (interrupting her) the Captain is so refined a Philosopher that my Friend the Count DE GABALIS* would be the fittest Person (with Submission to your Ladyship) to direct his Choice. One of his SYLPHS, GNOMES would make an admirable Consort for a Person of such a Metaphysical Constitution. Ab,

- See (in the former Volume) PYLADE'S XVIth Letter sent to OINNA, with The History of the Count De GABALIS, in French, it is now to be had in English of Mr. CURLL, in Burleigh over-against the Savvy in the Strand. Price 1s. 6d.

replied

80 LETTERS between PYLADES

replied he, why do you talk of Etherial Nymphs, I am for Flesh and Blood, not airy Phantoms: No, Madam, I shall not ransack the Elements for my Choice; I desire no more than your Assistance to gain the Party who most resembles yourself. You are very obliging, returned my Lady; but Mrs. Thomas, what think you of the Proposal? He has led me a weary Life to bequeath You to him. It is out of Fashion, said I, to leave Friends for Legacies. No, replied she, I do not mean at my Death, but only during my Absence. You may be assured, said I, very humbly, that I shall esteem him as your Ladyship's Friend. Well, Captain, said she, I have no more to say, you see I have performed my Promise. He thanked her for the Favour, and they looked upon one another with Eyes that shewed a Resentment for having spoke so much to so little Purpose; and as for my own Part, I was under no small Confusion when I found the Gallantry was turned to Earnest; but, Mr. Sydal, and Mrs. Astell coming seasonably to my Relief, put an End to the Discourse. You may, perhaps, expect something from Mrs. Astell, but, I will assure you, she would not know Me. However, I was even with Her at my Departure, and returned her as slighting a Notice; and left the Stoic should follow my Lady's Example, passed through the Room without so much as turning towards him. Upon cooler Thoughts, I am ashamed of the publick Incivility, which I should not have been guilty of, at another Time, in respect to my Lady; but I was so provoked with Mrs. Astell's haughty Carriage, that I knew not how to behave myself O, how hard is it, for one of our frail Sex, to ref

and CORINNA.

8r

the Vanity of public Applause. I have several Times put my Lady upon Pumping her for a Reason of this Alteration, and she protests to me, that Mrs. Aftel never gave her any; but her Ladyship's own Thoughts were, that she was so much sollicited by Women of the greatest Quality and Fortune, that she had not Time enough to repay all their Kindnesses with her Conversation. This may be true, for aught I know, and She who has no certain Subsistence, may be allowed to improve a Friendship with those that have; but methinks she might forbid the Addresses of her insignificant Admirer with a little more Decency, in Gratitude to her true Friend Mr. Norris, who gave the Acquaintance, but what she pleases; nothing against Stomach.

## P. S. This Paper was dropt, by my LADY, into my Lap.

## WOMAN the Crown of the CREATION.

## Of their CONVERSATION.

A WOMAN's Eyes may set Fire to a Heart, but it is her Breath must blow it into a Flame.

## Of their MODESTY.

Modesty is the darling Attribute of a WOMAN; the Great Moral Diffinction between Her self and Man. This it is that recommends her Beauty, and invigorates every Charm, that upholds Vol. II. G the

82

LETTERS between PYLADES

the Dignity of her Nature, and preserves the whole Circle of her Vertues inviolable. To talk of immodest Women is Nonfense, there can be no such; for to lose their Modefty, they must lose part of their Nature, and then they are no longer Women, but Monsters; consequently such as degenerate in this Particular, bring no Scandal upon the Sex, for they cease to be Members of it.

## Of their LEARNING.

WOMEN have Knowledge sufficient to make their Lives useful, and their Ends happy; and all beyond that, is toilsome in the Pursuit; besides there is no such Thing as a perfect Acquisition.

## Of their PRUDENCE.

PRUDENCE is a Serious Consideration of our own Condition, and a constant Management of our selves, agreeable to the Sense we have of it.

## Of their BEAUTY.

I take Care, for my own Part, never to decry any Woman for being absolutely ugly, because her Person does not fall in with my particular Notion of what is taking and agreeable; for I make no Doubt, but in all such Caes, it exactly answers the Idea which One, or More, have framed to themselves of a perfect Beauty.

An

and CORINNA.

# An IMITATION of the Second Satire of Boileau.*

Inscribed to PYLADES.

THOU, whose ingenious fertile Brain,

Bring'ft forth with Pleasure not with Pain

Instruct me in the Knack of Chime,

And how to tune my Words with Rhime;

For Phæbus did to Thee impart

His Knowledge in the Tuneful Art:

And something more he gave, as due;

The Art of Verifying too.

So smoothly flows your Vein, and clear,

So sweet your Choice, yet so severe:

Each Thought and Word in order plac'd,

Each Line, with Wit, and Learning grac'd :

* Bollene Addreffes his 2d Satire to Moliere.

G2

And

84 LETTERS between PYLADES

And when you condescend to write, The Muse unask'd affists your Flight.

But I! by vain Caprice betray'd,

Have of a Pastime Penance made:

And for some Folly, or some Crime,

Am doom'd perpetually to Rhime.

In vain! I change the tuneful Feet,

In vain! my silly Noddle beat;

For when the Verse calls out for White,

The Muse presents me Black in Spite;

And when I'd sing some wond'rous Bard,

Instead of Dryden, she cries Ward.

My Patience loft! I then give o'er,

And almost vow I'll write no more.

Yet, when I've somewhat eas'd my Spleen, 'And rail'd at Muse and Hippocrene,

Methinks

and CORINNA.

85

Methinks the Goddess does appear,

And chide me for my dull Defpair.

Then, Maugre all my former Pain,

I snatch the Paper up again;

Forget what I so lately spoke,

And great Apollo's Aid invoke.

Yet, if for Rhime I could admit

A cold, insipid Epithet,

I need not biting Fingers stand,

But take a jingling Word at Hand :

For if I prais'd some beauteous Phillis,

I'd call her, Whiter! than the Lillies,

Or else compare her to the Sun,

That Metaphor would easy run.

Transposing thus a few fine Words,

Which Custom, or kind Chance affords,

I without Art, or Genius either,

Might quickly tack two Lines together.

G 3

But

86 LETTERS betweent PYLADES

But my crofs Muse fuch Verse refuses,

And trembles at each Word the chufes;

Difdains to fill a vacant Place,

With Words that may her Work disgrace;

Vex'd at her Choice, begins a-new,

And writing three Words, blots out two:

Yet still dislikes! still strives to mend!

And thus her Labours never end.

Curs'd be the Wretch! who first confin'd

To Rules of Art, the nobler Mind :

Curs'd! be the First this Art admin'd,

Curs'd! be the Demon, which infpir'd,

'And fetter'd up his Thoughts sublime,

In strict Imprisonment of Rhime:

Forcing us piece-meal to rehearse,

And split the Sentence with the Verse.

O PYLADES! how bleft were I In my belov'd Obscurity?

If

and CORINNA.

87

If I had ne'er the Muse addreft,

Nor with the Frenzy been posflest :

No pamper'd Nun herself could please

With fewer Cares, or greater Ease;

Or lead a more unaactive Life,

More free from Fear, or void of Strife.

But now, this tedious Doggrel Strain,

With nightly Fumes, invades my Brain:

And when the Town with Sleep is bleft,

'Tis then the Muse disturbs my Reft ;

Commands me from my Bed to rise,

And follow her where'er the flies.

Then like some poor inchanted Fool,

That's made a rambling Witch's Tool,

I can't forbear, but up must get,

And run, and strive, and pant, and swcat;

Till wearied with this haggard Trade,

And by her hum'rous Will delay'd,

G4

With

88

LETTERS between PYLADES

With Discontent I fit me down,

And envy Secretary Brown, $^{*}$

On Mevius, $\dagger$ whose prolific Muse

Each Month whole Volumes does produce :

And with a formal dull Pretence,

Sets up for Wit, in Spite of Sense.

What, tho' not one Poetic Line,

Does in an hundred Pages shine;

The Pow'r of Wit he can defy,

And yet find Fools enow to buy!

Mavius! is sure himself to please,

Observes no Rules, and writes with Ease;

Admires his own Gigantic Strain,

Then hugs himself, and writes again :

Where-e'er he goes, he does rehearse,

And murders all his Friends with Verse.

* Tom Brown.
† Sir R. Blackmore.

But

and CORINNA.

89

But 'tis not so with Wits polite;

They chuse with Care each Word they write;

And think the Muse too flowly flies!

If too sublime she does not rise.

The Critic-World they charm with Ease,

But ne'er their nicer Selves can please.

So far from Pride, or vain Conceit,

So modest, yet fo nobly Great;

That when they hear their Works admir'd,

They wish they ne'er had been inspir'd.

O Thou! who feest my jaded Muse,

Prithee thy kind Endeavours use :

Teach me the Method of Inditing;

Or qualify this Itch of Writing.

# ESSAY on the Mischief of giving Fortunes with Women in Marriage.*

Fæcunda culpæ fæcula Nuptias

Primum inquinavère, & Genus & Domos.

Hoc Fonte derivata Clades

In patriam populum; fluxit.

Hor.

THE most accomplished Writer of his Age, Sir William Temple, does in a very elegant Similitude, represent the vain Attempts of such as endeavour to alter the common Course of Things, and to reform the World by their own Models, by comparing them to "A Man in a little Boat, who tuggs at a Rope that is made fast to a Ship; it looks as if he

* This Effay written by PYLADES 1712, was Published by CORINNA in the Year 1727.

refol

and CORINNA.

9.1

resolved to draw the Ship to him, but the Truth, and his Meaning, is to draw himself to the Ship, where he gets in when he can, and does like the rest of the Crew when he is there." This were enough to deter every considerate Person from presuming to find Fault, at least from proposing any Amendment of what he thinks amiss; since tis more than probable his well-intended Labours will not meet with their deserved Success. And this perhaps may be the Case, where one Man shall conceitedly think himself wiser than all the rest, and Dictator-like, impose his own private Opinion as the Rule and standard of all other Mens Thoughts and Actions. But where the Grievance is very great, and the Complaint universal, where every one suffers, or is in Danger of it, and would willingly be relieved, sure one need not fear humbly to propose Ways and Means for a Redress.

And let every Man judge for himself whether here be not something in MATRIMONY, the most important Concern of Life, very much amiss, something that yet calls for a Reformation.

For how frequent are the unfortunate Matches, how rare the happy? The loud Complaints which all Places refound from the Houses of the married, or the more soft (but more pathetic) Whispers unloaded into the Bosom of some compassionate Friend, do sufficiently testify, that of those who fail into this dangerous Ocean, very now make a lucky Voyage, very many sink under their insupportable Misfortunes, and almost all have more Reason to repent than rejoice at their Adven-

92

LETTERS between PYLADES

Adventure. The repeated Quarrels and Brawls, and Contentions among the Vulgar, the too common Dislike, Resentment, and Discord among those of a higher Rank, and the many ignominious Separations, and scandalous Divorces among all Sorts, confirm the Observation, and render Marriage in the general Opinion of Mankind, as the French term it, Un grand Mal. (a great Evil.) Even among those, who break not out into open Broils, but carry it fair enough outwardly with a good Face upon the Matter, how is the matrimonial Contract invaded, if not violated, by little Jars and Animosities, by fullen and peevish, or haughty and domineering Behaviour towards each other? The Tyranny of the Husband, and the Rebellion of the Wife are alternately inveighed against with equal Violence and Justice, and where both Parties complain, and both are in Fault, it gives the World Occasion to think that Sir Thomas More had good Reason to compare Marriage to a Bag of Snakes with one Eel among them, where, tho' you catch never so cautiously at the Eel, it is twenty to one but you are stung by a Snake, and that there is so much Gall succeeds the Honey-Moon, as makes the Cup so nauseous after the first Draught, that both Parties are quite sick of their Condition, and not half so eager to tye the Gordian Knot at first, as now (if it were possible) to undo it again. Hence the sacred Institution is burlesqued and derided by Libertines, and Wedlock is become as much a Jest as Love; it is entered upon blindfold, and without Consideration, by the rash and short-sighted, but with

and CORINNA.

Fear and fore-boding Apprehensions by those who can take above one Month's Prospect into their View,and is generally attended with such unhappy Consequences as imbitter all the Comforts of Life, and make the Yoke severely gall the Necks of those who have undertaken to bear it.

This Disease is grown Epidemical, and I wish long Habit has not made it incurable, but since in healing any Malady, the Discovery of the Cause from whence it proceeds is one half of the Cure, why should we despair of Success, when it is evidently conspicuous, that the original Fountain of those prevailing Calamities, which (like an impetuous Deluge) so frequently overflow a Conjugal State, is Men's marrying merely for Money, without any Regard to the other genuine and more noble Ends of Matrimony. This is the true Source of all those Miseries, which are in this Condition for the most part as justly suffered as complained of; nor is it to be admired, that such a fruitful Root as Avarice should spring up, and branch out into an innumerable Multitude and Variety of Evils. However, since the Public is injured by the Ill-Fortune, and worse Conduct of private Persons, and Men big enough for Wives have so much of the Child in them fill, that they will not be instructed without Discipline, will not take the wholesomeest Physic without Compulsion, for a Remedy of these and many other national as well as domestic Calamities and Disorders, suppose it were enacted,

93

That

94

4 LETTERS between PYLADES

That Women should bring no Portions with them in Marriage, no more than what is just sufficient to put them in handsome Cloaths, suitable to their Ranks, and to defray the moderate Expenses of the Wedding, which among private Gentlewomen may be limited to 100l. and so proportionably to the highest Quality not above 1000l.

And to prevent, or frustrate all secret Compacts, and fraudulent Contrivances, that might probably happen in such Cases, it would be expedient to ordain,

That every Woman's Fortune, whether in Land or Money, should upon her Marriage descend to the new Heir Pale, and be recoverable by him in due Course of Law.

This I fancy will at first Sight, and upon a transient View, appear very surprizing and paradoxical indeed; much like that Law, which to prevent Drunkenness, and the Abuse of Wine ordered all Vineyards to be destroyed.

What! Give no Fortunes with Women? Who will marry then? Who do you think will take a Wife without something to maintain her? Our Daughters stick long enough upon our Hands; it is already, and then surely they will grow quite superanuated, and we shall be stifled with Petticoats in a little while, for Want of a timely Removal. In fine, it must needs be a great Hindrance to Matrimony, and the Multiplication of People.

Bu

and CORINNA.

But perhaps upon a deliberate Examination, it may appear quite contrary. For if we make but just Allowances for the many likely Matches, which are broken off merely upon the Account of Fortune and Settlement, without any Dislike of the Parties; if we consider the many Delays and Difficulties those Men meet with, who let up for Fortune-Hunters (as they are called) whereby many of them spend all their Days in the Fox-chase, waste themselves in a wild Pursuit of the Golden Dream, and after many a tiresome Journey and mortifying Disappointment, after many an anxious Hour and sleepless Night, must wither away, and at last die Old unpitted Batchelors. On the other Hand, if we survey the female Part of the World, we shall presently differ, that almost all those who either with or against their Wills live to be old Maids, are such as having considerable Fortunes, and little else to recommend them, but exalted above themselves with a vain Conceit of their pregnant Bags, refuse such suitable Offers as are made them at a proper Season, and stand so long in their own Light, being dazled with the Splendor of their delusive Cash, that at length they lose their Opportunity, which always flies a-pace, and when once gone is never to be recalled again. There be a great many Difficulties in this Way to Wealth, which are oftentimes insuperable. The Father must be satisfied with a Fortune, and the Son must be pleased in a Woman too. The Father of the Woman must acquiesce in the Son-law's Estate, and the Woman herself must like her

95

96 LETTERS between PYLADES

her humble Servant, or else there can be no Match. And where there are so many Persons concerned, whose Interests may seem to thwart one another, it is not much to be admired, if more Treaties of this Kind fail than succeed. These and many more Impediments would be clearly removed by the proposed Institution, which therefore must evidently tend to the Promotion of Marriages, and the Increase of Mankind. It must therefore necessarily be approved of by the Politicians, who tell us, that the Riches of any Country do not consist so much in the Fertility of the Soil, as the Number of the Inhabitants.

But because some Men are apt to be alarmed at any Thing, that has the least Appearance of Novelty or Innovation, and cannot endure the Thoughts of being turned out of a common (tho'a dirty and incommodious) Road. To prevent or remove all Fears and Jealousies of this Kind, it may be convenient to observe, that this is no new-fangled whimsical Project of a dreaming Enthusiast, or sickly Brain, but has of old been honoured with a Place among the Maxims of the most understanding Men, and introduced into the Laws of the most happy, and most refined Governments.

Wise Solon among his renowned Laws, which were also for their Excellency transcribed by Embaissadors, sent on Purpose from Rome to Athens and strictly observed by the Romans, this divine Legislator, I say, forbad Dowries, ordaining that a Bride should bring no more with her, than

ar.1' CORINNA.

97

three Gowns, and some slight Household-stuff of small Value; for he would not that Marriage should be mercenary or vendible, but that the Man and Woman should cohabit for Issue, Love, and Friendship.

And Lycurgus the Lacedemonian Law-giver, so much admired when living, and adored when dead, allowed no Woman to marry unless without a Fortune, that Men might not be allured to Marriage by Interest, but from the sweet Constraint, and unsophisticated Distates of Nature, from that mysterious Agreement and Sympathy of Minds, which alone can make Men happy in a married State. And this was one of those famous Ordinances, which received this Testimony from the Oracle of Apollo at Delphos. That his Laws were excellent, and that the People which observed them should live in Happiness and Renown. Which the Spartans found experimentally true for the Space of five hundred Years, as long as they continued in the strict Observance of them.

These among the Antients; and to shew that it is as singular as new, and mention One for All, among the Moderns, there is a Passage à propos in Sir William Temple's Essay on Popular Discontents, written with such Strength of Reason, and inimitable Elegance of Style (as indeed all his Writings are) and founded upon such solid Principles of true Philosophy, that I cannot deny my self the Satisfaction of transcribing it.

Vol. II.

98

LETTERS between PYLADES

"I will not say the Races of our Nobility and Gentry are much impaired within these forty or fifty Years, though I have heard others lament it, by their Observation of many successive Parliaments, wherein they have served; but no Man I suppose will doubt they are capable of being improved, or think that Care might not as well be taken for Men by public Institutions, as it is for the Races of other Creatures by private Endeavours. The Weakness of Children, both in their Bodies and Minds, proceeds not only from such Constitutions or Qualities in the Parents, but also from the ill Consequences upon Generation by Marriages contracted without Affection, Choice, or Inclination; (which is allowed by Naturalists upon Reason, as well as Experience.) These Contracts would never be made but by Mens Avarice and Greediness of Portions with the Women they marry, which is grown among us to that Degree, as to surmount and extinguish all other Regards and Desires: So that our Marriages are made just like other common Bar-gains and Sales, by the meer Consideration of Interest or Gain, without any of Love or Esteem of Birth, or of Beauty itself, which ought to be the true Ingredients of all happy Compoi-tions of this Kind, and of all generous Productions. Yet this Custom is of no antient Date in England, and, I think, I remember within less than fifty Years, the first noble Families that married into the City for down-right Money, and thereby introduced by De-

and CORINNA.

69

"grees this public Grievance, which has since ruined so many Estates, by the Necessity of giving great Portions to Daughters; impaired many Families by the weak or mean Productions of Marriages made without any of that Warmth and Spirit, that is given them by Force of Inclination and personal Choice; and extinguished many great ones, by the Aversion of the Persons who should have continued them. I know no Remedy for this Evil under our Sun, but a Law, providing, that no Woman, of what Quality soever, shall have the Value of above Two Thousand Pounds for her Portion in Marriage, unless she be an Heiress, and that no such above the Value of two hundred Pounds a Year shall marry to any but younger Brothers."

"By this, Mens general Hopes of making their Fortunes by Wives, would be turned to other Courses and Endeavours more useful to the Public; young Women would not be exposed by the want of Fortunes, and despair of marrying without them: Noble Families would not be exhausted by Competition with those of meaner Allay, in Point of Portions: Marriages would be made upon more natural Motives, and more generous Considerations, than meer dirty Interest, and Increase of Riches without Measure or End, Shape and Beauty, Birth and Education, Wit and Understanding, gentle Nature, and agreeable Humour, Honour and Virtue would come in for their Share in such Contracts as must always have so great an

" Influence upon all Mens Lives, and their Posterity too. Besides, some little Damps would be given to that pestilent Humour and general Mistake of placing all Felicity in the endlefs Degrees of Riches, which, beyond a certain Proportion, suited to each Rank, neither conduce to Health nor Pleasure, to Ease nor Convenience. The Love of Money is the Root of all Evil; which is a Truth that both Morals and Politics, Philosophy and Divinity, Reason and Experience all agree in, and which makes the common Disquiets of private Life, and the Disturbances of public Governments.

## Quid non mortalia pectora cogis

## Auri sacra fames?

The united Authority of this great Triumviri might perhaps be sufficient to convince every rational Reader; but to pursue this Matter a little farther, and set it in a clear Light, let us take a more exact View of the present Custom of chusing Wives by their Weight, and the Consequences of it, and then consider a little what would be the necessary Effects of a contrary Practice, that by setting the One against the Other, we may the better discern their several Differences and Defects, make a true Comparison between them, and give the just Preference where it is best deserved. Now the first Question in the Choice of a Wonian is, What has she? Or, What Fortune will her Friends give her? If that be well answered, there is hardly any other made; but

and CORINNA.

101

presently the Lady is admired and adored with all the Extravagance of Passion. She is as beautiful as Venus, tho' her Glass would tell her the contrary, if she did not look with her own Eyes. She is ingenious, good-humoured, virtuous, wellbred, and in short, she wants no natural Charms, nor acquired Accomplishments, till she is flattered into Marriage with a Man who cares for nothing but her Wealth; and in less Time than the Courtship lasted, she has good Luck, if she be not slighted, despised, and abused. Nay, so little is the Woman valued, let her Fortune be ever so great, that among Men, who have not the worst Reputation for Immorality in the World, if it be asked, How they can marry a Woman for her Money whom they cannot love? It is presently answered, If she has an Estate to maintain me as a Gentleman, I care for no more, I can have Women enough that I like.

But there is another Method less generous, yet more frequent than the former. There are a Sort of People in the World, commonly of the Feminine Gender, called Match-makers, tho' they do not care to own the Name. These make it their Business to pry into the Domestic Concerns of others, that no Way belong to them, and having got Intelligence of young Women's Fortunes, they endeavour, if possible, to get some Acquaintance with their Persons also; and by this means, having Gudgeons enough always ready to bite at the Silver Hook, they contrive, and too often confummate unhappy Matches, to the utter Ruin of both Parties. The Man expects a Fortune, but

102 LETTERS between PYLADES

but like Ixion grasp a Cloud, and rages at his Disappointment. The Woman is equally incensed at the like Delusion in her Hopes of a great Estate; and if they do not part by mutual Consent, they live a very uncomfortable Life together. Sometimes an innocent Virgin, unacquainted with the wicked Ways of the World, is sold to a profligate Rake; and sometimes the Man is made a Fool of, and enslaved to a Wife, who instead of Money brings all Sorts of evil Qualities. But suppose there be no great Deceit on either Side, Mrs. Letter-Carrier will be sure to make a good Bargain for her self, and be extravagantly paid on both Hands; by the Man for procuring him so rich a Wise, and by the Woman for helping her to so good a Husband. That this scandalous Practice is oftner used than disclosed, our Courts of Judicature will not suffer us to doubt, since sometimes upon Discovery of the Cheat, and ambidextrous Compact, Mrs. Match-maker is denied her Gratuity (as she calls it) and put to her Relief at Law.

But the Consequences of marrying meerly for a Fortune, are much worse than the Means generally taken to get one. From this Original proceed the many fatal Accidents, which have attended the stealing of Heireffes. Upon this Account we have in our own Age beheld a very Tragical Scene, which occasioned the making a cruel A&C. (as the Fortune-Catchers term it) That this Kind of Theft should also be Felony, and which might have been wholly superfeded by such a mild and wholsome Law as this would be, Under this

and CORINNA.

103

this Head may be ranked all those Cheats and Impostures, which are very often put upon the World by Women's setting up for great Fortunes, whose Reputations perhap among their Acquaintance are gone as well their Money; and the same Deceit is as commonly returned upon them by the Men, who pretend to have great Estates, when it is well if they are not worse than nothing.

However, by this means they frequently decoy one another into Marriage, to the utter Ruin of many a private Family, and the great Detriment of the Public. And it may not be wholly superfluous to mention the fraudulent Compacts, and clandestine Agreements, which are oftentimes entered into upon making of Jointures and Marriage-Settlements. These, and on the other Hand, all the Shifts and Contrivances to detain or diminish Daughter's Portions would be entirely prevented, and the antient and well-grounded Law of Thirds would recover its primitive Use and Reputation again. But farther yet, when the Wives Money made the Match, the Coldness and Indifference which the Husband must necessarily shew towards her (for Passion will not long be disfembled) quickly heightens into Discord and Distention. The House is torn into Pieces by intestine Divisions, and destroyed by those who should support it. The Man is uneasy at Home, and therefore spends his Time and his Money abroad, in the Purfit of expensive Pleasures, and Violation of the nuptial Bed: The Women plead Precedent, and take the same

104

4 LETTERS between PYLADES

Licenle, which in Time comes to this Conclusion that they have no Home left to go to. From this Cause we may without Injustice deduce the many infamous Separations and Divorces which are so frequent among us. Infamous I call them, not because they are wrongfully obtained, but because it is a horrid Shame there should be such Occasions for them in a Christian Country, and are too convincing a Proof of the Growth of Religion, and the general Corruption of Morals.

There is another Inconvenience attends this pernicious Practice of marrying for Money; which is, the preposterous Conjunction of the Head and the Tail of the World together. The Duke and the Draper's Daughter, the Dutchess and the Debauchee, the Cast-off Countefs and the Broken 'Squire, which, if it had no other ill Effects, is the ready Way to confound all the useful Distinctions, which Reason began, and Custom has confirmed amongst Mankind, and entirely to abolish Nobility, or at least deprive it of all its antient hereditary Honour and Dignity. But this is not all, where the Parties are so unequal, the Contempt from One, and Resentment from the Other, are the fruitful Parents of more Mischief and Trouble than any Body can imagine, but such as have unhappily bore their Share of them; and the disagreeable Affinity, which is hereby contracted, is thought Dishonourable on one Side, and is often found Burthensome and Oppressive on the other. For which, and other good Reasons, no less than Two of the Seven Sages made it their particular Advice to Match Equal.

No.

and CORINNA.

105

Nor does the Evil terminate here, but is often derived to late Posterity, for when that kindly Warmth and sincere Affection is wanting, which is a necessary Ingredient in the Composition of matrimonial Bliss; when the Duties of the Bed are performed with Indifference, or perhaps with Reluctance from one Party, it is not to be wondered if the Imperfections of the Cause are transmitted to the Effect; for as Men Sow they will Reap, and it is true in a Natural, as well as Logical Sense, that the Conclusion always follow the weaker of the Premises. But let Naturalists pursue this Argument to the utmost, and I doubt not but the Result of their Enquiries will be this, That most of our Diseases are owing to the Distempers or Disaffections of our Parents. If they only were afflicted for their own Faults, the Matter would not deserve to be so much lamented, but when they entail Misery upon their Offspring to many Generations; when innocent, but unhappy Infants shall suffer not only in their States, but their Bodies too, for the Vices and Follies of their Progenitors. Methinks, meer City and Good-nature should make us forward to seek some effectual Redress, if any such might be found. And sure it might be found, if Men were once restrained from that fordid, mercenary, ungenerous Practice of marrying any thing or Money, and turning a divine Ordinance into the vilest kind of Merchandise.

But now let us survey the bright Side of the object, and look upon Matrimony divested of its ark Veil, free from all unnatural Blemishes, and

106 LETTERS between PYLADES

and arrayed in its Primitive Purity, and genuine Lustre. And under this Character it will admit of no other Motive or Inducement, but generous Love; Love the most noble, the most delightful, and most divine Passion of the Mind; the very Soul of Life, and Cement, that not only unites, but animates the happy Pair. Where that reigns, ineffable Pleasures surround them in the Day, and rest upon them at Night. They wake to new Joys in the Morning, too great to be impaired by the common Accidents and Misfortunes of Life, and like the glorious Light of the Sun, not to be darkened with every flying Cloud. They who become one Mind, as well as one Flesh, upon this proper and rational Incitement, will seldom or never fail of attaining the true and excellent End of Matrimony in a vigorous and healthy Off-spring, which may be able to go through all the Labours of Life with Courage and Credit, which in their tender Years will be the greatest Delight to their Parents, and when grown up, the most solid Comfort and Support of their Age. While those sordid Wretches who have no Passion for any thing but Money and think no Face beautiful but their adored Idea impressed upon Coin, are deservedly punished with Sterility, or what is worse, a puny fickle Race, fit for nothing here but to maintain Quail and Apothecaries, the Grief and Burthen of the Progenitors when living, and their Disparagement when dead. For the many unhappily born, the Crazy and the Crooked are generally the Products of distorted Inclinations, and are more

and CORINNA.

debted for their Generation to frigid Interest than genial Affection. But if the generous Principle of Love and Honour were universally established among Men, they would sure be as careful in their own Breed, as in that of their Cattle, and other Creatures, and in the Choice of a Female Consort would principally take Care of her being well qualified in all Respects for the great End of their Creation. And because under this Consideration Mental Endowments would among all rational Persons be accounted very momentous and estimable, therefore every Man would be disposed to like and elect such as (according to his Notions and Opinion of Things) he thought most excellent and agreeable. The Honourable would esteem good Understanding and Breeding, untainted Vertue, and a Descent from noble Ancestors. The Learned would be apt to admire Wit, Ingenuity, Knowledge, and Discretion. And the Gentleman would value agreeable Humour, good Education, and genteel behaviour. Nor would the good Housewife be reprived of her just Worth. And Beauty, the particular Gift of Heaven, and uncontested Prerative of English Women, would not want its due admiration. In short, Birth and Beauty, Wit and Understanding, Good-humour and Breeding, Knowledge and Virtue would be reckoned the chief Excellencies of the Fair Sex, and for which they ought in Reason, and would then in Fact be most esteemed. Men would then chuse such as were most like themselves in Constitution of body, and Temper of Mind, such as had the same

107

108

58 LETTERS between PYLADES

same Desires and Aversions, which are the strongest Cement, and most inviolable Bond of Love and Friendship. At least there would never be that vast Disparity of Age, which is too often bound together in Hymen's cruel Chains.

And here I cannot but observe, how common, yet how unspeakably miserable is the Condition of those young Women, who by covetous and narrow-spirited Parents are compelled to marry some wretched old Hunks, who has nothing to make him tolerable but his Dirt and his Dross. This indeed is a worse than Mezentian-Cruelty, to conjoin blooming Youth and withered Age, verdant Spring, and hoary Winter, vigorous Health and Life, with Impotence, Infirmity, and Death. What greater Torment! than for a charming Virgin to be condemned to such loathed Embraces, to be banished from all the suitable Enjoyments of Life, to be made a Nurse instead of a Bride, and to have no Hopes left, but in the last Remedy of all Diseases. To be forced into such a State, wherein she not only loses herself, but the Public loses a Person well qualified for Propagation, for the Care of Children, and Domestic Concerns; and for all the Offices of a good Woman, and a virtuous Wife. Yet this is the deplorable Fate of too many, which it is to be hoped might in great Measure be prevented, if Money were once deprived of its unjust, but extensive Power and Magic Force in making of Matches, and the marrying purely for Riches among the Men, could be no longer used as a Precedent by the Women who having been taught and accustomed not to

and CORINNA.

prize themselves for their own Wealth, it is to be presumed would not be so apt to value Men on the same inconsiderable Account. Nor would there be any Room left for the vain Expectation of burying the old Husband, and marrying again to great Advantage with a mighty Jointure, since Widows also should bring no Fortune to a second Husband, but all their Estate should upon their Marriage descend to the next Taker in Law.

But there are several other collateral Advantages of such an Institution, too many to be foreseen or enumerated, some of which that are of more public Influence perhaps it may not be impertinent to mention. It is to be hoped, that Fathers would not so frequently live beyond their Means, and inflave their Estates, leaving their Heirs to fetch Fortunes to redeem them. However, Sons could not have this Excuse for Extravagance, and making deep Wounds in their Patrimony. That they might easily heal all again with a Wife's sanative Portion. An old Miser, who had only Daughters of his own Blood to inherit his ill-gotten Wealth would not have that Temptation or plausible Pretence to make himself miserable all his Life, that he might render his Children the more unfortunate by their excessive Portions after his Death. Nor could our Women marry into foreign Countries, and carry away vast Sums with them, to the great Loss and Injury of the Nation. But these are little Things in Comparison of the immense Advantages which might be reaped from such an Institution; an Institution

109

of

110 LETTERS between PYLADES

of such universal Influence upon the whole Course of Human Life, as must necessarily introduce a new, and more beautiful Face of Affairs into the World, and would go perhaps the farther of any one Thing toward the reducing Mankind to its primitive Purity, Simplicity, and Innocence. It is to be hoped we should by this Means re-enjoy the Blessings of the Golden Age, and as far as possible recover lost Paradise again, by making Matrimony quite otherwise from what it is generally at present, but what it was in its first Appointment designed to be, The happiest State on Earth. Since all these Advantages, Natural, Moral, and Political, would undoubtedly be the Consequence of marrying for generous Love, and the Cure of all the Evils, which attend the present Practice; how can this Inquisitive, this Virtuous, this Reforming Age any longer endure the Tyranny of a Custom so contrary to Nature, to Reason, and to Religion? How can we, who are jealous of our Liberty in other Things, be so tamely wedded to Servitude in this, where it is so very much for our Happiness to be free?

But here I expect a whole Army of Objections ready to rise up against me, more numerous than formidable, and which, tho' never so weak, may be apt to boast of their Power if not answered and subdued.

It may perhaps be alledged, that Men may now if they please pursue their own Inclinations and Marry for downright Love, without any Regard to Money ; and therefore there is no need of any Alteration.

and CORINNA.

It is true indeed they may do so, but then according to the common Course of Things, what must be the Consequence of it? They must in all Probability plunge themselves into unavoidable and unpitied Poverty, than which nothing is more grievous to a generous Mind. For if it be a younger Brother who marries thus, it is commonly one Part of his Business and Hopes to make his Fortune by a Wife; since all that can be spared from the Heir, or laid up out of the Father's Estate, is little enough to put off the Daughters, and he can expect nothing but his Education, and some small Matter to set up with it. This then in him must be a fatal slumbling at the Threshold, and throwing away all his Hopes at the first Cast, which we must not suppose these Men of Fortune will do, especially since they are apt to arrogate to themselves all the Wit of the Family, tho' their Superiors in Birth have the Estate. But the Case is much worse with an elder Brother, if he makes such a false Step, and commits such an irretrievable Error. The Estate, which should have been his, if he had brought a Fortune to be divided among his Sisters, must be sold to raise their Portions, or what is worse, loaded with the heavy Burden of corroding Mortgage, which he, unhappy Man, can never expect to discharge; for having been bred above the sordid Arts of Gain, he is a very unequal Match for the Men of Business, the Harpies of the World, and therefore can have but flender Hopes of recovering himself among them. His necessary Expences daily increase, and his

111

112

LETTERS between PYLADES

his Means of supplying them continually diminish, till at length looking round on all Sides, and finding no Comfort, he either links under his insupportable Misery, or takes some desperate unwarrantable Course to deliver himself out of it. How unfortunate then is this Man's Condition, who must either marry for Money, perhaps with little or no Inclination, or else must endure all the Extremities of Want. All therefore that I plead for, is, that we may be delivered from this Slavery, that we may have no Force put upon us, but being left to our Native Liberty, may have no Discouragements, however, if we have no Advantage, by following our Inclinations in the Choice of our most intimate Companions for Life.

If any Man out of a presumptuous Advancement of Prerogative should go so far as to deny Women Sense (as some have done Souls) $^{*}$ and say they are not fit to chuse for themselves, and therefore ought not to be left so much to their own Liberty, as they would be, if they had no Expectation of Portions from their Governors. I cannot think such a downright Assertion, without any Manner of Reason for it (which is only the Argument of a bad Cause, and a weak Advocate) will find any Abettors; nor does it follow, that however qualified Women may be to make their own Elections, they would be less apt to consult their understanding Friends about the Disposal of themselves; but in all Likelihood rather more. For it is rightly observed, that where People are left to their own Government, after
