and CORINNA.

113

they come to Years of Discretion, they are generally more cautious, especially in Matters of Moment, than when they are ruled and directed by those, who may be supposed to have more Reason, but oftentimes have less; and it must be allowed that every Body is best Judge for themselves in Cases of Good or Evil, that to nearly concern them.

If on the other Hand, out of a mistaken Defence of the Fair-Sex, any one should say, the Women's Condition would be a great deal worse than it is now, if such an Act took Place; I cannot see wherein. For so long as they continued single, they would live in all Respects as well as they do now; and when they marry, their Husbands, who must in this Case be necessarily supposed to have a real Kindness for them, would undoubtedly maintain them in such a Manner as was most for their Reputation, and most suitable to their Estate and Quality. All the Injury therefore that Women would suffer by it is this, that it would not then be in their Power to throw away themselves, and their Money too, upon Men who cared only for the latter; and having spent that, grow uneasy and negligent, if not abusive, towards the unfortunate Wife who brought it.

If the Men of Morals should be afraid that this would make Women out of a covetous and libidinous Desire resolve to keep their Estates, tho' at the Loss of their Honour; they may please to consider, that almost all those, who after a mercenary odious Manner prostitute their Bodies, Vol. II. I to

## 114 LETTERS between PYLADES

to the infinite Hazard of their Souls, are such as having been bred gentilely, but wanting Portions to invite Men of suitable Birth and Education to marry them, are seduced to sell themselves for a scandalous Maintenance; or such as having some Advantages of Nature, but none of Fortune, are tempted to supply their Indigence at the Expence of their Chastity. These, to mention no more, might have fairer Hopes and a better Prospect in an honest Way; and therefore would not be so liable to be overcome by any unlawful Sollicitation. Nor would Reputation, the brightest Ornament of the Fair-Sex, be so lightly regarded, which now, tho' violated, they are too apt to think they may be soldered up again with a little Money; and she who has enough of That, imagines she can want nothing else. But then there would be no redeeming the lost Jewel, no Equivalent in the Place of it, which would certainly awaken their Caution and Diligence to preserve it always clear and unblemished.

But methinks I hear Younger-Brothers in a general Complaint, that this would be a great Hardship on them, who are Men of Fortune, and must endeavour to get Wealth and a Wife together, and if deprived of that Means of advancing themselves may be forced to take more unwarrantable Courses. This may seem to carry some Weight with a partial Observer of Things; but if the Moiety of those prodigious Sums, which are given with Sisters in Marriage, were divided amongst Younger-Brothers for their Settlement in the World, I think their Condition would be much

and CORINNA.

115

much more eligible than it is now; for then they would have a sufficient Fortune of their own, without being forced to marry against their Inclinations to obtain one; and sure it is more desirable to possess an Estate, and the Liberty of pleasing one's self in a Wife, than to run the Hazard and take the Pains of getting both together.

If it should be whispered, that many Men whose Estates are incumbered would lose a very compendious Way of releasing them, and that many Families of Worth and Fame would be quite extinguished for Want of a Wife's Fortune to support them. It may be aloud replied, Let every one suffer according to his own Demerit; and if Men will spend their Estates in Vice and Extravagance, it is fit they should endure the just Consequences of their Folly, and highly unreasonable, that a Woman's Fortune should be paid away as the Wages of another's Sin, and only afford new Fuel for a fresh Course of Luxury; and Intemperance. If it should be farther urged, that Fathers often leave their Sons clogged with Debts, which they should never have contracted themselves, and that this is the easiest Way of discharging them. It must be answered, their Case will admit of no Exception, if it does of any Pity; for it is consonant with the Justice both of Divine and Human Laws, that Children should bear their Father's Iniquities.

But I waste Time in removing Straws. And who at length can be against this Propofal? Not the Ladies; since it secures them of real Affection in their Admirers, delivers them from all

1 2

116 LETTERS between PYLADES

false Pretences, and puts it in their Power to make themselves happy with the Man, that loves them, and they also love. Not the Gentlemen; since it would restore them to the Liberty of making their own Choice without Constraint, and bring their Felicity almost to their own Disposal. Not the Nobles; since it would augment and establish their Honour. Not the Plebeians; since it would promote their domestic Tranquility and Peace. Not the Rich; since it would not rob them of their Wealth. Not the Poor; since they would not be injured by it. Not the Married; since their Lot is already cast, and they ought to give others fair Play. Not the Unmarried; since they would above all others receive the greatest Benefit by it. In a Word, since all Ranks and Degrees of Men, from the highest to the lowest, must in Reason approve such an Act as is herein proposed, what should hinder the collective Wisdom of the Nation from taking it into their serious Consideration? Why should a Matter so manifestly conducive to the public Welfare and Happiness of these Kingdoms be any longer neglected by those who watch over them for their Good, and deservedly reckon themselves, not only their Representatives, but their Guardians, Patrons, and greatest Benefactors. But if after all that has, or can be said, to evince the Reasonableness of such a Statute, it should be thought a Subject too mean and trivial for the Counsels of such an August-Assembly, or if after a thorough Debate, and due Examination, the inveterate Custom, Of giving For-

and CORINNA.

117

Fortunes with Women in Marriage, should be confirmed, and permitted still to prevail against all Opposition; there can be no other Reason assigned, I think, for such a Procedure, but that we are wiser than the most learned and flourishing People, which have in any Age of the World been recorded in History, the Greeks and Romans, or else (what I hope will never be justly objected to an English Parliament) that we are not yet arrived to so high a Pitch of Prudence or Probity.

Can teach me how to ask, and give me Happiness.

And lo! I see the winged Messenger,

And is at once both Here and There.

O! who can tell his Charms, his ev'ry Grace,

What pleasing Smiles, what Love shines in his Face!

With how great Joy he condescends

To the mean Office of a Guide,

As willingly his Counsel lends,

Which from his Lips thus pierc'd my ravish'd Ear.

If void of Danger, far from Noise and Strife,

## II.

Grant me, propitious Heav'n, a Rural Seat,

Far from the Noise and Vices of the Town,

Whose very Air is now contagious grown.

Let it not be magnificently great,

Splendid, and gay,

T' invite each idle Traveller that Way;

But cleanly, wholesome, neat;

Let all its Beauty be

Convenient Uniformity.

And grant me such a competent Estate,

As may, like Manna, my few Wants supply, Without Defect or Superfluity;

Without Defect or Superfluity;

Not the vast Treasures of the miserable Great,

Which make their Owners sweat,

With Fear to lose, or Care to get.

(Nature is satisfy'd with little Store,

All that's above belongs to Friends or Poor.)

Let me be from low worldly Cares exempt,

Rich in the Golden Mean,

Below dire Envy, and above Contempt.

May next my House a Garden lie,

Not curiously contriv'd to feast alone the Eye.

Let it do that, and other Things beside;

Let it each Sense with proper Objects please;

May Touch,and Taste, and Smell,and Sight,

Find here unpurchas'd genuine Delight;

While it displays all vegetable Nature's Pride,

From humble Grass, to lofty domineering Trees.

## III.

A few good Books to be well read,

The choice of the Living and the Dead,

Are next in my Desire;

For in this solitary State

I most of all my Mind would cultivate,

And not be said to sleep, as well as to retire.

In useful Knowledge I would still improve,

And like th' unweary'd Sun,

Inceffantly Art's glorious Circle run;

By Contemplation and Seraphic Love

Exalted to the blissful Realms above.

A Study should both feed and please my Mind;

Here ev'ry vulgar Earth-sprung Thought,

Here ev'ry Drug, by Senses brought,

By holy Chymistry should be refin'd

Into Celestial Food; and with the Bee,

To teach me Labour and Frugality)

In this secure Retreat Contented live and die;

Nor stoop so low as to be Great,

Nor sell, for Toys, thy Birthright Liberty.

Down this calm Stream the Pinnace fails with Ease,

Not tofs'd above the Skies with raging Seas,

Thence to be cast with dreadful Ruin down again,

Into the wide devouring Bowels of the Main.

Let the best Pilot Reason guide Thee on,

That when thy Course is run,

And Life's uncertain Voyage done,

Like a well-treated Ghost thou may'ft depart,

With a contented Mind, and chearful Heart;

And looking back on past Felicity,

Enjoy it o'er again; and now prepar'd to die,

Launch without Fear, into the boundless Ocean of Eternity. Here best thoul't learn, what's fittest to be known, God, and thy Self, and thy Religion; And

CORINNA's Complaint to PYLADES. Occasioned by her receiving a Letter signed Nemo.

DID I for sordid Gain attempt to Write,

Or in a popular Applause delight;

These envious Creatures wou'd have some Pretence

To whet their Weapons in their Own Defence:

With Justice, then, they might proclaim a War,

And dart their poison'd Arrows from afar.

But I, who ne'er to Laurels did aspire,

Ne'er wish'd for Fame, or wrote a Line for Hire,

Still from those vulgar Ends my Soul was free,

Nor aught desir'd beyond Obscurity.

Of Me, methinks they need not be afraid,

Me! who will ne'er usurp their Rhiming Trade.

Yet O, my worthy Friend, in Notes Obscure

These Birds of Night on Me their Fury pour;

From gross Untruths, they false Conclusions draw;

And say, I pillage Books, I never saw.

## To CORINNA.

M ADAM,

Windsor-Castle.

HAVING been in constant waiting ever since my Return hither, I could not perform my Promise sooner; and to confess the Truth, it is more in Obedience to your Commands that I do it now, than any Opinion I have of the Theory in Debate between us.

* These are the LETTERS hinted at by PYLADES, in Page 57, ut sugra, with which, he complains, CORINNA favoured the Stoic.

K 2

What

## 132 LETTERS between PYLADES

What occurs to my Memory of the many Particulars in that Book (which I thought deserved Reflection) are chiefly these Three: "1. That "the Author denies us to love Bodies. 2. That "Women are incapable of this fine Love. And "lastly, That it can only be between Two." How ridiculous do these Propositions appear when we consider the wonderful Works of God; in the Beauty, Usefulness, and Ornament of the whole visible Creation, both animate and innamate? All which is but Matter differently modified, so necessary to the Continuation of the World, and Mankind in general; and yet we are forbid to love, or take delight in them. The Psalmist cries out, for all the Creation to praise God; what meaning has it, but that we should be extaied with the stupendous Texture and Beauty of it? I say Beauty, there being many Things which perhaps might be every whit as useful, were they less beautiful, had not God designed we should love them: For Instance, what signifies that extraordinary Prettiness we see in some Person's Eyes? Perhaps there may be Brutes may see as well or better.

But we will suppose the Motives which induced our Author to write after this Manner, were drawn from his observing, how the Generality of the World are led away by material Objects, contrary to that divine, immutable, and indifensible Order, which ought to be observed by all intelligent Beings: And because some Persons who have not considered the Dignity of their own Nature, by supposing a real Good in external Ob-

and CORINNA.

133

Objects, have not only disturbed the Harmony of the Creation, but plunged themselves into many Inconveniencies; they presently exclaim against these Objects as the sole Cause, thereby raithly charging that upon God and Nature, which is meerly the Result of their own Free-will. Having gained as they think this first Point, they give a Loose to their superstitious Impertinence; "Is it so, my Brethren, do material Objects give us such Shocks, nay, trip up our Heels in the Way to Heaven? Make halte then, escape to the Mountains; it is as much as your Souls are worth to wear fine Cloaths, see a Play, go to Court, &c." It would be endless to recount all the trifling Arguments the Gentlemen of this Class bring against Objects; and I know not of one Divine (not excepting the Author of the Whole Duty of Man) that is not for the Doctrine of Flying, but not a Word of Advice how to Stand our Ground.

But by your Leave, Madam, you and I will not be faried out of this beautiful System of Matter; since we well know, that Matter neither in whole, nor part, can hurt us without our own Leave: We will give every Particular its due Value and Place, and use it just so far as shall be consistent with the Dignity of our Nature, and the Honour of God who gave it.

I could say much more on the Unreasonable nels of these Assertions, but as Writing, or Talking much, is not my Talent, give me leave at present to lay down the Rules by which I square all the Actions of my Life; and the rather, because K 3

134 LETTERS between PYLADES

cause you may see by them what my Thoughts will be on any divine, moral, or political Subject whatsoever.

(1.) That the Standard of Perfection is God; Wisdom, Truth, Reason, Order, I mean the fame with God.

(2.) That as Man, Woman, Brute, Vegetable, or any other Thing approaches this charming Standard, just so far, and no farther, we ought to value it.

(3.) That nothing is really good or evil but what is essentially so; I mean, that whatever depends on Opinion, or Time, is but little more than indifferent.

(4.) That whatever any one doth, he doth it to himself, (i.e.) a Man can do himself good or evil, but neither to another.

You see, Madam, I have discovered my Laws of Thought; and you may like or dislike as you please, but give me Leave to tell you, that if you allow of them, I expect you should exactly observe them, without the least Dispensation. However, that you may not accuse me hereafter, I will tell you the Consequence of these Rules in Practice; for as to the Theory they sound well enough, and the Divines themselves often talk, but never practice them: They will say you are unnatural, disobedient, uncivil, whimsical, proud, foolish, cowardly, and perhaps mad; and tho' it is strange, that Men should conclude thus from blind Principles, yet it is Matter of Fact, and that not only by the Unlearned

and CORINNA.

learned and Ignorant, but by People of all Tenets and Professions that are reckoned otherwife.

I hope, Madam, you will pardon my not waiting on you when last in Town, having but three Days, and then to return with the Queen, which kept me in a constant Hurry; you may easily believe, I will do my self that Favour as soon as I can, and would you honour me with such pretty Letters, as you do Lady Chudleigh, it would be a great Addition to the happy Retirement I enjoy at Court. Have the Bounty to render my Service acceptable to your Mother and Grandmother, and believe me to be,

neither Leifure nor Inclination to think of any thing else.

The little Acquaintance I have with the Subject of that Book, ought to forbid my answering your philosophical Reflections: But as to the Heroic Virtue you propose, I still take Leave to different from your Opinion; since daily Experience assures us, that every one is not qualified for a Hero in Morality. Our Saviour, who condescended to take Human Nature upon him, to bear our Infirmities, and to suffer Temptation, was certainly best able to judge for us; yet is he so far from encouraging us to rely on our own Strength, that he commands us to pray against being led into Temptation. Shall we then, in Opposition to the Divine Wisdom, and on purpose to make a Parade of our own Fortitude, wilfully thrust our selves into it? I pretend not to dogmatize, what I say is always with Submission to better Judgments, but I would gladly be informed, whether this Opinion of our Sufficiency, this acting against a positive Precept, has not some Resemblance with the Sin of our first Parents; and whether it is not often attended with a sad Conviction of our own Weakness.

Mistake me not, Sir, I am not for turning Ascetic neither; what I mean is, that we should enjoy such innocent Diversions as fall in our way, without making them the Business of Life, and that we should not thrust our selves into needless Temptations, for the sake only of overcoming them. I know you will call this a Virtue of the lowest Class, and say it deserves not

and CORINNA.

137

the Reward of an heroic Boldness : I grant your Affertion, and yet shall think my self sufficiently happy, if I can believe my self as I ought, in this mean, this unambitious Station.

The Communication of your excellent Rules is very generous; and since you permit me the Freedom to like or dislike, I shall make use of the given Liberty, by telling you my Thoughts of those Articles which I can least affent to.

(1.) I agree with you, that the Standard of Divine Perfection is undoubtedly God, but the Standard of Human Perfection I take to be Jesus CHRIST, whom, as God-Man, we may lawfully imitate; but how far we may presume to approach Divine Perfection, is referred to a farther Explanation?

(2.)

(3.) I agree with you; that common Opinion is, for the most part, common Error; but by Esfence, I understand Being: The Question therefore is, whether the Term Essential Evil, does not of Consequence lead us into the Error of the Manichees.

(4.) As Experience has taught me, that I have received Benefits from some, and Injuries from others; so I cannot help thinking them (as Circumstances have occurred) to be occasionally good or evil; to do good, and to communicate, is what we are commanded, by sacred Writ, which being dictated by the Spirit of Truth, would never have enjoined an Impossibility; and if it is in our Power to do good to another, it is certainly in our Power to do evil to him also. I assure

fure you, I have no Notion of this fine-spun, stoical Cobweb; and shall therefore say no more about nothing: I am with due Respect,

## The CAPTAIN'S REPLY.

## MADAM,

HAVING partly forgot what I wrote in my last, I must for this Time be content to guess at it by the Hints of your Letter, tho' for the future, I shall take a Memorandum, since I write to so exact a Critic.

If, by a Hero in Morality, you mean (as I sup pose) a Person who endeavours the Dignity of his Nature, I cannot compliment your Opinion it being mine, that every Body is qualified for it even Ideots, or else they could not be chargeable with Sin and Punishment. When we expect the Action of any created Being, whether animate or inanimate, we consult their Nature (that is the Experience we have of it, and not look for Grapes on Thorns, or Figs from Thistles. But the chief Cause why we take up with this mean Opinion of ourselves (for we are wonderfully humble sometimes) is, that Example and Custon

and CORINNA.

139

have begot Habits, which plead strongly for the Body, and renders a Conformity to the Divine Pattern irksome to Human Nature.

Others with you, Madam, if I take you right, imagine we rely upon our own Strength and self-sufficiency. I have met with a great deal at this in my Time, to the Ruin of many a good Pudding, but could never find Cause to suppose they had any regular Idea of the Matter: For can it be imagined, that a finite Being, who lives and moves, and is that Being in and unto an infinite, eternal Being, can think he does my Thing good, or indeed evil? For, by Virue of established Laws, before Sin entred the World, God obliged himself to impower him to act by the Motion of his Will, purely of himself.

You say, we are commanded to pray we may not be led into Temptation; very true, which thus paraphrase: "Grant me such Strength of Soul, such bright Views of whatever may happen, that I may not call Evil Good, and Good Evil; but, without Surprize, may justly conclude, how far they are the one or the other, and act accordingly." But we are by no Means to desire, or expect that no Temptation should occur; for then, God must alter the course of natural Causes continually, for every whose Prayers are heard, which would be not only inconsistent with the Wisdom of the FIAT, but very often impossible; and besides, at which is a Temptation to one, and consequently an Evil, may, in many respects, be a Good

140

140 LETTERS between PYLADES

Good to another; nor is the diverting of natural Causes in this Case necessary, there being no Danger we shall commit Evil, if we clearly know it to be so. And it is certain that Matter, whether in whole, or in part, in this Figure, or that, is, in a strict Sense, neither Good nor Evil: But as it is capable of any Figure, so it may occasionally be either Good or Evil. I must own my self at a Loss to distinguish between a Person guilty of an Evil, and one who would have been guilty, had he not been prevented (i.e.) a Murderer, and one who died in going to be one.

The great Mischief the Doctrine of flying has done in the World, evinces the Falsity of it How many of both Sexes have I seen in my short Time, bred up in the Doctrine of touch not taste not, &c. who coming to act a Part in the World, have fallen the most easy Victims to such ridiculous Temptations, as the greatest Debauchee would not have been moved with?

Pray shew me the Text, where I am denied diverting (I had almost said physicing my Machine) since material Objects may many Way be occasional Goods, both to Soul and Body and repeated Experience has convinced me, that Music will raise my Spirits, and invigorate my Blood, more than the richest Cordial in the World. How is this like the Sin of our first Parents? It was not the Beauty of the Fruit tempted your Granny (that was a Folly inconsistent with their perfect State) no, Madam, it was Pride damne

and CORINNA.

141

damned Pride, which made her strive to be independent of her Maker!

As for my own part, I never thought any Thing a Temptation but Woman, and the plain Truth is (tho' perhaps better omitted) it is rather the Idea of my Mistress, that I love, than any Excellence she is possessed of. I never yet had the Happiness to know one Person that could bear a strict Examination, perhaps never shall, unless you are pleased to discover your self.

In short, Madam, there are few material Objects but what one may see through at first Sight, and the hardest Pieces of Nature, Man and Woman, if (instead of Opinion and Sense) we judge by right Reason, of what they say and do, that is, if we conclude that a Person who can be guilty of the least base Action, is also capable of the greatest, if it falls in their Way, there would be no more need of the Philosopher's Project, of making Windows in our Breasts, than there is for our being deceived without it. And I would never desire a better Knowledge of any Person's Principles, than I could give my self at first Sight, provided they would discourse with Freedom but one half Hour. And would your discerning Ladyship make use of this Criterion, there would be no Occasion for the many, and perhaps just Complaints of Flattery and injustice, with which you daily load our Sex; thereby accusing your selves also of Vanity and impertinence. I cannot be positive there is one of Your Sex fit for a Friendship, but I can, that there

142

LETTERS between PYLADES

there is one of my Own : However, to pleafe you, we will be as we are.

Your allowing the Standard of Human Perfection to be Jesus Christ, is to own with me that it is God. We having no Idea of God but by Reason, that is the Word made Flesh, we are commanded to be perfect as God is perfect yet none, I suppose, ever entertained so absurd a Thought, as to suppose we should be as perfect as God; for then it would unavoidably follow we should be equal to God, which were the most detestable Blasphemy. On the contrary, that Creature is the most perfect that arrive the nearest to the Dignity of its Nature. A Fly I think, has not the Perfection of an Horse nor a Horse, I am sure, of a Man, and yet there may be a perfect Fly, Horse, and Man; God being the Cause of all Beings, necessarily include the Perfections of all Beings in himself.

So that when I say the Standard of Perfection is God, my Meaning is, that to perfect my Nature, I must contemplate Perfection; or which is the same in God, Perfection as far as I can comprehend it; neither is it necessary I should comprehend Infinite Perfection, seeing I am a Finite circumscribed Being, and so Perfection (or if you had rather call it Infinite Perfection) does not belong to me: But if I comprehend, and practice so much as my Nature is capable of, God will look on me by the Methods Wisdom has ordained, and which are absolutely necessary, for Infinite to view with Pleasure a Finite Being as perfect Man,

1

and CORINNA.

It is true there are some People who are for mitating Perfections in God, which do not belong to their Being; as to suppose Marriage, Feasts, Public Employs, and the Diversions of Life, to be Imperfections, Things that are not in themselves evil, but are great Arguments of out Weakness. These refined Gnetlemen do not consider they argue directly against Nature; for without these Incidents, there is an End of the whole Frame of all Things, both general and particular: For Instance, A thinks himself perfecter than B, for not acting like B; tho' the actions of B, were the Cause of A's very Existence; and without which, he must soon expire. Excellent Stuff!

Upon my Life, Madam, I know not what was the Error of the Manichees, nor is it a Farming Matter to You and I. What I meant by Essential Evil, was, that which is in its own Nature in Evil. For Instance. A without Reason bruises the Face of B. A in this Case is guilty of an Essential Evil; for view the Action of A from eternity to Eternity, it is still Evil; but the pain that B suffers cannot strictly be called an Evil, seeing there was a Time that it was not, and a Time that it shall cease to be.

I am rambled hither again, $^{*}$ and shortly for Portsmouth. I am,

## M AD AM,

*i. e. To Windsor.

143

Your most humble Servant,

R. H.

144 LETTERS between PYLADES

CORINNA's Rejoinder.

SIR,

THE Death of my dear Grandmother has so entirely employed my Time and Thoughts, that I could not possibly answer your very elegant and instructive Epistle, till some Time after you left Windfor, nor could I tell where to direct, till Mr. Carrol did me the Favour of a Visit on New-Year's Day. Thus much in Apology for my Silence. But now, Sir, for your Letter.

What a pretty Hint do you give me to compliment your Faculties, when you complain of having forgot the Substance of your last, and at the same Time repeat it verbatim. But alas! how are you mistaken? I am as far from Compliment as I am from Court, and can only say you have given such Proofs of the Fidelity of your Memory, that you need never be at the Expence of a foul Copy; that, upon my Word, I am not Critic enough to deserve such Nicety, and I am too lazy to follow the Example.

By a Hero in Morality, I do not mean what you are pleased to suppose : I think every one ought to endeavour the Dignity of his Nature, and perhaps every one may be qualified for so doing. What I meant by That, which gave you such Offence, was only this, that we should not, out of a mistaken Heroism, rely on our Strength, so far as to thrust our selves into needless Temptations, for the Sake only of overcoming them; but

and CORINNA.

145

that having craved the Divine Assistance, we should in the Strength of that, boldly resist whatever Inducements to Sin we meet with, in our lawful Employments, and necessary Diversions. And I am persuaded that every sincere Christian will find occasional Tryals enough, without seeking unwarranted Dangers. The Apostle says, Let him that stands take Heed left be fall. You know the Occasion of Origen's Lapsation, and I make no Doubt but that you remember my Predecessor; Mrs. Honywood, of famous Memory, and how dear her Presumption cost her.* I could add some modern Instances of the same kind; but I think it needless, if you consider the Words of our blessed Lord, viz. When they persecute you in one City, flee to another. Now would I fain know to what Purpose was this Permission, if they were not allowed to make use of it? Life, Honour, and Riches (which perhaps are still the greatest Temptations this World affords) were offered to the Primitive Christians, if they renounced their Religion; their Motives for Perseverance and a religious Heroism, were certainly more glorious, than any we can pretend to; and yet were they not only permitted, but even commanded to fly Temptation: Does it not seem therefore, that our BLESSED SAVIOUR made this Allowance for Human Frailty, that those who were not qualified for the Crown of Martyrdom, might escape without Sins to themselves, or Offence to their stronger Brethren? and is it not as reasonable to believe, that such chosen Persons who are raised by God for public Examples, should

Vol. II. L be

- See the Postscript to this Letter.

## 146 LETTERS between the STOIC

be endued with a greater Degree of the Divine Fortitude, than those who move only in a private and common Sphere.

This, Sir, is my entire Thoughts of the Matter, and it is equal to me whether you stile me a high or low Flyer, so you will but allow that I adhere to the right Medium of Truth.

Now would I give something to taste your metaphysical Pudding, and to be informed what Ideas are most proper for so sublime a Dish. But taking it for granted the Relish cannot be amiss, when you cook the Pudding [or Argument] I will suppose it good, and take Leave to assure you, that I meant not we should pray against Temptation in general, but that we might not be overcome by it; which, as far as I perceive, is to the same Purpose with your philosophical Paraphrase; and therefore, since we agree in the main as to this Article, why should we wrangle about Words, and SPIN out a Controversy for the Sake of Opposition only.

I protest, Sir, you wrong me, if you think I would strain a Text to prevent the physicing your Machine; I know none that denies you either Diversion or Medicine, a little of the first serves my Turn, and much less of the latter. As for the Operation of Music, and material Objects being sometimes occasional Goods, I agree with you, and never condemned the Use, but the Abuse only; and as I did not attribute the Fall of our first Parents to the Beauty of the Fruit, so I have no Occasion for a Rejoinder. But am much surprized you should bid me dif-

and CORINNA.

cover myself, who am naturally so averse to all Artifice, that I have not Dissimulation enough for the common Conveniencies of Life: How ever, I should own my Obligation for the good Opinion you are pleased to express, could I perluade my self it were any other than a Court Sugar-Plumb, to sweeten your bitter Reflections on my Sex. And to return your Freedom, I will own to your self what I have often said to Lady Cudleigh, that I thought you deserved an Esteem, but surpassed my Understanding.

Your Rule for the Tryal of People may be safe, but seems not over charitable: However, since there is a Necessity of Wisdom as well as Innocence (as the World now goes) I will not contest with you for so obsolete a Virtue as Charity. But as I never was so charmed with Flattery, as to have any Occasion of Complaint against your Sex, so I beseech, nay, conjure you, by all you hold dear, even by the Charms of your invisible Mistress, do not tax me with the Vanity and Impertinence of accusing those who never offended me. Your extreme Freedom in discovering your Contempt of my Sex, I should pass over in Silence, were it less general; but to include all under one Character, seems to deviate from that immutable Justice, which you proposed for the Standard of all your Actions. However, Self-love, Sir, begets Self-opinion, which being predominant in my Sex, as well as yours; permit me to assure you, that I could name several Ladies, whom I have the Honour to know, that are perfectly qualified for the most exact FRIENDSHIP; I mean not the

147

co:n-

148

## LETTERS between the Stoic

common Social Leagues, vulgarly called by that Name, but a generous, disinterested, and inviolable Amity: I say inviolable, because, as it can never be but between Two Persons equally virtuous and well tempered, so it is next to impossible it should ever cease till Death breaks the Chain, or till the Death of those Principles on which alone it can be founded. Add to the above-named Requisites a Harmony of Opinions, and Community of Thoughts, and then by what less Title can we call it, than an Union, or rather Marriage of Souls? And how can we divide this noble Ardor on different Objects, without Injury to our better self; forgive me, if I had almost said without being guilty of mental Polygamy: For true FRIENDSHIP, like CONJUGAL LOVE, can be but between Two, and one Person can no more maintain several perfect Amities, than he can divide a lively Stream into many Channels, without weakening the Strength of its Current.

It is possible you may think me singular in my Notion of FRIENDSHIP, and that I allow too much Latitude to the World. I must own (setting aside Things sacred) I know none of a like Comprehension; for by the Monosyllable FRIEND, I understand nothing but what is good, and the greatest and only Good on this Side HEAVEN. And so great a Veneration have I for this sacred Word, that I can never hear it abused for sordid or mercenary Ends (as it too often is) without a very warm, and, I think, a just Indignation. But mistake me not, I would not be understood that we should love but One, and hate,

hate,

and CORINNA.

149

bate, or be indifferent to all the rest of the World. True FRIENDSHIP is so remote from that churlish Vice, that it creates a Tenderness in the roughest Tempers, it increases Compassion where it already is, and abundantly improves our CHARITY. CHARITY, or Love of Benevolence, (call it which you please) is certainly the most noble, most God-like Virtue, that can be practised by Human Kind. It is the most often commanded, and inculcated of any throughout the New Testament, and no where more elegantly and emphatically described than in the 13th of the Corinthians. But why do I make a Distinction in Words when there is none; the Thing FRIENDSHIP, CHARITY, and BENEVOLENCE, being all but synonymous Terms for one Virtue, and the most that can be said is, That FRIENDSHIP is a contracted CHARITY, and CHARITY a diffused FRIENDSHIP. I have Friendship for all the World in this Sense, but can admit but of one Object for an intense and perfect AMITY.

I could enlarge on this Subject till I had quite tired you, if I have not done that already, for it is what extreamly delights me, and perhaps I have pleased my self with Charms in this fine THEORY, which I may no more meet with in the PRACTICE, than the most Perfect-Woman living can compare with your Ideal Mistress.

I hope I have said enough to let you see, that, according to my System, Friendship ought not to be commenced between different Sexes (save in one Circumstance) nor can it possibly be divided into many Branches: However, if L3 you

## 150 LETTERS between the STOIC

you are not yet convinced, pray refer it to my Lady Chudleigh, and I will stand by her award. I am,

SIR,

Your most humble Servant,

CORINNA.

P. S. CORINNA having, in the foregoing Letter, mentioned the Case of her Great-Grand-Mother Mrs. Honywood; I shall here insert the Distich, in Latin and English, written upon a Venice-Glass which this Religious Gentlewoman, being in a Fit of Dispondency, threw out of her Hand, saying, as surely as this Glass breaks, I shall be Damned; but the Glass remained whole.

On my Great-Grand Mother Hony-wood's Venice-Glass, that brake not.

Vitrum, Cor tritum terris illisa fuerunt,

Illesum Vitrum & Cor tenet una Manus.

Thy Glafs, Thy Heart, were Both cast to the Ground;

One Hand preserves Thy Glafs and Heart Both found.

The

and CORINNA.

151

The CONCLUSION.

MADAM,

Received the Honour of yours, for which I humbly thank you. I am highly pleased with my self, that you approve my Maxims, and can be very easy that you conclude wrong from the Principles you allow: For surely, if as you agree, every Thing is to be loved as it partakes of the Divine Nature, there will be no Difficulty in loving as many as your Nature comprehends; and seeing a thousand Persons may be equally perfect, they ought equally to be beloved. You know very well, you can have an Idea of a far greater Perfection than you are like to meet out of your self; and yet I believe you would think your self happier than any of your Neighbours, could you find One some Degrees below it; and would, I doubt not, in Justice give him as great a Share in your Affections, as by his approaching Perfection he is capable of.

This is the Language of Truth, (Madam) which I can venture to speak only to You, and one more in the World; for all that I have ever read on this Subject deny, that a Friendship can be between more than two. I wonder at none so much as Mr. Norris, who it is probable inclined you to this Opinion, which it is impossible you can have any regular Idea of. I shall request my Lady Chudleigh's Arbitration when she comes to Town, which I hope will be very soon,

L4

152

and am highly delighted with your making Choice of so good a Judge.

I am, with Truth and Respect,

MADAM,

Your most obedient Servant, &c.

R. H.

# LETTER XXIV.

## PYLADES to CORINNA. *

It is a certain Observation, that light Passions and Troubles are Talkative, but very great ones are silent and Dumb. I am sure, no Words can express my Concern, when I promised my self the Satisfaction of hearing after a long Sulpence, that you were in a fair Way of Recovery, to be surprized with the dreadful Apprehensions of a dangerous Relapse; and to read your Last Dying Farewel, strikes me with inconsolable Grief, and almost breaks my Heart. But while Life remains there is some Hopes; perhaps God in his infinite Mercy may yet restore you.

* In Answer to my Last Farewel (as I then thought it to be) wherein I had desired him to take Care of my Mother, and to pay all my just Debts; telling him, in the said Letter, I had made my Will (which he should find in a certain Place) wherein I had bequeathed him my Library, worth above 100l. and some Manuscripts for which alone I had been offered'30l. It being all that I had of my own.

E. T.

to CORINNA.

153

you, at least all honest Endeavours ought to be used, and therefore I will write to Doctor Colebatch in your Behalf, that you may not be cast away for want of seasonable Assistance, unless I hear of your Amendment next Monday at Cirencefer, where I shall be to receive your Letter, and shall take that Opportunity of writing by the same Post. Dr. Colebatch is a very honest Man, and has quite left off the Practice of Acids, and has done Sir John Guife more good than any Body else, in a Case not much different from yours: You may depend upon him for his Fidelity and Secrecy, and therefore pray follow his Prescriptions, if you find any need of them. And in order to render the Means God has ordained, the more successful; I desire you would not link under any Pressure of Mind, or be afflicted for any Disappointments in temporal Concerns. What I said to you on that Account, was not a Feint to buoy up your Spirits, but from a moral Certainty, that in a very little Time, I shall be able to afford you, and your Mother, a sufficient Maintenance, although she should never get a Farthing again as long as she lives. You need not doubt, my dearest, only Love, but all your Requests shall be sacred to me, and shall to the utmost of my Power be punctually performed; unless it be, that I shall do more for your Mother and Creditors, and less for my self, than you have directed: But God grant I may never be put to this Tryal.

I have very little Inclination at this Time to talk of Business, for my Heart is too full of more

## LETTERS from PYLADES

more weighty Concerns; and should not do it, but that what you mention requires Hafte; and therefore if Mr. Jodrell thinks I may come up soon enough, immediately after I receive your Answer to this, and it be any Business that does not require constant Residence in Town (for that I cannot undertake) I will endeavour to come up, if you can give me any Hopes of Success.* But I can say no more of these trivial Affairs, till I hear good News of you, which God grant I may receive by next Post. Pray write an Answer to This next Tuesday, that I may have it next Thursday at Cirencester. My Spirits are sunk so low, that I can add no more, but my most servant Prayers to the Omnipotent Author and Giver of Life and Health, that he would be graciously pleased to raise you Up again, to make this Life comfortable, and to crown you with immortal Glory in the next. Adieu! my dearest, only Love, my Heart bleeds for you.

PYLADES.

## LETTER XXV.

His Refolutions to assist Her in surmounting all Her Misfortunes.

![Image 166-10](imgs/cropped_page166_idx10.jpg)

A MEER unactive Commiseration of your Sufferings (my dear Corinna) would afford you

- This relates to an Employment in the Law propofed to PYLADES

to CORINNA.

155

you but poor Comfort, under such Calamities as have lately, and do still oppress you; neither is that all the Consolation I shall administer, for if you can shift but one Fortnight longer, within that Time, I design to be in London; and if Providence shall in Mercy restore your Health, I doubt not, but it will enable me to make you easy in all other Particulars. I shall give you farther Satisfaction when we meet; at present I can say no more, but that I desire you to be very careful of your self, which is a Request that I am sure I have need to inculcate, since you so frequently neglect it; and keep somebody constantly with you, till you are better able to help your self as well as your Mother. I shall perfectly chide, when I come to Town, if you are not more careful of,

Your most faithfully affectionate,

PYLADES.

## LETTER XXVI.

## His Fears of Her Death.

THE Receipt of your two Letters, my dear CORINNA, which came both together (feeing the Superscription in your own Hand) a little revived me; but the Contents of them give me so little Hopes of your Recovery, that I am almost as disconfolate as ever. God knows what is best for us, and to his Divine Plea-

156

## LETTERS from PYLADES

Pleasure we ought at all Times contentedly to submit; but as long as there is Life, there remains Hope, and room for Supplication. Nothing troubles me so much as that Disquietude of Mind you complain of, which is certainly a great Prejudice to your Health, and a Bar to your Recovery; and which I perceive arises chiefly from the Apprehension of your being burthensome to Me. You know I told you long ago, that when once that happens, I will put a Stop to my Supplies; and therefore you need not harbour the least Uneasiness upon that Account. What I have hitherto done, I can very well bear, and I shall be able to do a great deal more, with Chearfulness, if I can but perceive you endure your Misfortunes with such Equanimity, and Resolution, as may be a Means of preserving and restoring your Health; the Want of which, will be the greatest Disappointment; and, if Matters succeed according to my Expectation, perhaps the only Obstacle to my Happiness. But no more of this at present. I shall be in Town next Month, and then I hope to find you well, and to make you easy.

PYLADES

## LETTER XXVII, Confolatory.

## To bear Her AFFLICTIONS patiently.

AS long as you complain, I must be concerned; nor is it in my Power to obey your Commands of not grieving for you: How-

## to CORINNA.

157

ever, since Expressions of Sorrow will afford you No Comfort, and me but Little, I will be silent; only imploring the Divine Author of Life and Health, to give you Consolation equal to your Troubles; to remove from you all Pain of Body, and Anxiety of Mind; and if it be his good Pleasure once more to restore you to Health, the greatest Blessing of Life.

P. S. I have not The WILL, you mention, but it is no Matter. Pray be easy and quiet as to all Concerns of that Kind: As for Disappointments, they are so common, that they ought not to be regarded; all will do well at last, if the main Article of all, your Health, can be restored, which I hope is not impossible, and for which you thall never want the Sinceré Prayers, &c. of

Your PYLADES.

## LETTER XXVIII.

## Complaining of Her Silence.

Shurdington, Dec. 20. 1712.

I Received no Letter from you this Week (my dear CORINNA) which revives a thou- land Fears in me. God grant they may be vain. Pray write next Week, and give me that Satisfaction before I go to Rendcombe; where I must accompany Sir John, $ ^ * after the Holydays are over, till he goes for London, which, I suppose,

will

* GUISE.

158

## LETTERS to CORINNA.

will be about a Fortnight; and during that Time, I shall be deprived of your Epistolary-Conversation. I wish you a happy Christmas, and as soon as I can procure some Money for you, I will; but Disappointments are so frequent, that I can promise nothing, till I have it in my Hands. Adieu! the dearest only Love, of

PYLADES.

## LETTER XXIX.

## Congratulates Her Recovery.

Cannot but rejoice exceedingly, to hear that your Health is so much recovered, and the latent Cause of your Illness so happily removed. If through God's Mercy you can at last obtain a tolerable State of Health, I hope we shall by Degrees overcome all other Difficulties; and in the Conclusion of a great many Troubles, more sensibly enjoy the succeeding Peace and Tranquility. But these Halcyon Days are yet in distant Prospect; I wish they may speedily arrive. In the mean Tinie, I beg you to be very careful of,

Your most faithfully affectionate,

PYLADES.

P. S. With this, You will receive a Copy of Mr. Trapp's Poem on Badminton House.

## A

DESCRIPTION OF THE

Magnificent and Delightful SEAT Of His GRACE

The Duke of BEAUFORT, IN

GLOUCESTERSHIRE.

BLEST Heav'nly Mind, who from thy Native Skies

Art sent to watch this blissful Paradise. Whose Care and hallow'd Presence guard and grace The Noble Somerset's Illustrious Race.

Great

160

warm,

A DES BADMINTONIANE.

Great Thou, for sure so vast a Charge as Thine,

Heav'n to no vulgar Angel would assign:

Say, did the World a lovelier Scene display,

When Nature rose all gaudy and all gay,

And smil'd upon the New-created Day,

Than these delightful Walks, this sweet Retreat,

And all the Pleasures of this happy Seat?

Say, bright Interpreter, for thou wert by

When God, from Chaos and wild Anarchy,

Mark'd out the World, with pregnant Heat made

And spoke the shapeless Embryo into Form. Thou saw'ft the Earth its Virgin Beauties wear, And in the Youth of its first Spring appear. But Thou, left I with low unequal Lays Leslen that Greatness, which I strive to praise; Do Thou exalt my Voice, inspire my Muse, And glide into my Breast,and gen'rous Heat infuse

Brighten

EDES BADMINTONIANE.

161

Brighten my Mind with thy celestial Beam;

And make my Verse as noble as my Theme.

See how the lofty Fabric shines on high,

Scorns this low Region, and affects the Sky:

Proudly it rears its spacious Top, o'erspread

With wide unfolded Sheets of pond'rous Lead;

Which its blue Surface to the Sun displays,

And with strong Force retorts his scalding Rays.

Ev'n He, as oft as He his Courfe renews,

Scarce larger Plains of solid Metal views.

When pointed Beams thro' Earth's deep Caverns pafs,

Ripen thick Beds of Oar, and form the fluggifh Mafs.

A golden Globe fix'd eminently high,

With gilded Lustre strikes the wond'ring Eye.

Shines opposite to the bright Orb of Sol,

And seems to emulate his radiant Ball.

Vol. II. M The

162 AEDES BADMINTONIANE.

The stately Pile with Front majestic stands,

Our Admiration and our Love commands.

Claims our Respect, and awes us with Delight,

And while it ravishes confounds the Sight.

Such is the beauteous Out-side, such is seen,

The Case of that bright Pomp that shines within.

Now for a nearer View, my Muse prepare,

But view with Rev'rence, and approach with

Fear,

Be curious here, survey each Room of State,

And view the glitt'ring Honours of the Great.

What Scenes of Glory open to the Eye,

Puzzling the Mind with bright Variety.

None but the God-like BEAUFORT could dispense

Such vast Profusion of Magnificence.

First see, if Sight can comprehend it all,

The wide Dimensions of this spacious Hall.

No little carv'd Embellishments disgrace

The unaffected Grandeur of the Place.

EDES BADMINTONIANE.

163

No figur'd Arras from the forming Loom;

Nor less'ning Paint effeminates the Room.

Greatness would here in Gaiety be loft,

And want of Ornament adorn it moft.

The lofty Cieling of the high-built Roof,

Looks Majesty, and awes us from above.

Here if we speak, the long continu'd Voice

Improves its Accents, and extends the Noise:

Repeating Echoes from each Side rebound,

And the Air waving, trembles into Sound.

How will gay Notes and vocal Airs conspire,

In this sonorous Room, to fan the Fire

Of gentle Love, and kindle soft Desire.

When to the youthful Hero's longing Arms,

His beauteous Bride shall yield her Virgin Charms.

When tuneful Strings shall speak the gen'ral Joy,

And num'rous Verse salute th' auspicious Day.

If from this Prospect we divert our Eyes,

New Wonders still succeed, and still surprize.

M 2

Ob-

164 ADES BADMINTONIANE.

Objects so various that they all oppress

The Soul with too redundant Pleasantness.

Expression fail us where we need it most,

And faint Description is in Wonder loft.

Too scanty are the Bounds of Verse, let those Range more licentious in unbridled Prose,

Who hither led by Fame in Flocks repair,

And scarce their Adoration can forbear;

Let them on ev'ry single Object dwell,

And to their list'ning Friends distinctly tell,

How here, rich Furniture delights the Eye,

With all the Pomp of Princely Luxury.

How sumptuous Tapeftry they here behold,

Wrought o'er with glitt'ring Threads, and stiff with Gold.

And Paint so exquisite, and so refin'd,

No Age can tarnish, nor no Pencil mend.

Figures in which so many Charms conspire,

That e'en APELLES might with Rage admire.

But

EDES BADMINTONIANE.

165

But longer are their wond'ring Eyes detain'd By the Performance of a noble Hand.

A Room, all careless Females to upbraid By its great Mistress's fam'd Art array'd:

She to express her commendable Hate,

Of resitive Greatness and unactive State ;

Studious of Needle-work her Fingers moves,

And still the Credit of the Art improves.

The various Colours of the filken Thread,

The deep-dy'd Purple, and the fprightly Red :

To the fresh Beauties of gay Flow'rs she turns,

And with whole Spring her matchless Work adorns:

Carnations here their painted Leaves disclose, And there th' Embroid'ry blushes in the Rose.

That Grecian-Dame, $^{*}$ whose celebrated Skill, Old Bards in antiquated Legends tell,

M3

Who

* PENELOPE,

166

AEDES BADMINTONIAE.

Who ply'd the Distaff while her wand'ring Lord, Regions unknown and distant Seas explor'd. Should she revive and in this gaudy Room See Work transcending her ingenious Loom; With envious Indignation she'd repine, And all Pretensions to the Art resign. Nor stops our Wonder here, the Sacred Dome, Where all at solemn Hours devoutly come, With costly Ornaments she richly decks, And soars above the Practice of her Sex: Womanish Trifles and vain Toys she scorns, And with rare Skill, Religion's self adorns. Excellent Princess! but forbear my Muse, Thy seeble Nerves so great a Task refuse: Such pious Labour, Heav'n it self shall praise, And Angels warble in immortal Lays. O would our British-Ladies learn t' aspire To such Examples, and such Praise desire;

Their

EDES BADMINTONIAN E.

167

Their Worth and Quality they would advance,

And spurn the Niceties of modish France.

All this and more the glad Spectator fees,

And treasures in his Mind the beauteous Images.

How does his Joy the vain Reward excel,

Of those who warm'd with superstitious Zeal,

Travel to visit, with devout Intent,

The Shrine of some imaginary Saint :

Before the consecrated Nothing pray,

And to some mufty Relic Homage pay.

Much wiser Pilgrims They who hither come,

And carry such august Ideas Home.

See Objects that delightfully furprize,

Engage their Wonder and deserve their Eyes:

But who that maze of Pleasure can express!

Those winding Walks and verdant Wilderness.

0 for that Heav'nly Voice, that charming Tongue,

Which blifsful Eden's flow'ry Mansions fung.

## 168 EDES BADMINTONIANE.

Let that great Spirit leave th' Elysian-Groves, 'And change for these, those Shades thro' which he roves;

Refume his Pen and with harmonious Lays Describe more Pleasures than he there enjoys. Long, even, Rows of tall and slender Trees Stand thick and tremble with the murm'ring Breeze.

In beauteous Order rang'd, they shoot on high Their spreading Tops, and screen the azure Sky: In Friendship knit th'intangling Leaves they join, And curling Branches in each other twine. Here the Variety of diff'rent Ways Returning in themselves at once amaze The Mind, and with delightful Error please. She,† who the subtile Lab'inth did explore, And trac'd those intricate Meanders o'er;

- MILTON. † ROSAMOND.

Thro'

EDES BADMINTONIANE.

169

Thro' such inextricable Paths was led

By the safe Conduct of the guiding Thread.

0 had that Royal Maid been here confin'd,

Her Crown she chearfully would have resign'd :

No Clue she would have ask'd for her Release,

But here have spent her Days in lonely Peace.

With Pleasure these smooth Walks she would have croft,

And been more happy as the more was loft.

For Nature here and her fair rival Art

Confpire to please, and all their Charms impart.

Here no luxuriant Sprouts intangling meet,

Shoot cross the Paths, or clasp about the Feet.

All even stand in regular Array,

And the strict Artist's niceest Laws obey.

Hither when SIRIUS fcalds the Hemifphere,

And scatters Fevers thro' the fultry Air.

Hither the Hero chufes to retreat,

Nor feels th' Inclementy of Summer's Heat :

When

170 ADES BADMINTONIANE.

When Sol profusely scatters radiant Streams, And thro' the Branches darts his trembling Beams;

Here warbling Birds tune their melodious Notes,

And in soft concert strain their little Throats;

And Tufts of lively Flow'rs in blooming Ranks

Adorn the Walks and fringe the verdant Banks:

Their balmy Sweets they lavishly dispence,

Perfume the ambient Air, and blefs the feafted Senfe.

Here bubling Springs thro' founding Pipes convey'd,

Emerge beneath the cool refreshing Shade:

More pleasant was no feign'd Romantic Scene,

No purling Fountain edg'd with Native Green

No fancy'd Nymph's imaginary Throne

Fenc'd round with pendant Shades and living

Stone :

No

EDES BADMINTONIANE. 171

Nor could DIANA and her Virgin-Train,

Fatigu'd with Hunting, purer Springs attain,

When near cool Grots, secure from Phaxbus' Beams,

They bath'd their beauteous Limbs and wanton'd in the Streams.

Here Waters upwards forc'd, to Heaven aspire,

Forget their Nature and ascend like Fire;

The hollow Tubes of Lead eject afar,

Long trembling Streams, and wound the passive

So Whales when weary of unactive Ease,

Difpos'd for Mirth, embroil the peaceful Seas,

And to divert themselves a Tempest raise :

nro' their long Trunks they spout the foaming

and add new Waters to the pregnant Clouds.

et Nature there exhausts not all her Store,

Where

172

A DES BADMINTONIANE.

Where all that Verdure which for Human Use, The Third-Day of CREATION did produce, Is fed with vital Warmth and genial Juice.

Not that wife Hebrew-King $^{*}$ whose piercing Wit

Search'd Nature's Ways,and brought her Works to

Who knew each Vegetable's hidden Pow'r,

And trac'd the secret Use of ev'ry Flow'r;

Nurs'd Gardens more deserving of a King:

Nor in his Royal-Walks maintain'd a nobler Spring

For here besides those Plants which from our Earth

First rose, and to this Climate owe their Birth;

E'en Foreign Springs enrich the pleasant Soil,

And both the Indies flourish in our Isle.

The various Species grow in even Rows,

Unfurl their op'ning Buds, and all their Prid disclose.

Hei

- SOLOMON.

EDES BADMINTONIANE.

173

Here no degen'rate Plants forfake their Primè, Nor lose their Nature while they change their Clime;

But spread luxuriant and their Leaves unfold, Nor mifs th' Indulgence of their Native Mold.

For She whose Soul is still by Action bleft,

Impatient of the dull Fatigue of Rest.

The artful PRINCESS who still governs here,

Studies their Tempers with experienc'd Care,

And reconciles them to this Northern-Air.

Here with Delight she spends her happy Hours, Supports each drooping Herb, and props the falling Flow'rs:

So was the first of Human Kings employ'd,

Who a calm State of finless Peace enjoy'd ;

Eer Vice their God-like Nature did debase,

And Lust and Pride debauch'd their guilty Race.

And e'en when Boreas keens his Blafts, and pours Thick Storms of driving Sleet and wintry Show'rs,

When

## 174 ADES BADMINTONIANE.

When other Herbs shrink up and disappear,

And Frost and Hail deform th'inverted Year,

E'en then exempted from the common Fate,

These lovely Greens prolong their vital Date;

The Season's sharp Inclementy they shun,

And flourish Independant of the Sun:

Cherish'd with artificial Warmth they spread

Their thriving Leaves and fragrant Odors shed.

In depth of Winter, Spring her Pride displays,

Nor for dull Nature's flow Succession stays.

O may they long their healing Force impart,

To lengthen Life and aid Apollo's Art;

That no malignant bold Disease may dare

T' approach the noble Race, or taint this Air:

Health never interrupted may they give,

And add long Life to those by whom they live.

That You, Illustrious PRINCE,\* whom Heav' has rais'd

T' adorn that Pile which we have feebly prais'd

* HENRY, late Duke of Beaufort.

EDES BADMINTONIANE.

Long Ages may amidst this Grandeur shine,

Height'ning the Lustre of the Noble Line:

May You the Praise of After-ages share

With Him whose Name and Dignity You bear,

And be his Honours and his Vertues Heir,

Who little ow'd to his high Birth and State,

And without Wealth or Title had been Great:

Whose gen'rous Mind no Limits could control,

And be his Fame unbounded as his Soul.

Such Praises to his Memory are due;

For such He was, and still survives in You.

And as You now like Him your Honour wear,

Not with a Haughty, tho' a Graceful Air;

So future Times shall in your Actions see

His Courage and unshaken Constancy;

Whose stedfast Honour did all Change disclaim,

Firm to His first Resolves and STILL THE SAME.*

* SEMPER EADEM. — Queen ANNE'S Motto.

175

Whom

176

76 AEDES BADMINTONIANE.

Whom Fortune could no greater make nor less,

Nor Good could elivate, nor Bad depress.

Long may You live, your Country to adorn,

With Princely Heirs and Heroes yet unborn.

And long may She whose Cares you yet enjoy,

Thrice happy while Her prudent Counsels sway,

Who forms your Youth with Masculine Address,

And yet with more than Female Tenderness,

Long may She live, in this delightful Place,

T' admire your growing Fame, and bless you smiling Race.

And when you Both shall late from Earth remove,

And change These Mansions Here for Those Above

No Honours shall you lose, but as of Old,

Rome's Emp'ror was with Deities inroll'd;

Commence Immortal as He put off Man,

And where the Monarch ceas'd the God began.

EDES BADMINTONIANE.

So when for Heav'n this Grandeur You shall leave,

More Grandeur in Exchange You shall receive;

A happier Seat than this You there shall find,

And wear more Glories than you left behind.

CORINNA

P Y L A D E S,

W I T H

An Account of some BOOKS.

NOW to refume the Thread of our usual Correspondence, and continue Les Nouvelles de Lettres.

Imprimis, The Book I have made you so long expect from our Britifis Virgil, Mr. Dryden, is at last published, under the Title of, FABLES Ancient and Modern, from HOMER, OVID, BOCACE and CHAUCER. You may therefore suppose it both pleasing and instructive; for you know the Value of any Thing from that illustrious Author. Sir Richard Blackmore hath Paraphrased Job and HABAKKUK, in Heroic Verse; how he has succeeded I cannot inform you; but he has been most terribly handled at Parnassus; and the Belle Esprits have unanimously declared against him. The other Day I committed an unlucky Blunder at Mr. Browne's Shop; for in-

CORINNA to PYLADES.

179

flead of asking for his Paraphrafe, I enquired for his Satire on Job, which mightily offended some Friends of Sir Richard's, that were in the Shop; though I was so far from Design, that if they had not corrected my Mistake, I had remained ignorant of it still; and so much for Maurus, the inimitable City-Bard.

The Adventures of TELEMACHUS now appear in English; they are generally said to be composed by the Arch-Bishop of Cambray, for the Use of the Duke of Burgundy; but as long as we have the Book, no Matter who is the Author. You know I am an Enemy to all Romances; This is One, and yet I would have you read it, and am satisfied your Time will not be lost.

I forgot to tell you in my last, that the Dispensary is come to a Fourth Edition, and if you have not seen it, you have missed one of the finest and most correct Pieces of Wit, that hath been printed since Absalom and Achitophel. There are several other Books lately Published; but that I might not deviate from my Text, I have mentioned none but what belong to the Muses: You know to whose Works I was indebted for my Health; and I really fancy, there is more Virtue in some Books, than a whole Cart-load of Medical-Traff. History informs us, that Alphonso the Wife, King of Arragon, having lain under a languilhing Disease a long Time, and tried many Remedies in vain, forbid the Physicians to prescribe him any more; and the better to divert his Melancholy, applied himself to the Reading of Quintus Curtius. That History gave him a plea-

N.2

## 180 CORINNA to PYLADES.

fant Amusement, and before he had read it through, was perfectly cured. Upon which, he burst out into this rapturous Expression; Fare wel HYPPOCRATES; farewel AVICENNA; and long live QUINTUS CURTIUS; the only true Physician I return you the Divine Herbert's Poems with Thanks.

On PYLADES's designed Voyage to New-York.*

O Would some pitying Pow'r enlarge my View!

And teach me what to shun, and what pursue Love! prompts me forward, thro'a foreign Way

But Tyrant Duty still commands my Stay :

Duty's a Guardian, which I must not lose;

Yet such a Lambent Flame, who can refuse?

A Love so pure! so perfect! so intense!

So truly free from all Alloys of Sense,

As e'en by dying Nuns might be confess,

And center boldly in an Angel's Breast:

* The Lord Cornbury offered PYLADES a very considerab Post, if he would attend Him to his Government of that Place.

CORINNA to PYLADES.

181

To keep this Love, I could my Life forego;

But losing it, I shall my Duty show.

Just Heav'n instruct me, what thou doft require,

And either crown, or else correct Desire.

CORINNA.

P. S. In Return for Mr. Trapp's Poem on Badminton-House, I here send you a Present, made me by Lady Chudleigh, it is the Account of Nixon, the famous Cheshire Prophet.

IN the Reign of King James the First, the Honourable Family of Cholmondley, at Vale-Royal in Cheshire, kept (according to the Custom of that Age) a Fool, his Name was Nixon; but so very a Natural, that he was not capable of speaking six Words together; Yes or No was the Top of his Eloquence; nor was he ever heard to utter even those Words, but when pressed by Hunger, Thirst, or some Natural Dictate. However, by continual Custom, and the Care of the Steward, he was made capable of Driving the Oxen at Plough; when, one Day, as he was in the Field performing this Duty with his Fellow-Servants, he let fall the Goad, and stood immoveable, with his Eyes wide open, and fixed towards Heaven; nor could all the Words, Blows, Pinching, &c. of his Fellows, fetch him out of this Trance for the Space of above an Hour; at the

N 3 End

## 182 CORINNA to PYLADES.

End of which Time, he recovered of himself, and taking up his Goad, went on with his Employ as if nothing had ailed him. The Men who were at Work with him, asked him, with great Surprize, What made him stand still? and why he had delayed them so long when to their great Astonishment, he made them a sensible Discourse of near two Hours length, without the least Hesitation: In which, he foretold the Civil Wars. The Death of King Charles the First. The Restoration of King Charles the Second. The Abdication of King James. The Revolution by King William; and many other Things, not yet come to pass. Adding, that for a Confirmation of the Truth of what he had spoke, He should be sent for by the King to London, and starved to Death. When he had finished his Speech, he returned to his former Capacity of Silence; and was never observed to have any better Understanding or Speech than he had before this happened. The Men who were at Work with him made a Report of this as a Prodigy, when they returned Home to their Master; and this Prophecy has been preserved by Tradition ever since among the Descendants of that Worthy Family, and the Country People.

It is observable, that King James the First, hearing of this Accident, had a Curiosity to see the Fellow, and sent for him to Court, giving him in Charge to One of the Officers of his Household, to provide him with necessary Lodging and Diet in his own Palace; but withal, to keep him up close, and make strict Observation

on

CORINNA to PYLADES.

183

on his Behaviour, that he might be satisfied there was no Imposture in the Matter. The Gentleman did so, but alas performed his Trust with a Severity the King never intended. For the Court removing for Ten Days to Theobalds in Hertfordshire, he was obliged to attend the King, and going in a Hurry forgot poor Nixon, who being locked up, and not having the Benefit of Speech, was, as he truly foretold, starved to Death.

There were Three other Particulars, no less remarkable than this his Prophecy.

The One was, that whenever the Wall which encompassed Vale-Royal fell down, it denoted some very great Change in the Government; and that if it were a serene Day, and the Wall fell inwards, it shewed an advantagious and happy Change; but if it were a stormy Day, and the Wall fell outwards, and any of the Stones fell into the Brook, it betokened the direct Contrary.

This Prophecy caused the successive Heirs of that Seat to keep several Workmen in Yearly-Pay to support the Wall; and once a Month to inspect it all round; so that by continual Repairing, it was buttressed both within and without, and but the Week before it fell, the Workmen had viewed it according to Custom, and given in, that it was now so strong, it might reasonably be expected to last an Hundred Years without any Repair. Yet on Trinity-Sunday, 1688, about Eleven in the Forenoon, being a calm, clear, Sun-fbiny Day, without a Breath of Wind, this Wall fell flat on the Ground within the Court, and not so much as a single Stone fell outwards. What Change was

184 PYLADES to CORINNA.

produced in the Nation, by the Transactions of that very memorable Day, is too well known to need a Repetition.

Nixon also said, that in Process of Time, there would be an Heir born to that Family, who should be of very great Parts, and arrive to greater Honour than any of his Predecessors. That the Time would come, there should be Three Competitors for the Crown of Britain; and that this Gentleman should be eminently instrumental in placing the Right Prince on the Throne, and securing a Lasting-Peace with a continued Series of Honour and Glory to the Nation. That he should be known by an Eagle which would come at the Time of his Birth, and sit at the Chamber-Window till he was born, and after some Time fly quite away.

# LETTER XXX.

## Pylades chides Corinna for neglecting Herself.

Thank you for your Literary News, &c. and for Nixon's Prophecy. But I must chide: I perceive you do not take so much Care of me (my Dear CORINNA) as I desire, and as your Indisposition does necessarily demand. Why would you eat Meat before your Stomach would bear it; and by too much Frugality retard your Recovery; and in the Conclusion, enlarge your Expences without diminishing your Pains. I have often told you; that you should never want Neceffa- ries,

PYLADES to CORINNA. 185

ries, at least as long as I am able to support you : And at present I have Ten Pounds (which I can well spare) ready to return you, as soon as you can lend me the comfortable News that you are able to receive it. And for what is past, let it not give you one uneasy Thought; for I need say no more on that Subject, but desire you to turn the Tables, and ask your self this one Question; Whether if you were in my Circumstances, and in yours, you would not do even more for me, than I have ever yet done for you? And what is such Friendship worth, which goes no farther than bare Words or Self-Interest. Pray let not the common Vicissitudes and Uncertainties of human Affairs disturb your Thoughts, and preadice your Health; for if all Things do not succeed with you according to Promise and Expectation; yet Matters are not altogether so bad with me: And, I think, I shall get more Money this year, than ever I got in one Year in my Life. Therefore pray deny yourself nothing that is convenient for your Condition, but support your spirits with as much Fortitude as you can; and say, that through the Direction and Assistance of Providence, I bid you hope for better Days in a short Time.

I am heartily sorry for the Disappointment in Mr. Lytton's Affair, after so much Pains; but more concerned for your Illness (my Dear CORINNA) and what makes me the more troubled is, that I am afraid your Illness is owing to our Disappointment. But why should you thus affect your self, and me, for common Accidents and

## 186 PYLADES to CORINNA.

and Trifles, that need not touch us unless we yield to them. You know I have assured you, that I am morally certain of supporting you, though your Mother should never get a Farthing more; and therefore, why should you be uneasy upon any Misfortune of this Kind? Is not Health the greatest Blessing in this World? and is it to be thrown away because we are deprived of a less? Pray call up your Philosophy, or at least your Religion to your Aid, upon such Occasions. And I desire you to send me down in your next, the Sum Total of all your Debts; for in less than three Months, I hope to make you easy on that Score In the mean Time, if you are in present urgent Necessity, pray let me know, that I may send you up a farther Supply before Christmas.

## PYLADE

P. S. I am very glad Mr. TRAPP's Poem gave you that Satisfaction which I both hoped and de fired it might do: But to let you see (my Dea CORINNA) how much your Judgment is biaffed by Friendship, when you give my Numbers Preference to the Poetry-Professor's, I have out of the Oxford-Verses sent you,

187

# A Translation of my Lord SALIS- BURY's Poem on the Death of Prince GEORGE.

To the QUEEN.

Royal Relief, whose lamenting Breast With too great Sorrows has been oft opprest;

Inur'd to dismal Woes, constrain'd to see The mournful Death of all your Family. A num'rous Race, and hopeful Off-spring gone; Your Consort dead! left comfortless alone.

What tender Words, what Verse can give Relief

To such unusual, such transcendent Grief! How shall I hope to sooth your throbbing Heart, And veil the Absence of your dearer Part!

Or

Or how delude with unobserv'd Delights Your pensive Days, and solitary Nights!

Should I presumptuous undertake to show The verdant Laurels on his youthful Brow,

And manly Courage of his tender Years:

Such, such he was (you would repeat in Tears)

When Hymen crown'd our Hopes, and join'd our Hands,

With Love sincere, in Sacred Marriage Bands.

Should I describe his vast Maritime Sway,

And how all Seas did their great Lord obey;

This would enhance your Grief: And O you'd cry,

O whither shall I now for Succour fly.

To whom securely can I now commit

That weighty Charge, the Conduct of my Fleet?

Who can alleviate my Domestic Cares!

Or who so well direct my Foreign Wars!

On the Death of P. GEORGE. 189

To whom shall I my secret Thoughts disclose! On whom with certain Confidence repose!

But why should you thus dolefully bemoan, Unconquer'd QUEEN, your Royal Consort gone! Why thus resign your self to mournful Cries! Why fall those Tears from your lamenting Eyes! Let vulgar Souls indulge their wretched Grief, Accusing changeless Fate without Relief. Your nobler Mind those Passions must subdue, Your Kingdoms, and your Senate calls on you. On you alone the Auftrian Hopes depend. Your conquering Armies, and your Fleets attend Your Dread Commands, impatient to possess The Trophies and Rewards of new Success. Let Britain's QUEEN her sad Complaints forbear, Suppress her Sighs, and for new Joys prepare. All former Woes in dark Oblivion cast, And each Youre Triumph Bill once

And each Years Triumph still exceed the left.

With trembling Terror strike your flying Foes, Who vainly your victorious Arms oppose. Secure of Conquest, deign to Rule alone, Your People's only Guardian, and your Own. Your lost Assistant you deplore in vain, You can Alone the weighty Charge sustain, Alone may Conquer, and alone may Reign.

This POEM is one of those which pleased me most in the Reading; and I can assure you, the Latin is very elegant: I wish I had been able to render the Beauty of it in English, or do it any tolerable Justice in the Version; but such as it is I question not but it will find a favourable Acceptance with you, for whose Sake it was undertaken, that I might communicate as far as I was able, that Pleasure which I conceived in the Reading of so polite a Composition.
