# A Pastoral ELEGY,

On HENRY Duke of NORFOLK.*

## PYLADES.

## O! whence CORINNA flow those pregnant Tears

That call for Mine, and raise my utmost Fears?

Why sit you drooping in this Shade alone,

And make the Woods lament to hear your Moan?

Tell me why thus excessively you grieve?

I fain would know, because I'd fain relieve.

## CORINNA.

My Grief is greater than I can express;

Exceeds all Bounds, admits of no Redress.

## PYLADES.

0 therefore tell! that I may bear a Part,

And bring some Comfort to rhythmbbing Heart,

Companions in Affliction ease the Smart.

- This PASTORAL was begun by Pylades in the Country, and furnished by Corinna in Town.

192

# A Pastoral on the Death

## CORINNA.

I would not, for that Cause, my Woes reveal Nor let my dearest Friend my Sorrow feel: And since I am afflicted, loft, undone, Compleatly wretched, let me be alone.

## PYLADES.

Unless I may with you participate In all your Passions, ev'ry Turn of Fate; Unless I'm suffer'd to condole with you, How can I think your promis'd Friendship true In vain on your Affection I depend, If unsincere; in vain you call me Friend; And I conjure you, if you think me so, No longer thus from me conceal your Woe.

## CORINNA.

Well! since with such a forcible Constraint: You prefs to know the Cause of my Complaint

Whet

## of the Duke of NORFOLK. 193

Wherein your self an equal Portion bear,

With melting Heart the difmal Tidings hear.

OPYLADES! our mutual Hopes are fled,

How shall I speak those Words? Great Pollio's dead.

## PYLADES.

Forbid it Heav'n! our gen'rous Patron gone; Great! Good! and Just! all Worth contain'd in One.

## CORINNA.

Alas, too true!

Last Night I heard the sad amazing News;

As I was going forth to fold my Ewes ;

Unhappy Ewes! which now may bleat in vain;

And with unpity'd Cries fill all the Plain,

Once my Delight, but now no more my Care;

Go wander Flocks, while I lie sobbing here.

VOL. II. O PYLADES.

194

A Pastoral on the Death

## PYLADES.

If Silence, or if Sighs cou'd ease our Pain,

Or our lost Pollio bring to Life again ;

With silent Tears we'd wash our Griefs away,

And in long Sighs! wear out each tedious Day:

But Lo! I hear the melancholy Sound

Thro' all the Plains goes heavily around,

Pollio's no more! Great Pollio is no more!

Rebounds from ev'ry Rock, and distant Shore:

The Woods, and Hills the dismal News disperse,

Lamenting Echoes the sad Words rehearse,

And ev'ry hollow Cave, with doleful Tone,

Redoubles Nature's universal Groan.

Then in pathetic Verse his Death proclaim,

And in sweet Numbers celebrate his Fame.

## CORINNA.

That he vouchsaf'd to hear my humble Lays, Shew'd his unbounded Goodness, not my Praise;

Nor

of the Duke of NORFOLK. 19

195

Nor were his condefcending Smiles e'er meant

For Commendation, but Encouragement:

Judiciously he'd calm the Muse's Heat,

And regulate her inharmonious Feet:

Direct her in a new and nobler Way,

Yet call her down, when she had soar'd astray:

Thus did he not my humble Song despise,

In Hopes I might to higher Notes arise:

Then weep sad Maid, thy fatal Loss deplore;

Thy great, thy god-like Censor is no more.

## PYLADES.

Behold, CORINNA, where our PAN appears,

See what a melancholy Look he wears,

Vast is his Sorrow, boundless as his Mind,

Put manly Vertue hath his Tears confin'd:

What broken Accents from his Lips do flow!

Musing he walks, and seems depress'd with Woe.

02

Then

196

A Pastoral on the Death

Then with a Sigh, he cries my Pollio's gone!

And hark, how all the Swains his Lofs bemoan:

These Thoughts, methinks, should give us some Relief,

PAN mourns his Fate, PAN shares our mutual Grief.

## CORINNA.

The Royal Sorrow is to Pollio due,

PAN knew his Heart, and PAN possess'd it too;

To our great Pastor, he was ever dear,

And next to PAN, we Pollio did revere;

For Pollio strove th' Arcadians still to please,

And, by his thoughtful Care, secur'd our Ease.

Justice he lov'd, and Peace was his Delight:

Mild was his Face, yet so divinely bright,

Wolves, Bears, and Tygers, fled his awful Sight.

Unhappy Plains! your dismal State deplore,

Unhappy Flocks! your Guardian is no more.

PYLADES

# of the Duke of NORFOLK.

197

## PYLADES.

Forfaken Nymphs, and Swains, your Lofs proclaim ;

Hills, Vales, and Groves, reverberate his Name.

## CORINNA.

See! see, lamenting Swain! with wond'ring Eyes;

What beauteous Streams of Light, adorn the Skies!

What wond'rous Harmony is this we hear?

See! Shepherd fee! our Pollio does appear :

Behold, what dazling Glories round him fhine;

The Mortal Part cast off, he now is all Divine.

Smiling he fits, crown'd with immortal Bays,

And with a gracious Nod accepts our duteous Lays.

03

2

PYLADES.

# A Pastoral.

## PYLADES.

Then let our Sorrows vanish all away,

Like Clouds dispers'd before approaching Day

Let's cease untimely, and in vain to mourn;

For Pollio gone, who never must return:

Benign he fits in his exalted State,

Fix'd in the Skies above the Pow'r of Fate:

Where, like a glorious Star, he shall remain,

To shed auspicious Influence on our Plain:

And while he fits Above, secure of Fame,

Let us Below, perpetuate his Name.

# LETTERS

F R O M

Mr. NORRIS TO

STUDIES.

## LETTER I.

Bemerton, April 25.

MADAM,

YOU have here my particular Thanks for your very extraordinary and obliging Present, which entertained me with great Pleasure and Wonder, and in which I can find nothing to tensure but the Meanness of the Subject, though to confess the Truth that is my Fault more than

200 LETTERS from Mr. NORRIS

than yours.* You have, I perceive, a very pregnant Genius for Poetry, and, I doubt not, for greater Things, and if you will be advised by One, who has had some Conversation with the Nufes, you should not employ very much of your Time in their Company, which in you would be better bestowed upon more serious and useful Studies.

God has given you a great Force and Strength of Spirit, and it is Pity, but that its Motion should be as well determined. I pray him to direct it upon the best Things, and in the best Way, remaining with the greatest Respect,

Your very humble and obliged Servant,

J. NORRIS.

## LETTER II.

GOOD MADAM,

I pray forgive my Rudeness in deferring so long my Answer to your obliging Letter of which some accidental Interruptions, together with my late Indispositions of Health, have been the Occasion. I should very gladly comply with your Request, in giving you such poor Assistance as I am able, towards the Direction of your Studies, but cannot so well do it at this Distance, and

* Corinna had sent him a Copy of Veres upon his Writings, which is hereunto subjoined.

to CORINNA.

upon that little Knowledge I have of you, unless you will be pleased to let me know what your Education has hitherto been in the Way of Learning, what Progress you have made in it, what Languages you understand besides your own, that so I may know what Books you are capable of reading, and withal what your Design is, and what sort of Books they are that you would read, whether such as serve only to the Promotion of Christian Life and Practice, or such as tend more immediately to the Illumination of your Mind, and the Improvement of your Reason and Understanding in the Way of Science and Speculation. If you please, Madam, to give me some few necessary Hints concerning these Things, that I may not be altogether in the dark in what I have to do, you shall receive my Instruction and Advice. In the mean time, you have the Prayers and best Wishes of

Bemerton,

June 12.

## Your Humble Servant,

LET-

# 202 LETTERS from Mr. NORRIS

LETTER III.

MADAM2

SINCE we are rational Creatures, whose greatest Happiness consists in the perfect Contemplation of Truth and Love of Good, I think it concerns us most to apply our Thoughts to those Things that tend to the Improvement of our Reason, and to the Regulation of our Manners. The former as our best Learning, and the latter as our best Wisdom. As for what the World calls Learning, which consists rather in the Furniture of the Memory, and Force of the Imagination (such as Knowledge of Matters of Fact, History, Languages, great Reading, &c.) than in any clear intellectual Sight of Things, though I do not exclude, yet I would not much commend it, as not understanding the great Value of it, or how it contributes to the Accomplishment of a reasonable Soul, whose greatest Learning must needs lie in the most perfect Habit of Thinking, and whose best Study must consequently be that, which best serves to give her that Perfection. In Order to which the first Thing you should regularly do, is to apply yourself to Geometry (in which I am glad to hear you have had some little Initiation) as being the Foundation of the rest, and that which will qualify and prepare your Mind for the Contemplation of any Truth, and for the profitable reading of any Books. Among which the first would commend to you, is L'Art de Penser*

* The Art of Thinking. This Book is translated into English by Mr. Ozell.

to CORINNA.

203

both for the Value of the Book, and the Subject it treats of, which is Logic. The next that I would have you read, shall I say, or study, as that which lays the Foundation of all Science, and will best conduct and methodize your Thoughts, is Malebranche's Recherche de la Verite.* This, I say, I would commend next, but that in Order to your reading him with Pleasure or Advantage, it is absolutely necessary you should understand the Cartesian Philosophy, whereof Malebranche is a Superstructure and Improvement. Descartes therefore you must next undertake (after some Competency in the Principles of common Geometry) beginning at his Metaphysical Mediations, thence proceeding to his Principles, and after that as you please in the rest of his Works. When you understand Descartes thoroughly, read One or Two of the best Cartesians, such as Robault and Regis†, and by that time, and truly not well sooner, you will be fit to undertake Malebranche, whom when you have made your own, you will be able to direct your self in all your farther Progress. Only there are a few Books, which I would put into your Hands, some for Science, some for Entertainment, and some for Practice. I shall only mention them, leaving you to range them under their distinct respective Heads as you come to read them. A late French Treatise of the Knowledge of One's self, in four Volumes, a most

* Malebranche's Search after Truth. Which is translated by Mr. Taylor.

Jacobi Rohaulti Physica & Regii Philosophia. The former of these is likewise translated by Dr. John Clarke.

204 LETTERS from Mr. NORRIS

most excellent Book. Poiret's Cogitationes Rationales, and his L'Oeconomie Divine, a great and noble System. The Port Royal's Morality of the New Testament. The Moral Essays of Monsieur Nicole. Dr. More's Ethics, his Immortality of the Soul, his Mystery of Godliness, and his Philosophical Poems. Burnet's Theory. Dr. Nichols's Conference with a Theist. Wilkins of Natural Religion. Dr. Allefree's Sermons, not so much for their Exactness, as for the Natural Flame of Wit, and Spirit of Devotion that shines in them. Dr Scot's Christian Life. The Country Parson's Advice to his Parifissioners*, a plain, but very good Book, written with great Judgment and Confederation. Mr. Locke of Understanding, is a Book you should read, and may with great Advantage; but I would not have you read yet, and when you do, it must be with Caution and Circumspection. I might be more large, but I consider your Capacity, and am loft to charge you with too much at once. Thel I think, may serve for the present, and for some of them, there will be a Necessity of a Language or two, Latin is more difficult, and French will now answer all, which therefore I would have you learn out of Hand. It is the most commanding, and therefore most useful Language present, and Malebranche alone will abundant reward all the Pains you shall take in it, which need not be great neither, if omitting the tedious

* By the Reverend Mr. Hervert, Author of the TEMPLE Sacred Poems, &c.

to CORINNA.

Way of learning the Grammar, you only read over twice or thrice the Particles, next the Verbs, and then proceed to go over the Dialogues, and after that any plain Book with a Translation, by which Way you may be Mistress of French so far, as to read a Book by the Help of a Dictionary, in a Month's time. I speak upon Experience, and would have you try. And so I pray God to bless and prosper your Studies, and to make you Wife here, and Happy hereafter.

## I am, MADAM,

Your sincere Friend,

and very humble Servant,

Corinna to Mr. Norris.

## Reverend SIR,

Have perused your Book, $^{*}$ with great Admiration; and I hope not without some Advantage; but the Argument being too sublime for my weak Capacity, I dare not presume to give my Opinion of what so much transcends my Understanding.

- His Theory of the Ideal World. Vol. I.

## 206 CORINNA to Mr. NORRIS.

standing. The Account which I hear from others is, that the Generality of Readers cannot prevail with themselves, to allow that Brutes are men Machines. Though at the same Time they grant you have set the Matter in a clearer Light, that any have done before, and confirmed it with Arguments, they are not able to answer, which shews, how almost impossible it is to overcome habitual Prejudices, and the strong Impressions of Sense. A learned Divine of our Acquaintance was pleased to object, that your THEORY supposed Precogitancy in God; which being an Imperfection was absolutely inconsistent with the Divine Nature, and resulted only from the Defects of Finite Understandings, and Limite Power. But God being pure Act, could not be reasonably, and philosophically said to premeditate or contrive before-hand, since every Thought of the Divine Nature was immediately Productive and to Think with the Almighty was to Act. It is also enquired by some, whether there may not be such a specific Difference in Thought, that System of Matter fitly disposed, and organized may be capable of what you call impure Thought or Imagination; as being only the Operation one Body on another, after a peculiar Manne And as there are pure intellectual Minds without Body above us, so whether there may not be sensible Bodies without intelligent immaterial Souls below us. Since they say, it is not less difficult to conceive, how pure Spirits can apprehend sensible Objects without being united to Bodies than how mere Matter duly formed, may per-

CORINNA to Mr. NORRIS.

207

ceive some Impression of Body, without the Intervention of an immaterial Soul. But granting a specific Identity of Thoughts, according to your Hypothesis, quite stops the Mouths of such Enquirers. I am sorry, I have not time to enlarge farther, as I could with, who am,

## LETTER V.

## MADAM $_{2}$

I need, and I beg your Pardon, for not answering your Letter (for which I thank you) so soon as I should, and indeed as I would, if I had not met with many Hindrances since I received it, which, as I remember, was no sooner than May Day, though it be dated the 30th of March. You cannot imagine what a Hurry my Life is, and what little Command I have of my Time, which instead of being free and entire for my own Use and the Service of my Friends, is lost and dispersed among a Company of Strangers, to whose importunate Visits I am continually exposed, an Unhappiness, which I can more easily lament than remedy, but which my Friends I hope will forgive. As to the Objection, which the learned Person you speak of,

208 LETTERS from Mr. Norris

is pleased to make against the Theory of the Ideal World, that it supposes Precogitancy in God; I grant it most readily, and it is what I contend for, as a necessary Requisite to his Operations ad extra, or doing any Thing without himself, which cannot be effected without some Pre-conception of it within, unless you will suppose God to act ignorantly, and without knowing what he does, or is about to do, which is impossible. But then I deny that All Precogitancy is an Imperfection. Indeed Precogitancy as it signifies a laborious and anxious Fore-thought or Contrivance, such as is accompanied with that intense Application of Mind, which we call Study or Premeditation, this indeed is an Imperfection, and such as results from the Limitedness of Understanding, and consequently cannot agree with the Perfection of an infinite Mind. But as for Precogitancy absolutely considered as it signifies only an Ideal View or Preconception of Things, this is so far from being an Imperfection, that without it God could neither be what he is, nor do what he has done. Nor is it any Objection against Precogitancy in God, in this Sense of the Word to say, that every Thought of the Divine Nature, is immediately Productive, and that to Think, with God, is to Act. I grant that Though in God is immediately Productive in Opposition to any Manual Operation, or Physical Motion but not in Opposition to a Fore-thought, which must as Necessity intervene, or mediate. And so again, to Think, with God, is the same as to Act. It is true, in this Sense, that God acts by Though

Though

to CORINNA.

209

Thought, and not by Motion. But it is not true, in this Sense, that all Thought in God is Action, and so (which, I suppose, is what this Objection means) that there is no thought in God antecedent to Action, or no Fore-thought. For that Thought whereby God acts, and which is indeed his Action, is Thought of Volition, and in this Sense to Think, with God, is to Act; but then there is another Thought in God which regulates and conducts, and in Order of Nature precedes this Action, and that is Thought of Perception or Idea, and in this Sense to Think, with God, is not the same as to Act. And this I take to be a clear and a full Answer to your first Objection, which I desire you to accept, with my Thanks and Service to the learned and worthy Author of it.* As to your second, I do not at present well understand it. It seems to me to be confused and wide. But I may consider farther of it, and perhaps may give you my Thoughts of it. I wish you well, and shall pray for your Health and Prosperity in the best Things, and remain with mine and my Wife's most affectionate Respects,

## Your most humble

and faithful Servant,

J. NORRIS.

# 210 LETTERS from Mr. NORRIS

LETTER VI.

M ADAM,

Bemerton, Sept. 3.

MY more than ordinary Engagements of late in a great and important Work that I have been writing*, and in reading over One of the subtileest, and perhaps One of the largest Books in the World, in Order to that Purpose might be pleaded as an Apology for my Neglect of you, but I chuse rather to rely upon your Goodness to excuse me; and certainly none but yours would do it: So very defective in Civility and Respect have I been towards you. And yet, I think, as uncourtly as I am, I should not have omitted that just Return of Thanks which I owed you for your kind Present, if my Wife by my Request, had not undertaken that Part of my Behalf: However, late Acknowledgements are better than none; and I desire you now to accept my Thanks for that Favour, as also for that ingenious Performance of yours, which you were pleased to impart to me: By which I perceive you have no Reason to complain of your Dullness in learning French, as I hope you will not do my Advice in putting you upon it. Without any Compliment, you have made a very eminent Progress in that most charming Language; though to excite your farther Diligence, there are a few Faults in your Paper, which by this time I sup pose you your self are sensible of, and are we

enoug

* His $\mathcal{T}$ theory of the Ideal World. Part II.

to CORINNA.

enough able to amend: Your main Defeat is, that you do not always rightly take the Thoughts of your Author; but when you do, you fail not to express it to the utmost Advantage, which, indeed, only shews that as yet you are not so much Mistress of his Language, as you are of your own, which yet may in a little time be hoped for from your Parts and Industry: Both which, I befeech God to favour and prosper with his Blessing, and to direct to the best Purposes, that you may be as eminently Good, as you are like to be Wife, and well accomplished. In the Prosecution of which excellent End, you may freely command any Service or Assistance that you think you need, and can receive from,

# 212 LETTERS from Mr. NORRIS

## LETTER VI.

M ADAM,

YOU will, I fear, imagine, ... whether I have been very Busy, or that I am very rude for not writing to you all this while; and indeed the main Reason that has kept me from writing to you, is because I have been writing for you being more than ordinarily engaged of late in The Second Part of my Theory, which is now drawing towards a Conclusion, and which, when finished, shall wait upon you for your Companion, if you do not find either from your own Thoughts, or more valuable Books, better Entertainment. In the mean time (and high time it is) you have my hearty though late Acknowledgement for the Packet* which you sent me the Contents of which were very surprising greatly admire the Performance of the Child (it be really his) and was also very well please with your Poetry, though still I would not have you, who are capable of greater Things, addi your self to it any farther, than as a Diverfio

* The Packet Mr. Norris mentions in this Letter, was a first Treatise of his own Writing, intitled, A Father's Advice to His Children. It was turned into French by Lord CHARLES, Marque of Worcester, at Eight Years of Age, and was given to Ceruley by the Marchioness his Mother, to send to Mr. Norris for his probation and Correction. It was afterwards printed under the following Title.—Confeit Spirituel, ou Avis D'un Pere a Jesse Traduit de l'Anglois en Français par un jeune Milord, âge de brulé à 12°. 1698.

to CORINNA.

## LETTER VIII.

## MADAM,

YOURS of January 23d. we received not till (as I remember) the last Day of February, which I mention that I may not appear guilty of more Slackness than indeed I am, in paying my Respects where they are so justly due; and for the Delay of which I ought, however, to ask your Pardon; though perhaps I should not have needed it so much as I do, if my vacant Time had not been of late fill'd up with some Engagements more than usual. I am sorry to hear that (among your other Afflictions) the State of your Health is no better; that such an infirm Body should fall to the Lot of so ingenious and improveable a Mind; and that you should be forced to condemn to Physic those Hours, which you might employ so much to your Advantage in Study and Contemplation. But, Madam, the Health of the Soul is more in our Power, and

The Marchionefs of Worcester.

and very humble Servant,

that

## 214 LETTERS from Mr. NORRIS

that the Indispositions of the Body call upon us the more to regard, as I doubt not but that you do: And if you can have that better Health, you may with a very thankful Patience submit to the Will of God, in the Want of the other. My Book is now about upon being published.* I have given Order to my Bookseller, Mr. Manship, in Cornhill, to present you with One; but if he should happen to forget or defer it, be pleased to give your self the Trouble to send to him for One, where it will be ready for you, though I am almost ashamed you should read it, it is so grievously abused in the Printing. But you must read it with the greater Care.

My Wife desires you to accept of her humble Service, and begs her Pardon for not writing to you all this while, hoping she shall not be much longer in your Debt.

I am, M AD AM,

Your sincere Friend,

welcome to me, if it had not brought with it an Account of your Illness, which, with the Misfortunes mentioned in my Wife's Letter, affected me with a very compassionate Concern, since they befall a Person to whom I heartily wish all Happiness and Prosperity. However, I hope, that the happiest Part of your Life is to come, or at least, that these Afflictions, by the Goodness of God and your Christian Prudence, will turn to your greater Advantage in the End. CORINNA being extreamly pressed by a near Relation (whom she was obliged to obey in all lawful Commands) to Communicate with a Dying Friend, who was a Diffenter, desired Mr. Norris's Advice on the Case, who sent her the following Solution of it.

P 4

A

216

LETTERS to CORINNA.

## A Case of Conscience resolved.

AS for the Case which you propose to me, I would by no Means advise you to comply with what is desired of you; nor do I see how you can safely do it, because, indeed, I think it a Schismatical Practice. For, in short, if the Communion of the Church of England be lawful (as you who can and ordinarily do communicate with her must be presumed to acknowledge) then they who separate from her are Schismatics, and then to communicate with them is to partake in their Schism.

J. NORRIS.

P. S. Madam, I would not have you think that I forget you, or abate in my Respects towards you, though it has so fallen out that I have not writ to you so long; for which I beg your Pardon, assuring you, that I continue an honourable and worthy Remembrance of you, wish you all Happiness, and remain

Your obliged Friend,

and very humble Servant,

J. NORRIS

## O D E.

To the Reverend Mr. JOHN NORRIS.

ROUSE up my Muse! enquire the Way,

And to Heav'n's Laureat, Homage pay;

But rev'rently approach his Shrine,

The Place is hallow'd, he divine;

And at his Feet, thy humblest Off'rings lay.

Presumptuous Muse! What canst thou bring

Worthy his Sight, or Ear?

The Theme, requires a nobler Strain;

Ah then desist! left you profane,

What most you would revere.

Yet something whispers thus, Sure he

That's rais'd so near the Deity,

Will like That too accept, Love, and Humility.

On

218

ODE to Mr. Norris.

On this Assurance tune thy Lyre, and sing Of that celestial Bard, who sung thy heav'nly King.

## II.

Propitious Fate did Albion bless!

And 'ere we wish'd, for what we now possess,

Or Norris did our Isle adorn,

Th' Almighty Word to Nature said,

Let a Man-child be born.

And in our Likeness made:

The active Hand-maid soon obey'd,

And while the jarring Atoms strove,

Cull'd out the finest Seeds with Care,

And in just Order did prepare

An Entity all Love.

Fearfully, and divinely wrought,

With all its Members to Perfection brought

The noble Embryo lay,

On

Only the better Part was wanting still, But that exceeded, mighty Nature's Skill.

When lo!

From Heaven's great Treas'ry came

A beatific Ray,

Excepting one, as bright a Flame

As e'er submitted to an earthly Frame.

Thus you, Sir, Heaven's peculiar Care!

Of better Make, and more refin'd

Than vulgar Mortals are;

Heroicly, exalt your Mind,

And visit, whilst you're here, those Realms you left behind.

Not Earth's grofs Atmosphere, retards your Flight,

Nor can the duller Organs intercept your Sight.

## III.

Proceed, our second Psalmist, bless us still

With more Effects of thy well-temper'd Quill;

Teach us in Doric Numbers, Things divine;

And perfect thy Design;

Thou, who alone canst make agree

Rough Wisdom, and smooth Harmony,

In charming Numbers do'ft express

A philosophic Energy,

And we, in that soft pleasing Dress

Instructive Ethics see.

Long had the sacred Muses captive been

To Man's imperious Sway,

Who rudely forc'd them from their Prince,

And made them Slaves to Lust, and Spieen,

They silent mourn for Man's Offence,

And with Regret obey.

My Dear CORINNA,

THIS has been a very long Week. O! it is an Age methinks since I saw you. I am impatient under the tedious Absence, but shall not be able to get Leisure till Friday in the Afternoon; and then, if possible, I intend to see you, and make my self amends for the Penance I have endured.

## LETTER XXXI.

## After his Return Home from London.

WITH a penfive Heart did I travel from London, and leave my Dear Corinna in such Anguish as I could not bear to hear and see; nor could I without the greatest Reluctance depart: But Necessity, and over-ruling Providence, must be obeyed. Ineffable Grief retards my Pen as much as it stopped my Voice, when last I saw you in those Agonies, which have not yet an End; and I am as unable to write now, as I was to speak then. Good God, what Torments do you Q 2

227

## I am

Your PYLADEs,

228

LETTERS from PYLADES

endure! what Anguish do I feel! but there Hope in the Divine Mercy, on which alone rely; and can add no more, than join with the Doctor in my most fervent Prayers, &c.

Your Disconsolate

PYLADES

P. S. The above-written Bill, on your old Friend, Mr. Bound $^{*}$ , is for Part of the Money due for the Things lost in my Portmanteau, and, I hope, will be paid you at the Time appointed. If you can make this hold out, till I come to Town in Easter-Term, I make no doubt, but I shall be able to afford you a farther Supply.

## LETTER XXXII.

## Country Chit-Chat.

I Was at Gloucester last Monday, to see my Cousin Payne, and going to visit Mr. Wheeler, he not being at Home, I was entertained for about half an Hour by his Wife, with a great deal of Mirth and Focularity; though the Subject seemed to speak otherwife, like the Comedy of The Funeral. For she, I know not how it came in, was lamenting the Lofs of the Young Dutchefs of Beaufort; commending her Excellent Quali-

* The Gloucester Carrier.

to CORINNA.

229

lies, her Sweet Disposition, and saying what Influence she had, by her winning Behaviour, gained over the Duke, who, she feared, would run into the common Vices of the Family, now this Retaint was taken away; and, indeed, there is so great Probability of it. From hence she ascended to her Relations, and told me, that in her younger Years, she was in a House where they had my Lord Dorset's (then Buckburst's) Company for several Days, how very engaging he was in his Conversation, enough to delude any Woman live; but yet, how much a Gentleman, how very well bred, and far from any thing that was indecent in Civil Company. Then she told me some of his Intrigues, among the rest, how he courted a Yorkshire Lady of about Three Thousand Pound Fortune; and, during the Courtship, was, by King Charles's Contrivance, married to the old Countess of Middlesex; notwithstanding which dishonourable Desertion, he afterwards Debauched this young Lady, and had a Child by her, whereupon she went over to France, and her Brother, who was then of the Temple, bowed Revenge against the Offender, but could never find an Opportunity to execute it.

Hence, she digressed into an Invective upon the infelicity of Greatness, and declared it her Opinion, that she thought a moderate Fortune and Condition of Life was much more eligible than the higher Ranks and Qualities of the World. And yet, quoth she, they are never willing to leave them; hereupon alledging the Story of old Q3 Baron

230 LETTERS from PYLEDES

Baron Leigh, of Ditchley in Oxfordshire, who lying upon his Death-bed, his Friends were comforting him with the Discourse of the Joys of Heaven, and the Happiness of another World. Agreplied the old Lord, there may be such Pleasures and Felicities as you talk of, for aught I know, but I cannot tell what Share I shall have in them; I am very well satisfied with my Lot here, I know what I have at Ditchley, and I could be contented to live at Ditchley still. In Requital of which, I told her what was reported of Bishop Nicholson, who, when his Friends in his Sickness cheared him up with the Hopes of seeing him Abroad by a certain Day named, replied, Alack, I am afraid I shall be in the Kingdom of Heaven before that Time. At which she laughed so heartily, that I was afraid she would have crackt her Apron-strings. Then she would tell me some News, it was said, pretty Miss Collier, the Maid of Honour, was fallen in Love with Doctor Moss, of Gray's-Inn, for his Preaching. Well, I warrant this Woman thinks to find something more than Man in this eloquent Tickle-Text. O la! how the poor Woman will be deceived! Ha, ha, ha, Well, ay, ay, well she hopes to go to Heaven in a String, ha, ha, ha but she will be as far from it as ever, &c. At this Rate, she would have run on till dark Night had overtaken me, if I had not abruptly took my Leave. On Tuesday our Kindred from Gloucester dined with us, to the Number of Ten, beside Servants. There was nothing remarkable in the Conversation; all common Talk, and so not a Word of it worth repeating.

231

## to CORINNA.

My Governor has given his Consent for Matt's going to London, and soon after our Affize, which begins next Saturday, we shall prepare for the Journey, which, for your Sake, is very desirable to

Your most faithfully affectionate,

PYLADES.

## LETTER XXXIII.

## Thanks Her for his Entertainment.

LAST Monday, according to my Expectation, I received both your dear Letters, for which I return you my hearty Thanks; especially since they were doubly welcome, by bringing me the good News of your Health, abating the Headach, which, indeed, is no very slight Distemper; but, if my Wishes can prevail, you may assure your self, my dearest CORINNA, that it shall not endure long. As for my Share of that Pain, I am so far from wishing to part with it, on the Account you mention, that I had much rather keep it as long as I live, than that you should take any of it from me; for that would be but removing the Disease to a more sensible Part; nor indeed am I ever like to be totally free from it, so long as you lie under its afflicting Tyranny.

Q4

232

LETTERS from PYLADES

I thank you for my Entertainment at Mrs. Creed's where I could have been well contented to have stayed with you longer, if that melancholy Accident had not frighted me away, which was truly dismal in all its Circumstances. But let us draw a Veil over that Misfortune, and open a bright Scene of Varieties at Mrs. --- (I have forgot her Name;) well, I am very glad you have found greater Satisfaction than you promised your self, and question not, but that your agreeable Tempers, at least in loving and admiring, as well as practising ingenious Arts, will mutually entertain and improve each other. I should be very glad if she would be so generously kind, as to direct your Hand a little, and communicate some of her Instructions to you; for I am sure a very little Information would be sufficient to make you perfect in Theory at least, the Beauties and particular Excellencies being attainable only by Practice. O what a contemptible Animal is the gentle Knight, if that Story be true! I can compare him to nothing more properly than that vile Fly, which having buzzed about sometime, with an impertinent Noise, is contented at last to seaf on an Excrement. Of all the Gallantry that ever I heard of, certainly this is the highest (shall I call it) or the lowest. Mrs. Egleburt might leave her Maid very well, but the Bulk of her Estate she left to two Relations of the same Name, and after their Decease to Mr. Masters, of Cirencester

Well

* At Ondie, in Newfoundland.

The unhappy Death of a young Lady. See Vol.I.Pag.261

to CORINNA.

Well, it is impossible that the stiff Stoic should be so metamorphosed, first into a courteous Squire, and then into a complaisant Boon-Companion. Is all the Pride, the Dignity of Nature, and Slap-dash Presence of Mind come to this at last; to get drunk for Company, and be Vicious rather than Singular. I always had an Aversion for Sufficiency, which Sir William Temple calls the worst Composition of the Pride and Ignorance of Mankind; and, I think, I have no Reason to change my Opinion for Sir Solomon's Sake. It is no small Satisfaction to me, that you happened to make such a Discovery, because it is just that Hypocrisy should be detected; besides, I am well pleased with the Resolution you have taken therepon; and so farewel, Sir Solomon. If the Apartment at Lincoln's Inn be not despicable (for I did not view it all over) certainly the Price is such as no Body can like. I am glad to hear Mr. Hanson is so well satisfied about our Formosan-acquaintance. Thus much in Answer to your two dear Letters, and now am I very sorry that can muster up nothing to requite them; but my Life is at present so erratic, that, I think, greatest Part of my Time passes away on Horseuck, and I am in this Respect, so much like a rolling-stone, that I cannot gather so much as a little mossy Country-News to divert You. For have this Week been at Ashton in Wiltshire, on Message from my Governor, and have attended John Guise about our Parish, in visiting the freeholders against the Election, in both which Expe-

233

234

## LETTERS from PYLADES

Expeditions, I found nothing worthy to be repeated. And yet you will not think me so full of Business neither, when I tell you what a Woodcock I was the other Day, to stand almost an Hour gazing at a Peacock, which was spreading his Tail and strutting as proud as an Emperor. For you must know, the GRACES* had a Peacock and Her lately sent them by a neighbouring Relation; and now the gay Bird begins to display his painted Tail, they are as fond of it, as he himself is of his fine Feathers: So that we are all, at least in Complaisance, obliged to admire the beautiful Present.

I have read over the Planetary Worlds, of Fontcelle, and the WORLD in the MOON, of Bishop Wilkins, but can never entertain such a strong Fancy, as to think with the Author of the Latter, that there may be an Aerial Passage discovered thither. Like the Theories of the Earth, of Burnet, Whiston, &c. I take them to be meer Conjectures, and find so many Things therein depend on Supposition, that I am not like to be much the wiser for all that can be said on that Subject However, the Amusement was agreeable enough and I return you my Thanks for it.

I think I have rode away my Head-ach pretty well, but I have still, by Fits, some little remain of it. I hope you are intirely free from that, and all other Illness; the Assurance whereof will be

of all Things the most conducive to my Health and of all News the most welcome to

P. S. This is our Anniversary Wake, called Hocktide, (whereof you have a wrong Account in HEYLIN's Cosmography) and Company coming in, I am forced to conclude in Hafte. Pray take care of me, and write when you can. Adieu, my Dear, and only Love.

## LETTER XXXIV.

## On the Death of the Peacock and Hen.

ACCORDING to my own Hopes, and my Dear CORINNA's Wishes, I arrived safe at Burdington last Night, where I found the small family in good Health; and the first News I was accosted with was, that the Peacock and Hen were both dead. I presently concluded they had famished them this hard Weather, during my Abence; but it seems the Case was otherwise. And we suppose, some Rafcal or other, endeavouring to steal the fine Feathers (for the best Part of them were cript off) had catched the Cock, who was pretty strong, and unwilling to be hand-

236 LETTERS from PYLADES

handled, though very tame to be fed, and in struggling, so bruised and hurt him, that it caused his Death. But what in my Opinion was the most remarkable, and upon which Account I am the more particular in the Narration, was the Fate and Fidelity of the Hen; for where the Cock was found dead in the Orchard, there lay the Hen also, mourning over her deceased Spouse, and when he was removed, she walked about the House, calling in a very melancholy Tone, as she was used to do, when she had at any time lost him, refused all Meat that was offered her, and two or three Days after,the mournful inconsolable Turtle died; not with Hunger, for she was in full Flesh, but with very Grief. Turtle, I call her, because what is related of that Bird, was here unquestionably verified in the strictest Sense: And how this can be accounted for, by Mr. Norris's imaginary Mechanism, I must confess, is past my Comprehension. My Sister PHEBE is much troubled for this Loss, and I am not without some Concern. I have not had Time to pick up any Country News yet; if the approaching Holidays should afford any Occurrences,worth your Notice, you may(as Opportunity serves) expect them from

Your most faithfully affectionate

Dec. 21.

PYLADES.

P. S.

to CORINNA.

P. S. My Service to your Mother: I long to hear of your Health, and wish you a happy Christmas. Pray take care of me my dearest only Love; Adieu.

## On a Coat of Arms cut in Paper by CORINNA.

ET such as seek a Name in Heraldry,

And from old Times deduce their Pedigree;

In painted 'Scutcheons fet their Honours forth,

Blazon their Arms, and magnify their Worth:

An easy Conquest this Cut-Paper gains

O'er all the Herald's glaring Pomp and Pains;

At least with Me, who hereby understand

As well the faithful Heart, as skilful Hand.

L. E T T E R XXXV.

Country-Courtship.

Am in great Pain and Fear for your Health my Dear CORINNA, which you say continue in such a State, as will not allow you to make any Brags, or give me any solid Consolation. You too great Confinement within Doors, is undoubtedly one Injury to it; and I wish you could find more Opportunity of diverting your self abroad among your agreeable Acquaintance. In Order to contribute what I can to your Entertainment (during my Absence, which is unavoidable, and is as great an Affliction to me, as it can possibly be to you) I will tell you a Comical Mess Courtship, between an old Man about Eighty, our Town, and Mrs. Hague, aged Seventy, when you may remember, I told you had her lately couched by a Mountebank, and was the by made quite Blind. The old Courtier, you must also know, is very Deaf. His Name is Rana and therefore for Brevity sake, we will only the Initial Letters of their Names. You must be informed, that this old blind Woman lives alone, without any Servant, or so much as a Cat, Rat, or Mouse, in the Cottage with him. The amorous Dialogue follows as she related this Week at our House; nor is it disowned the ancient Inamorato, whom I have since coursed upon the Adventure.

After much knocking at the Door, wh is always kept locked, and you must knc

to CORINNA.

239

the Lady of the Castle called out at her Window, to know who was there, but the Knight being Deaf, heard not her Voice, and therefore redoubled his Fury, till at length down she came, not a little disturbed at such rude Thumping at her Door, to enquire what was the Matter. No sooner was the Door opened, but she knew the Voice, the trembling Lover made his Entrance, and thus began his pathetic Address.

K. Mrs. Hague, how do you do? I called on purpose to see how you do.

H. Thank you, John. This is very kindly one of you. Pray fit down. (So down the it, and he close by her.)

R. I hope, Mistress, I may beg Leave to give you a Kifs.

H. A Kifs! alack a-day, why so? no, by no means, you must not kifs me : My Face is so ore, through the Distemper of my Eyes, that I cannot endure any Body should touch it.

R. Well, then let me kiss your Hand; and soying hold on it, he fell a smugling the poor Skin and Bones most unmercifully. At which, only, she was not a little offended, and was ford to scrub her Hand an Hour together, after he was gone, as she said, to make it clean, and fetch the ill Scent he left upon it. Mistress, quoth he, will you give me Leave to open my Mind to you? H. Why, what have you to say to me, John, that requires so much Ceremony? there is no need of all this between old Neighbours.

R. Why,

240 LETTERS from PYLADES

R. Why, then to speak plain; as I am a downright Man, you know I have a Mind to come a wooing to you.

H. A wooing to me! that is a pretty Fancy indeed. Sure you do not think I would marry at this Age, or if I did, that I would ever marry you. No, I do assure you, I will never marry any Man that has not Sixcore Pounds a Year.

R. Why, I must confess, my Estate is not near that; but I hope we may do very well together. You shall lie as I do,and you shall eat as well as I do, and you shall have one to wait on you, seeing you be blind. Pray let me intreat the Favour to ask one Question. Is it in your Power to dispose of your House and Land, or have you disposed of it already?

H. What, do you think the Devil is in me, to dispose of what I have afore I am dead?

R. Nay, if you do not do it afore, I am sure you cannot do it after; nor you cannot carry it along with you, and so it is well enough still.

H. Pray do not you trouble your self about that, for you are never like to be the better for it.

R. Pray, Mistress, take it into your Confidence, and I hope you will be in a better Mind against I come next. And so away went Don, in doleful Dumps, from his cruel Dulcinea. This is all I can remember of a pretty deal more of such kind of Stuff, which she repeated. And do not you think Midsummer Moon has some Influence on the Country too, as well as on the gentle

to CORINNA.

Knights of the Town. Poor gentle Knight $^{*}$ (if we may yet call him by that Name, among the Flames, and Raptures, and Extasies of his scorching Breast, this hot Season) I heartily commilereate his Condition, and therefore, in Pity to him, have sent him an old Recipe for Love, which I long ago met with among some approved Receipts, and which, if you have the same Regard for him, that I have, you will communicate to him, when he makes the next Complaint.

Love, like the Gout, invades the lazy Brain:

Bustiness prevents the Passion and the Pain.

## LETTER XXXVI.

## Thanks Her for Her Care of Him.

YOUR kind Concern for my Health deserves a particular Acknowledgment, in Aniwer to which, I thank God, I have at present no Cause to complain, since my Carcase is in a good State of Strength; but the Reason why I joined Leisure and Health together was, because, having Vol. II. R b.c

* Lorey Cowwell, Fig.

## 242 LETTERS from PYLADES

but little Time in the Day to write long Letters, and Nocturnal Lucubrations being always very pernicious to me, I could not gratify my Dear CORINNA in that Particular, without disobeying her Commands, in the necessary Care of my Health; which as I value more for her Sake than my own, and would most willingly at any Time expose it to the greatest Hazard to do her any Kindness; yet I thought myself obliged to have the same Regard to her Injunctions, as I could wish she would have to my Desires in that Respect; and I also judged it less Crime however, to be deficient to a bare Request, than disobe-dient to a positive Precept.

I thank you for that Quotation out of the Monthly-Mercury, which will be remarkable, let it prove true or false; but I am very incredulous of Prognosticks, till they are verified by the Event. Your Perambulations, I think, are generally attended with Impertinents; I only wish that Tom Glynn and Mrs. Hallon were joined together in lawful Matrimony, that they might be a just Punishment to each other. Alas! poor Once more; surely the most proper Place for him is in Moorfields, where perhaps clean Straw, and a dark Room, may prove an effectual Cure for his Caterwauling, though his own thread-bare Rules fail. Certainly his Love or his Wit are of a strange Complexion; for either the One must find a Remedy in the Scorn and Contempt of all the fair-healing Sex, or the Other, instead of inventing a Medicine for a melting Heart, has been adding Fuel to Fire, and made the Blaze past all

to CORINNA.

Relief, but the Martyrdom of its own unextinguished Flames.

In a late Conversation, which was chiefly concerning the Affairs in Germany, whereof Mr. Wheeler had lately an Account sent him by Dr. Slare, among other Things, he tells him of an old Prophet in Silefia, who foretold the Siege of Vienna, long before it happened, and was imprisoned for so doing; but the Event corresponding exactly with his Prediction, he was thereupon released, and had in great Esteem for his surprising Fore-knowledge of future Contingencies. He foretold the Incursion of the French into Bavaria, and has prefaced the Extirpation of Popery; that Rome shall within few Years be besieged by the Protestants, but not taken; for during the Siege, it shall be destroyed by Fire from Heaven. Let the Event determine the Truth of these Things, in the mean Time, while we are assured that God governs the World, we may rest satisfied that all the Actions of Men, however differing among themselves, shall conspire to the best and wisest End; and under this Confidence, we need not be very sollicitous about what shall happen hereafter, upon this Stage of perpetual Immutability.

P. S. Country News we have little; Paradise* begins to be very pleasant, tho' the Cherries are much blasted with the late Frofts ; but now my Bees are very Bufy, and require some Attendance; wherefore I hope you will, as oft as there is Occasion, excuse my Brevity.

*Palace's Garden R. 2.

Yours, &c. FYLADES.

## The DREAM.

Addressed to Lady PAKINGTON.

## 1.

KIND Sleep the fair Idea brought, To blefs my longing Sight;

But cruel Dream, as swift as Thought It vanish'd with the Night.

## II.

Tho' charming was each single Grace,

The Vifage mild and clear,

Yet was the lovely winning Face

To none but Me severe.

III.F

Corinna's DREAM.

III.

Forbear bright Nymph, I then complain'd,

To arm your murdering Eyes!

Nor torture with unjust Disdain,

A willing Sacrifice.

## IV.

Reserve these wonder-working Darts

For such as flight your Chain;

But spare those loyal Subjects Hearts,

## Lady Pakington's Answer.

Must always acknowledge kind Corinna to be the best natured and most generous Person in the World, since she can still have obliging Thoughts for One who so little deserves them, because she appears so ungrateful, and I have only to plead in my Excuse a great deal of Indisposition and Melancholy, which have been my constant Companions for a considerable time, and have so unfitted me for Conversation of this Kind, that I have almost forgot Writing, you will R 3 not

## 246 Lady PAKINGTON to CORINNA.

not wonder at this, when I tell you there has been two Buryings out of our Family within this two Months, and that since Sir John went to London, I have hardly stirred out, or seen any Company, except Trees and Woods may be called so; but I assure you, our Oaks have not the Quality of those in Dodona's Grove*, and therefore we shall have little Benefit of their Conversation. As unfortunately for me, the Time is past, when the gentle rural Deities appeared unto Mortals, and we have no Hopes now, of entertaining ourselves with the Dryades, the Hamadryades, or any of those pretty Wood-Nymphs so obliging heretofore; instead of them, a rougher God visits us often, Boreas, I mean, and by his boisterous Behaviour makes us believe what some have said, that we are either shoved nearer the Pole, than we were; or that our Climate is more Northerly than it is supposed to be. I have often wished the Muses had been so favourable to me, as you, which would make all Places pleasant; but ill-natured as they are, I invoke them in vain, and the only Way I have of finding them, is in your Verses, which I was glad of an Excuse not to restore when I was in London though at my Return I shall if you desire it. I cannot yet quit these Heathen Gods, without telling you, that I have a sort of Indignation against one of them for your Sake, and methinks Aeon lapius shows but little Regard to his Father, since he takes no more Care of the Health of a Per-

* This alludes to a Book, intitled, DODONA's Grove: Or, the Vocal Forest. By James Howel, Esq; 12mo. 1649.

fon, whom Apollo favours so much; but, I hope, he will be more good-natured for the Future, which will be very acceptable News, to

Your affured Friend,

and humble Servant,

Wristwood, May 30.

HESTER PAKINGTON.

## LETTER I.

## MADAM,

THE weak Defence* I have made for my Sex, is so far from deserving the Thanks you are pleased to give me, both in your obliging Letter †, and your admirable Verses ||, that it hardly merits the least Regard; as having nothing in it that can render it acceptable, besides the Kindness and Zeal with which it was written. I was troubled to see Them made the Jest of a very vain Pretender to Wit, and exposed by a scurrilous Pamphlet, rather than a Sermon*, to the malicious Cenures of invidious Detractors; of Men, who think they cannot be obedient Wives, without being Slaves, nor pay their Husbands that Respect

R 4 they

* The Ladies Defence. A Poem, written by her Ladyship. † See the former Volume, pag. 264. || See Corinna's Poems. 8vo. pag. 155. * The Bridewoman's Counselor. A Sermon preached at a Wedding.

248

## Lady CHUDLEIGH's Letters

they owe them, without sacrificing their Reason to their Humour. I heartily wish they had a better Champion, so good a Cause deserving to be managed by a more accurate Pen than mine: But, I hope, it will not be long before some ingenious Lady will undertake the Quarrel, and do us Justice. Give me leave to say, Madam, that, since you write so incomparably well both in Prose and Verse, none can manage it to greater Advantage than your self; it being a Province to which your extraordinary Accomplishments give you an undisputed Right. I am proud of the Honour you do me in desiring my Acquaintance, it is a Favour I cannot enough acknowledge, and which I could not expect from a Person to whom I had not the Happiness to be known. That Correspondence you have so generously begun, I hope you will be so kind to continue, which will be the greatest Obligation you can possibly lay on

Your most humble Servant,

Afton, Oct. 19.

MARY CHUDLEIGH.

# LETTER II.

M ADAM,

THE continuance of your Kindness to a Stranger, to one who has nothing to recommend her to your Esteem, nothing that can

juft

to CORINNA.

suitly intitle her to those Encomiums you are pleased to give her, raises my Wonder, and I look on it as the Result of an uncommon Genevrosity, of a more than ordinary Goodness of Temper: You take Delight in obliging, and bestow your Favours with such an endearing Sweetness, such an engaging Civility, that it is impossible to receive them without being sensibly touched by them; they make a deep Impression on the Mind, and with an agreeable Violence constrain the Persons on whom they are conferred, to become your Debtors, among which Number, I must own myself to be one: Yes, Madam, you have laid so many Obligations on me, that you have put out of my Power to make you a Requital: I have nothing to offer you in Return but Thanks, and a hearty and sincere Acknowledgment, which desire you to accept, as being the only Retribution I am yet capable of making you. The Correspondence you have so obligingly begun, shall (if you please to continue it) prove as lasting as our Lives, and I shall think myself very happy in the Conversation and Friendship of so ingenious a Person. I was troubled to hear of the melancholy Occasion of your Silence; the mindness with which you treat me, makes me interest myself in all your Concerns: I heartily wish Your's and your Grandmother's Health: It is a Pity, a Person who knows so well how to employ her Eyes, should have any Illness in them; but since you write me, that the Weakness was Casual, I hope by this Time you are holly freed from it; if you are, I shall beg the Fa-

Favour to hear from you as often as your Convenience will permit you to give your self the Trouble; for I am, with much Sincerity,

Your most humble Servant,

Ayblen, Dec. 8.

MARY CHUDLEIGH

## LETTER III.

Return my dear Corinna my humble Thank for both her obliging Letters, and for the Concern she is pleased to express for me. The Story you heard of our being over-turn'd by Waggon had not so much as the Shadow of Truth in it, we were not in the least Danger of any kind throughout the whole Journey. You desire a Description of Mr. Norris, which I will endeavour to give you. He is a little Man of a pale Complexion, but he has a great deal of Sweetness and good Humour in his Face, attended with an extraordinary Modesty, and a more than common Air of Humility; there seems to be a Reservedness in his Temper, but when you are acquainted with him, you will find it only the Result of Thoughtfulness: In a Word, he is a Man who Conversation is very agreeable, as well as instructive, and as I writ you, one who is very mud your Friend. I will leave you and Mr. Hemington agree about the designed Match with the Theoretical Nymph mentioned in Count Gabalis; then

to CORINNA.

is but one Thing that will render it difficult, and that is, — His Mistress before she is his Wife must learn a Catechism of his own composing. Now I would willingly know how we shall be able to teach it to an Aerial Lady? But this Point I leave you to settle.

I am sorry for your Grandmother's Illness, and I much fear your being forced to such a constant Attendance will very much injure your Health, at which, I assure you, I should be much concerned, give my Service to her, and to your Mother; I am, my dear Corinna, with the greatest Sincerity,

Your most humble Servant,

## LETTER IV.

Aflure you, my dear Corinna, your Letter was very welcome to me, because it brought with it the good News of your being past those Dangers which your late Illness threatened you with; I hope your present Complaints will vanish as soon as you have recovered a little more Strength, but I fear you will not be perfectly well till Spring, the warm Weather being necessary to compleat your Cure; but neither that, nor any other Thing, can be of use to you, if you are still negligent of your self, if you still keep irregular Hours; you ought to remember that there is a Regard due to your self, as well

251

MARISSA.

as

## 252 Lady CHUDLEIGH's Letters

as to your nearest Relations, and that you cannot without being guilty of a Crime, be careless of your own Life; it is a Gift bestowed on you by God, and as such ought to be valued; when he thinks fit to resume it, you must indeed part with it without a Murmur, but till that Time, it is certainly your Duty to preserve it with all imaginable Care; and I believe your Grandmother loves you too well to expect such Services from you as may endanger it. I am obliged to you for the Concern you express for my Health; but that Abstinence you fear may prejudice me, is absolutely necessary. The greatest Part of my Time is spent in my Closet; there I meet with nothing to disturb me, nothing to render me uneasy; I find my Books and my Thoughts to be the most agreeable Companions, and had I not betime accustomed myself to their Conversation, perhaps I should have been as unhappy as any of my Sex. But now, I thank God, I cannot only patiently but chearfully, bear a great many Things which others would call Afflictions; Life and Death are Things very indifferent to me; I am neither solicitous for the One, or afraid of the Other; I know myself too well to be flattered into a Belief of my being any way useful to the World my being out of it would prove no Loss to any of my Acquaintance; they would find every where much more valuable Persons, such as would be more capable of serving them, and whose Conversation would be much more entertaining and instructive than mine. I cannot help pleasing my self sometimes with the Hopes of shortly leaving

to CORINNA.

253

World I have very little Reason to be fond of; the Thoughts of lying down in a silent Grave, of taking Sanctuary in a quiet undisturbed Receis, have something very delightful in them; but when I consider, that it becomes me to conform my Will to that of my Maker, and to think that to be best for me which he judges to be so, I blame myself for giving Way to such Thoughts, and resolve to be sedate and easy under every Circumstance of Life, and to employ my leisure Hours in improving my Mind, in enlarging my Understanding, and in making some Progress in the useful Parts of Learning: Morality has been my chief Study since I saw you; the Essays I have written, are on Knowledge, Friendship, Life, Death, Humility, Grief, and on several other Subjects; you shall see them if I come to London this Winter*, but that I believe I shall not, because I find Sir George will not leave the Country, and it will be melancholy for me to be alone in Lodgings, it tired me the last Winter. Pardon me, my dear Corinna, for giving you this Trouble; the Kindness you express for me encourages me to it, and that will, I hope, incline you to excuse it. I am, with much Sincerity,

## Your most humble Servant,

Alyton, OH. S.

MARISSA.

# 254 Lady CHUDLEIGH's Letter

LETTER V.

M ADAM,

I am grieved to hear you enjoy so little Health it is a Blessing I heartily wish you, and all my other Friends, as being one of the greatest Pleasures of Life; while I had the Happiness of possessing it, I could cheerfully struggle with my Misfortunes, be pleasant in the midst of Storms and easy without any other Company but my Thoughts and Books; but now my Body links under the Pressure, and will not keep Pace with my Mind: Life is what I have for many Years had no Reason to be fond of, and a Grave has appeared to me the happiest and best Asylum; know it is my Duty to maintain my Post, to keep the Station God has assigned me; this Consideration made me leave Askton, and it is that which keeps me here; when I am fully recovered, I will return to my Home, to my solitary Recess, and there Think away the remaining Moments of my Life. I am very glad you have had the Advantage of good Conversation. You are happy Mrs. Bridgman's and her Daughter's desirable Company; when you see them, give them my humble Service and my Thanks for the obligin Concern they are pleased to express for me, which I shall ever look on as a Favour that can never be enough acknowledged. I never saw the Very writ on a Window in Bleinheim House, nor those written on the Dutchess's visiting the Relicks Richard the III.*

* See Miscellany Poems.

## to CORINNA.

I think myself infinitely obliged to Dr. Garth, so whom I desire you to do me the Favour to present the inclosed Letter. I am too weak to write much, it tires me; I can only assure you, that your Letters are very welcome, I read them with Pleasure; so much Wit and good Senfe is an agreeable Entertainment. I am, with great Reality,

Dear MADAM,

Your affectionate humble Servant,

Ecn, Oct. 13.

256 PYLADES to CORINNA.

LETTER XXXVII.

PYLADES to CORINNA. Defiring Her not to conceal any thing from Him.

YOU are very unkind, my dear Corinna not to say unjust in concealing your Miss fortunes from me, and denying me the Right I have to an equal Share in all your Afflictions. You know I have not treated you thus on the like Occasion, and by this unfriendly Reserve you increase my Fears and Suspicions, and really magnify the Evil, without making it ever the less to your self. I am fortified against all the Troubles of this Life, and, I hope, shall never meet with any more than what God will enable me to bear with Patience and Resignation; therefore pray be more friendly for the Future, and let me know the worst that has happened, whether you think it capable of Redress or not: It may at least alleviate the Calamity by dividing it; and the Strength of two united, may support what is too heavy a Burden for one. I shall long impatiently to hear from you again.

Your

Only PYLADE

C a

CORINNA to PYLADES.

257

To PYLADES at Aftrop Wells, whither he was gone for the Recovery of his Health.

Could not possibly compose myself to write by Tuesday's Post, but I hope, and am almost certain, this will reach Astrop before your Departure, your Letters coming to me the next Day after their Date.

I confess with you, that I do really believe your Notions of Decency are very different from mine. I blame not your Way of Thinking, neither do I see any Reason to change my own; Experience having taught me, that very many of my Sex lie under a bad Character for no other Cause than an unthinking and imprudent Conduct; and whenever I hear any Body say, They value not the World, it is sufficient they know themselves, I cannot help having very hard Thoughts of that Person, whether Man or Woman; nor have I ever found my self mistaken in the many Observations which I have made on this Occasion through the whole Course of my Life. But pass we this Chapter, since you found it reasonable to comply with me.

As for taking my Cousin Osborne into the House, the Case is unexceptionable: He was a near Relation by Blood, defrauded of his Estate, Pennyless, and Friendless: He came to England upon my Letter: He knew not whom to apply to; and after Eighteen Years Absence, he was as much a Stranger to the Town, as if he had ne-

VOL. II.

258

CORINNA to PYLADES.

ver seen it. In this Circumstance we received him into the House, and you well know the Money which was due to my Mother, from his Father and himself, was dearly wanted by us; and I could not imagine he would receive such a Benefit by our Means, and want the Honesty to pay so just a Debt. However, as soon as he commenced a Lover, I left the House, and sacrificed Interest to Decency; nor did I ever return till he had quitted it. I could do no more to express my Detestation of his monstrous Brutalities; and the Neighbours were all satisfied with my Conduct as being Eye-Witnesses of it.

As for altering my Condition, during my Mother's Illness, I am entirely of your Opinion, that a lingering Decay, which has held so long, is very different from a sudden and acute Distemper but give me Leave to add, that I have too well studied the Duty of a Wife, to expect I should have the Liberty of attending my Parent after having changed my Condition. It is true, you have generously promised to allow for her Support, I acknowledge your Goodness with Gratitude; but to go at a Distance from her, and leave her in the Hands of Strangers, at a Time when her Memory fails, and her Dissolution is so visibly approaching, would appear very odd, and not at all of a Piece with my past Conduct, neither could you have any Comfort in such a Companion, nor could I survive such a Separation this Juncture.

I have not forgot what Disquietudes I was under at the Bath, and at Esher (the only Times

CORINNA to PYLADES.

259

was ever absent from her) she was then in a tolerable State of Health, and for a long Time had treated me with such Severity, as if she had studied to make me renounce Duty. And yet even then, the many sleepless Nights, and anxious Days I endured for her, convinced me that no fond Mother could have a more yearning Tenderness for her only Child, than I had for my Parent. If it were thus with me at a Time when she had some Measure of Health and Strength, and was able to help herself, and go abroad, what would it be should I forsake her now, when her Strength and Faculties are all decayed, and the Doctor and Surgeon have declared, that it is impossible she can hold out long?

Consider, I beseech you, not only what I here write, but consider it like your self, a Friend, and a Christian; and consider also, how many Years I have waited on your Circumstances, without ever being uneasy at your Disappointments, and can you now refuse me a few Months in my turn, when I am wedged down by inevitable Necessity, and the Occasion is so just and laudable before God and Man.

As for the Term of Mourning, if it should be my Lot to survive, I am not so whimsical as you are pleased to imagine. I think, three Months Continuance, without altering my Condition, is a reasonable and decent Respect; but of that more hereafter, since it is not impossible but I may go forth, and then all Disputes are ended.

CORINNA.

CORINNA to PYLADES.

## A second Letter sent to Astrop.

YOU will excuse me for not answering Yours of the 19th, when I shall have acquainted you that I was five Nights the last Week without going into my Bed: My poor Mother continues very ill, and my Head, Hands, and Heart are all entirely employed. Nevertheless, I must not remain longer silent under this accumulated Load of Afflictions, left by not answering I con firm your mistaken Views.

Good Heaven! is it possible that the Groans of a languishing Parent should animate one with Chearfulness fit to attend Hymen? Is it possible that one of your good Senle should desire me to give you a Lodging, before you have a Right to demand it? What would the ill-natured Part of the World judge of me? Or what could even my Friends say, to excuse such a Misconduct? I am sensible you do not apprehend the irretrievable Censures that will attend it: You do not at present foresee the pernicious Consequences of it but on a serious Reflection, I am certain you will acquiesce with me.

We have hitherto observed a Conduct blant less before God and Man, and shall we, after many Years, by one false Step, render ourself Ridiculous for ever. No, PYLADES, I have to just a Regard for your Credit, as well as my owe to permit it. Pray do but think a little, I must furnish a Room for the Reception of a Low while my Parent lies expiring, I must receive h

CORINNA to PYLADES.

261

into the House before Marriage, &c. What could induce me to act so odd a Part, unless I were void of Natural Affection; unless I were in Necessity of a Cloak, and could not tarry; or perhaps that we had agreed to try how we should like one another for a Night, before we came together for Life. These and a Thousand other plausible Reflections would be the natural Consequence of such Proceedings. It is not enough to say, we know our own Innocence; we ought to avoid giving any Cause for Scandal, and our Blessed Lord has commanded us, to join the Wisdom of the Serpent to the Innocence of the Dove: Do but put the Case to your own Thoughts, as a disinterested Person, and tell me freely what Sentiments you and all the World must have of such a Daughter.

I shall say no more, for I am certain you are already convinced, and will no longer insist on what can never be granted. Excuse the Incoherence of my Stile, and pardon the abrupt Conclusion. I am taken so ill I can add no more, but my best Wishes for your Health and Happiness, &c.

CORINNA.

## LETTER XXXVIII.

From PYLADES after his return Home from Astrop, condoling the Continuance of her Illness, and wishing her Recovery.

Shurdington, March 19. 1715-16.

Am very glad the Bill came safe to Hand, and was duely accepted. I hope the Money is paid long before this Time, to your Satisfaction.

As to the surprising Light * which was seen in the Sky, on the 6th Instant, at Night, it was also visible in these Parts, but I had not the good Fortune to behold it, being at Home, reading by the Fire-side, and knew nothing of it till it was over: People were very much terrified hereabouts, but they say, such Things are common in the North; and there, the frequency of them, makes them to be little minded.

I can send you no News from our Affsize, but that Baron Price gave a very excellent and remarkable Charge to the Grand-Jury, setting forth the just Praises of King George, in a very long Harangue, and enlarging with equal Justice and Truth, upon the Wickedness, Villany, and Mifchief of the Rebellion, which, I hope, is now totally suppressed, and that the Fomenters of it will in due Time receive condign Punishment for their Guilt.

I am extreamly concerned for the Continuance of your Illness, but hope, as the Spring ad-

* It was called an Aurora Borealis.

vances,

263

# CORINNA to PYLADES.

vances, your Health may likewise keep Pace with it, and that this Summer may perfect your Recovery.

Yours, &c.

PYLADES.

## CORINNA's Answer.

I Return you Thanks for your Care about the Bill; it was duely paid, but I did not put you to the Charge of a Letter, because I expected one first from you at the Affizes.

My Course of Life affords me no News for your Entertainment, I see no Body, I know nothing, and Day and Night succeed each other, without much Variation, at least to me, who am sick of the World and its Inhabitants; and what is worse, quite sick of myself. An uneasy Mind in a decaying Carcase, is enough to render one unfit for human Society: Such am I, and as such, I admit of no Visitors.

Mrs. Bridgman's Sister, Mrs. Jessop, and Harry Cromwell, have done me the Favour of their Visits, but I have not seen one of them all. I have no longer any Relish for Conversation, nor any Reason to expose the dismal Habit I am fallen into. I rejoice in the Health of You and Yours, and shall most unseignedly take Pleasure in your happy Settlement, as being your obliged Friend, &c.

I wish you an happy Easter. My Mother, through the Divine Mercy, is recovered to a Miracle; she eats well, sleeps well, and feels no Pain ; but Strength is still wanting : She pre-

S4

264

## CORINNA to PYLEDES.

fents you her Service, and best Wishes. You desired the News of my Recovery, but you did not seem (if I took your Letter right) to wish to hear from me till then, and you see I was willing to oblige you; for I can no ways expect Health, while my internal Adversary remains within me; I have had a severe Shock from that Dolour in my Side, and find no Hopes of a Cure*. I return you many Thanks for your Care about that Money,and know not how to condole your Indisposition, till I am informed whether it be of the Mind or the Body; which ever it be, I most heartily wish you a Succession of Health and Happiness; but surely Domestic Occurrences might have been trusted to Paper, without Fear of displeasing the State; be that as you please. I am

Your obliged Servant,

Feb. 16. 1715-16.

CORINNA.

P. S. I hear no News, but of the Condemned Lords, who are to suffer To-morrow : They have petitioned the Parliament to intercede for them, and the Lords have Addressed the King for a Reprieve this Day ; but their Success is not yet published. As a Christian, I am exceedingly touched with Compassion for their Wives and Children;

* See The Case of Corinna's most furprising Indifposition. Appendix, No. 5.

## PYLADES to CORINNA.

and I think Mercy a Divine Attribute: But when I consider them as Traytors, who have made an open Rebellion, to dethrone the best of Kings, to subvert even the Religion they professed, and enflave all their Fellow Subjects, I no longer regret their Punishment, but acknowledge Justice to be an Attribute no less Divine than Mercy.

## LETTER XXXIX.

HOW often and how much are People abused and disquieted by groundless Surmises and talle Reports! Your Eye-Witness-Sir, was as much mistaken as any Hear-say-Evidence, or Oracle of the Bottle,* ever was; for most assuredly, I left London on the Tuesday Morning next after I saw you; I dined that Day at Teddington, and lay that Night at Slough; I reached the next Day to Faringdon, and thence got Home on Thursday, the first of this Instant. So now I have given you a particular Rout of my Journey, which must needs be much to your Edification, and cannot chuse but be very well worth your Reading, and my Writing. If I forgot to put any Date to my Letter, it is a Fault I am not usually guilty of, and therefore will the more easily obtain your Pardon. But since I have but one Home, my arrival there could be no uncertainty. I am extremely concerned for your renewed Illness, and hope it may not last long. I shall be very desirous to hear of your Recovery; but because I do not design

N. B. Shortly, after this Letter, Pylades came to London with a full Refolution to Marry; but Crinna put him off on Account of her Mother's Illness: upon which he went immediately Home gain, and the Two following Letters are the last he ever wrote.

## LETTER XL.

## My Dear CORINNA,

AS long as your Illness and Afflictions endure so long must my Concern and Trouble continually increase; and what adds the greatest Weight to these Calamities, is, that at present am unable to relieve them. Soon after Christma I hope, I may find some Means to assist you: the mean Time I must desire your Creditors Patience and Forbearance, with an Assurance punctual Payment, provided they will put you no Trouble or Charge.

Mr. Guife will keep his Christmas here, at my going to Gloucester will be uncertain; so th we must intermit Writing for a little while; as I hope the New-Year will begin more auspic ously, both to you and me. I return you m Than

## PYLADEs to CORINNA.

Thanks for your kind Wishes, and redouble them all to your self, and Mother; who am,

Yours, &c.

Dic. 10. 1716.

N. B. At the Bottom of this Letter, Corinna made this Remark viz. Thus flood my Affairs, at the Time of his Death; for I never saw him after this Letter.

## LETTER XLI.

Am very sensible of your great Loss, and heartily commiserate all your Calamities; but I cannot conceive how you can live without another Servant, considering your Mother's Condition. As to the Sickness at Oxford and Reading, have heard nothing of it; but our Country is as healthy as usual at this Time of the Year. Some old People have dropt off this Winter, but we have no Epidemical Diftemper among us. I suppose you have seen the Bishop of Bangor's Preservative, which is dispersed about the Country, and I hope will secure the better Part from the Poison of the Nonjurors; and if wholome rural Air and Exercise will but keep our Bodies in health, then we may enjoy sound Minds in sound Bodies†, which comprehends all the Happiness of this Life.

Mens sana in Corpore sano.

267

PYLADES.

## 268. PYLADES to CORINNA.

It has unfortunately fell out, more than once that upon your changing the appointed Day of writing, your Letters have come into my Father's Hand; and tho', if they were opened, there would no Treason appear in them; yet I could wish it did not happen so (as it did Yesterday, he being at Gloucester, and I not) because the Knowledge of your Hand in the Superscription may cause some Suspicion, no ways desirable. I must request you therefore, to be punctual to the Day, or else to omit Writing till farther Notice: At present, if you please, let there be a Cessation for some Time, unless any extraordinary Occasion requires it to be interrupted.

I am ever

Your PYLADES

# Christmas GAMBOL. A COMEDY.

Written by PYLADES, and Aeled at his Father's House by some Neighbouring Gentlemen and Himself.

L O N D O N :

Printed in the Year M. DCC, XXXII.

PROLOGUE,

Spoken by SPIGGOT.

T1S said that Christmas comes but once a Year,

And when it comes,it always brings Good Cheer;

I wish your Stomachs may be Good, since We

Shall here present you with Variety.

Old Christmas, now, invites ye All to feast,

And I bid welcome thus to every Guest.

Welcome to Christmas therefore now ye be,

And many a joyful Christmas may ye see.

Besides, that We may give ye more Content,

Christmas, in Masquerade, We represent.

We can't tell how 'twill please, but this We'll say,

You are all kindly welcome to our PLAY.

.
